# Sources: Nutrition: The Evidence

<!-- unread: Pollan, Greger, Attia, Spector, Rolfes, Gropper, Wardlaw, Big Fat Surprise, Good Calories, Ultra-Processed People, Fat Chance, Obesity Code -->

*Stage 1 research, 2026-09-24. Written before any lesson is drafted. For each source: what it is
good for, what it establishes, and exactly how much of it was read.*

**How this file was made, and how to read it.** Six research passes ran on 2026-09-24, one per part
below (A to F). In every part, **a passage in quotation marks was copied from text fetched on that
day with curl**: an abstract as Europe PMC's REST API returned it, a full text from PubMed Central,
or a guideline page or PDF converted to plain text by script, with no summarising model in the
path. Where a source was seen only through a search result or a model summary, the entry says so
("search-summary level only", "NOT READ") and nothing from it is quoted. Everything outside
quotation marks is the researcher's summary. **Every abstract a part cites is reproduced whole in
that part's appendix**, at the end of the part, because Mental Fitness and Sleep both found that a
research file quoting abstracts selectively passes its cuts on to the lesson (defect 26 in
`/draft-lesson`). A drafter quoting an abstract should read the whole of it in the appendix first.
Part F ran a script over its own quotations against the fetched texts; its result is recorded at
the end of Part F.

**Author names are given as the source page prints them.** Several sites blocked curl on the day
(dietaryguidelines.gov, hhs.gov, ods.od.nih.gov, nejm.org, ahajournals.org, jacc.org,
acpjournals.org, NCBI Bookshelf behind a reCAPTCHA, which was not bypassed). Where a part used an
Internet Archive snapshot instead, it says so and gives the snapshot date.

**The `unread:` line is the build's enforcement.** It names works a reader of this course is likely
to have met and that **were not opened for this course**: the popular books (Pollan's *In Defense of
Food*, Greger's *How Not to Die*, Attia's *Outlive*, Spector's books, Taubes's *Good Calories, Bad
Calories*, Teicholz's *The Big Fat Surprise*, van Tulleken's *Ultra-Processed People*, Lustig's *Fat
Chance*, Fung's *The Obesity Code*) and the three standard textbooks, of which only the publishers'
tables of contents were read (Whitney and Rolfes, *Understanding Nutrition*; Collene and Spees,
*Wardlaw's Contemporary Nutrition*; Gropper, Smith and Carr, *Advanced Nutrition and Human
Metabolism*). Some of those authors *were* read in the peer-reviewed literature: Taubes is a
co-author of Ludwig 2021 (Part B), Lustig's 2013 *Advances in Nutrition* review and his 2016 trial
were read at abstract level (Part D), Teicholz's Nutrition Coalition page was read (Part E), and van
Tulleken is a co-author of Dicken 2025 (Part B). **So the line names the books by title where the
author's name would also block a paper this file did read.** A lesson may name any of those books
as something that exists and must not cite one for a claim.

---

## The first thing Stage 1 found, and it decides the course

**In almost every public argument about food, the two sides agree on more than the argument
suggests, and the disagreement is about one of three narrower things: what replaces the food or
nutrient, where the low end of a range lies, or how uncertain evidence is turned into advice.**

- **Saturated fat** (Part C). Nearly everyone agrees it raises LDL cholesterol, and nearly everyone
  agrees industrial trans fat is harmful. The Cochrane review (Hooper 2020) finds 17% fewer combined
  cardiovascular events from cutting saturated fat, at moderate certainty, and little or no effect
  on deaths. The dissent (Ramsden's recovered Minnesota and Sydney data; Astrup 2020) reads the same
  trials as showing no mortality benefit. **Both WHO 2023 and the cohort work say the answer
  depends on what replaces the saturated fat**: polyunsaturated fat helps, refined carbohydrate
  does not.
- **Salt** (Part D). Both sides agree blood pressure falls when sodium falls (DASH-Sodium; He 2013)
  and that very high intakes are harmful. The dissenters (O'Donnell 2020, the PURE group) concede a
  population target of under 5 g sodium a day where intake is above it. **The fight is over going
  below about 2.3 to 3 g**, and the J-curve behind the dissent comes from spot-urine estimates that
  He 2019 showed can manufacture a J-curve from data where measured 24-hour sodium gives a straight
  line. Meanwhile SSaSS (Neal 2021), the hard-outcome trial the dissent said was missing, found
  fewer strokes and deaths with a salt substitute, which changes potassium as well as sodium.
- **Sugar** (Part D). Te Morenga 2013 (a review Part D says, from background knowledge rather than anything read, was commissioned for WHO's guideline) found sugar affects body weight
  through calories: swapped for other carbohydrate at equal calories, weight changed by 0.04 kg.
  WHO's stronger "under 5%" line rests on dental caries data, not on diabetes or heart disease.
- **Red and processed meat** (Part D). IARC 2015 and NutriRECS 2019 looked at largely the same
  evidence. One classified processed meat as a cause of cancer; the other advised adults to carry on
  eating what they eat. **The difference is not in the data but in how certainty and values are
  turned into advice**, which is the most transferable thing this course can teach.
- **Alcohol** (Part F). NASEM (December 2024) and the ICCPUD draft (January 2025), two US federal
  reviews within weeks of each other, reached different conclusions largely because of what each
  excluded (never-drinker comparison groups; Mendelian randomisation).
- **Weight loss diets** (Part B). Low-fat against low-carb makes little difference at a year when
  both emphasise food quality (DIETFITS; Johnston 2014), and adherence predicts loss better than
  diet type (Dansinger 2005).

**So this course's addition to the institute's claim sort is "instead of what?"** Memory added the
sample, Focus and Deep Work the instrument, Note-Taking the setting, Sleep the clock, Mental Fitness
what the comparison group got. Nutrition's version is sharper for food than "compared with what",
because nobody eats less of one thing without eating more of another: **a claim about a food or a
nutrient is a claim about a swap**, and saying what the swap was is how most of these disputes
narrow to their real size. (Stage 2 decides this; it is recorded here because every part of the
file points at it.)

**The second thing: nutrition's measuring instrument is weak, and both camps say so** (Part A).
Pooled across five US validation studies (Freedman 2014), energy intake reported on a food
frequency questionnaire correlates with true energy expenditure measured by doubly labelled water
at only 0.21, and under-reporting averaged 28%. The critics (Ioannidis, Archer) conclude the
observational literature is unreliable; the defenders (Satija, Willett, Hu; Subar) agree
self-reported energy should not be used as true intake and argue that ranking people, and
nutrients adjusted for energy, survive the error better. And Schwingshackl 2021 found that on
average cohort and trial evidence in nutrition agree, with the famous disagreements (beta-carotene)
coming from trials of supplements compared with cohorts of diets. **A course that teaches only the
critique or only the defence fails 3.1**, and a course that teaches both has something no popular
book gives a reader.

**The third thing: 2026 is an unusual year to write this course.** The *Dietary Guidelines for
Americans 2025-2030*, released on 7 January 2026 and signed by Robert F. Kennedy, Jr. and Brooke L.
Rollins, set aside the report of their own scientific advisory committee and commissioned a
separate "Scientific Foundation". They set a protein goal of 1.2 to 1.6 g per kg a day (the DRI RDA
is still 0.8), recommend full-fat dairy, name butter and beef tallow as cooking options, **keep the
10% saturated-fat ceiling**, remove the numeric alcohol limit, and tell people to limit "highly
processed" foods. The AHA, Harvard's Nutrition Source and others criticised parts of them; the
Nutrition Coalition and low-carbohydrate researchers welcomed them. WHO revised its healthy diet
fact sheet on 26 January 2026 and its meat Q&A on 23 September 2026. **This is politically charged
and recent, which makes it the place in the course where the neutrality audit bites hardest**: it
must be described in terms both its authors and its critics would recognise, with the points both
sides agree on stated first (Part E, 1.2), and it must be dated in the lesson so a later session can
revise it.

**The course is a sensitive domain under standards 3.4** (nutrition and medicine are both named),
and `sensitive_domain: true` is set. The specific risks: that a lesson reads as a diet
prescription; that a lesson reads as medical advice to someone with diabetes, kidney disease, an
eating disorder, a pregnancy or a drug that interacts with food (warfarin and vitamin K); that a
weight-loss lesson harms a reader with an eating disorder; and that the 2026 guidelines are
described with a side taken. Part F section 5 has the official wording for when to see a doctor or
a registered dietitian and where to go for eating disorders.

---

## What each part holds, and which lessons it serves

- **Part A, how nutrition science knows things, textbooks and syllabi.** The critique of
  nutritional epidemiology (Ioannidis 2018; Schoenfeld and Ioannidis 2013; Archer) and the defence
  (Satija 2015; Hu and Willett 2018; Subar 2015); FFQs, measurement error and doubly labelled water
  (OPEN, Freedman 2014); metabolic wards, Mendelian randomisation and GRADE; the WHI Dietary
  Modification trial, including the 19.6-year breast cancer mortality follow-up; the beta-carotene
  reversals (ATBC, CARET, PHS); Schwingshackl 2021 on when trials and cohorts agree; the three
  textbooks' tables of contents; three syllabi (Stanford, Wageningen, Berkeley); the DRI framework
  and the 2024 NASEM letter saying the AMDR should be retired.
- **Part B, energy balance and weight.** Hall 2019 (ultra-processed diet, inpatient) and Dicken
  2025; the carbohydrate-insulin model (Ludwig 2021) against the energy balance model (Hall 2022),
  and Hall 2021 and Ebbeling 2018 as the trials each side leans on; DIETFITS, Johnston 2014 and
  Dansinger 2005; Fothergill 2016 (The Biggest Loser); Pontzer 2021 and constrained energy
  expenditure; time-restricted eating (TREAT, Liu 2022); Look AHEAD and weight maintenance; GLP-1
  drugs briefly; WHO and NICE on obesity and BMI; why the 3,500 kcal rule is wrong (Hall 2011).
- **Part C, fat and the heart.** Hooper 2020 (Cochrane); WHO 2023; AHA 2017; the dissent (Astrup
  2020, Siri-Tarino 2010, Chowdhury 2014, Ramsden 2013 and 2016); Steen 2026 (Annals, risk-stratified)
  and Yamada 2025; LDL causality (EAS 2017) and its minority dissent; dietary cholesterol and eggs;
  trans fat, the settled case; Keys and the Seven Countries Study; the sugar industry documents
  (Kearns 2016) and the reply (Johns and Oppenheimer 2018); what replaces saturated fat (Jakobsen
  2009, Li 2015); the 2025-2030 DGA on fat.
- **Part D, salt, sugar, ultra-processed food and meat.** WHO sodium fact sheet (dated 11 May 2026)
  and the 2025 salt-substitute guideline; DASH-Sodium; He 2013; SSaSS; PURE and the dissent; the
  spot-urine critique (He 2019) and TOHP (Cook 2016); NASEM 2019 sodium DRI. WHO 2015 sugars
  guideline; Te Morenga 2013; Malik on sugary drinks; Lustig as proponent; WHO 2023 on non-sugar
  sweeteners and IARC/JECFA on aspartame. NOVA (Monteiro 2019); Lane 2024 umbrella review; the
  critiques (Gibney 2017; the Astrup and Monteiro debate; Braesco 2022 on classification). IARC on
  meat (Bouvard 2015, the Q&A revised 23 September 2026); NutriRECS (Johnston 2019) and its critics;
  WCRF/AICR; Cancer Research UK's absolute-risk figures, with a mismatch flagged.
- **Part E, patterns and guidelines.** DGA 2020-2025; DGA 2025-2030 and its Scientific Foundation,
  with named praise and criticism; the UK Eatwell Guide; Canada's Food Guide 2019; WHO healthy diet
  fact sheet (revised 26 January 2026); a comparison table. PREDIMED, its 2018 retraction and
  republication; Lyon Diet Heart; CORDIOPREV; DASH (Appel 1997); fibre (Reynolds 2019); protein
  (RDA, Morton 2018, PROT-AGE, kidney disease); vegetarian and vegan diets (Academy of Nutrition and
  Dietetics 2016; EPIC-Oxford, Tong 2019; B12); low-carbohydrate and ketogenic diets (Virta,
  Hallberg 2018; ADA consensus and 2026 Standards; carnivore survey); Blue Zones and the critique.
- **Part F, supplements, alcohol and safety.** USPSTF 2022; VITAL (vitamin D, omega-3); Cochrane
  on vitamin D and mortality; Endocrine Society 2024; ODS fact sheets (vitamin D, B12, iron); DSHEA
  and the FDA; adulteration (Tucker 2018) and liver injury. Folic acid (MRC 1991, USPSTF 2023), B12,
  infant and UK winter vitamin D, iron. Alcohol: Zhao 2023, GBD 2018 and 2022, Mendelian
  randomisation, the Surgeon General's January 2025 advisory, NASEM against ICCPUD, the DGA
  2025-2030, Canada 2023, the UK CMO. Pregnancy (fish advice, foods to avoid, caffeine). Eating
  disorders (NICE NG69, NHS, Beat; NEDA's helpline closed in 2023), when to see a professional,
  what "dietitian" and "nutritionist" mean legally, caffeine (EFSA 2015), and water (the "eight
  glasses" myth, NASEM 2004).

---

## What the field considers essential

The ideas an expert would say a course on nutrition evidence must cover, in the order they build.

1. **How the field knows what it knows.** Study designs (cohort, case-control, RCT, controlled
   feeding, Mendelian randomisation), what each can and cannot show, and why diet is harder to test
   than a drug: long latency, adherence, blinding, and the fact that every change is a swap (Part A;
   Wageningen puts study design in module 1, and this course copies that order).
2. **Measurement.** Self-reported intake and its error, and the biomarkers that check it (Part A,
   section 3).
3. **Energy balance and body weight**, including why the arithmetic of a fixed deficit is wrong
   (Hall 2011), why metabolic rate is more stable than folklore says (Pontzer 2021), and what the
   head-to-head diet trials found (Part B).
4. **The macronutrients**: protein needs and the RDA/DGA gap, carbohydrate quality and fibre, fat
   quality and the replacement question (Parts A 9, C, E 4 and 5).
5. **Dietary patterns rather than single nutrients**: Mediterranean, DASH, the national guides and
   where they agree (Part E).
6. **Micronutrients and supplements**: deficiency is real and supplements fix it; in replete adults
   supplements have mostly failed to prevent disease (Part F).
7. **The contested questions**, each with its best case on both sides (below).
8. **Reading a claim**, and **when a question stops being a self-help one** (Parts D, F 5).

---

## Common misconceptions

Collected from the "things that surprised me" sections of each part. Each is sourced in its part.

- **"Your metabolism slows down from your twenties."** Adjusted for body size, daily energy
  expenditure is stable from about 20 to 60 (Pontzer 2021, Part B 5).
- **"Cutting 3,500 kcal loses a pound."** The figure is roughly right for the energy in a pound of
  tissue; the error is assuming a fixed deficit, since expenditure falls as weight falls (Hall 2011,
  Part B 10).
- **"Low-carb beats low-fat for weight" (or the reverse).** At a year, with both emphasising food
  quality, the difference is small (DIETFITS, Johnston 2014, Part B 3).
- **"Eating in a window burns more fat."** Time-restricted eating did not beat the same calorie
  restriction without the window (Liu 2022, Part B 6).
- **"Science has proved saturated fat is harmless"** and **"cutting saturated fat is proven to save
  lives."** Neither: fewer cardiovascular events at moderate certainty, near-null on deaths (Part C).
- **"The US government now says saturated fat is fine."** The 2025-2030 DGA keeps the 10% ceiling
  (Part C 10, Part E 1.2).
- **"The 2015 guidelines said dietary cholesterol doesn't matter."** They dropped the 300 mg number
  and still said to eat "as little dietary cholesterol as possible" within a healthy pattern (Part C 6).
- **"Keys picked seven countries out of twenty-two."** That criticism concerns a 1953 graph, not the
  Seven Countries Study (Part C 8; flagged there to verify before teaching).
- **"The sugar industry is why we feared fat."** Overstated; Kearns 2016 calls its own evidence of
  influence on the review's conclusions "circumstantial" (Part C 8).
- **"Sugar makes you fat in a way other calories don't."** Te Morenga 2013 found the weight effect
  goes through calories (Part D 2).
- **"Supplements can't hurt."** Beta-carotene raised lung cancer in smokers (ATBC, CARET; USPSTF D
  grade), and supplements are not approved for safety or efficacy before sale in the US (Part F 1).
- **"An I statement means supplements don't work."** It means the evidence cannot settle the balance
  (USPSTF 2022, Part F 1.1).
- **"No safe level of alcohol" is what the Lancet study said.** The GBD 2018 abstract says the level
  that "minimises health loss is zero"; the phrase is WHO Europe's, and GBD 2022 found a J-shape for
  people 40 and over (Part F 3).
- **"Drink eight glasses of water a day."** NASEM's adequate intake includes water from food and
  "should not be interpreted as a specific requirement" (Part F 8).
- **"Nutritionist" means a qualified professional.** Not a protected title in the UK; "dietitian" is
  (HCPC). US rules vary by state (Part F 6).
- **"Blue Zones are proven."** The age-validation critique (Newman) is a preprint and the rebuttal is
  hosted by the Blue Zones company; the honest description is that the claim is disputed (Part E 8).
- **"Observational nutrition studies are worthless."** On average they agree with trials
  (Schwingshackl 2021); the famous reversals are mostly supplements against diets (Part A).

---

## Contested questions and the positions on each

Each is classified under standards 3.1. Every one is a **contested empirical question**, with value
questions (how to act on uncertain evidence) layered on top where marked.

1. **Is saturated fat harmful, and should intake be limited to 10% of energy?** Mainstream (WHO
   2023, AHA 2017, Hooper 2020): cutting it, especially replacing it with PUFA, reduces
   cardiovascular events. Dissent (Astrup 2020, Ramsden 2013 and 2016, the DGA 2025-2030 Scientific
   Foundation's "neutral stance"): the trial evidence for hard outcomes is weak and foods differ from
   their nutrient content. Settled by: a long, large, well-adhered trial with PUFA replacement and
   hard outcomes; food-specific trials; agreed rules on which old trials count (Part C, "What
   evidence would settle"). Consensus: strong that saturated fat raises LDL and that LDL causes
   atherosclerosis; genuinely contested on hard outcomes at usual intakes.
2. **How low should sodium go?** Mainstream (WHO, NASEM 2019's 2,300 mg chronic disease risk
   reduction intake, He and MacGregor): lower is better, down to about 2 g sodium. Dissent (PURE,
   O'Donnell 2020): moderate intake of about 2.3 to 4.6 g is not associated with harm and very low
   intake may be. Settled by: a hard-outcome trial of low against moderate sodium; 24-hour urine
   rather than spot samples. Consensus: strong on the blood pressure effect and on high intakes;
   contested below about 2.3 to 3 g.
3. **Is sugar uniquely harmful?** Proponent (Lustig): fructose is metabolised in a way that drives
   metabolic disease beyond its calories. Mainstream (WHO 2015 on Te Morenga 2013): the weight effect
   goes through calories, and sugary drinks are the clearest problem. Settled by: isocaloric trials
   with hard outcomes. WHO grades its <10% recommendation strong and its <5% conditional.
4. **Is "ultra-processed" a useful category?** Proponents (Monteiro, NOVA; Lane 2024; Hall 2019):
   yes, and the harm is not reducible to nutrients. Critics (Gibney 2017; Astrup in the 2022 debate;
   Braesco 2022): the category is inconsistently applied, mixes foods of very different quality, and
   adds little beyond nutrient content. Settled by: longer trials that separate processing from
   nutrients and energy density (Dicken 2025 is a step).
5. **Should adults eat less red and processed meat?** IARC, WCRF/AICR, Harvard: yes, and processed
   meat most. NutriRECS: the certainty is low and the absolute effects small, so a weak
   recommendation to continue current intake. This is where **a value question (how to weigh small,
   uncertain absolute risks against people's preferences) sits on top of an empirical one**, and a
   lesson must say which part is which.
6. **Is moderate drinking protective?** The J-curve (older cohorts; GBD 2022 for older ages) against
   the view that the apparent benefit is confounding (Zhao 2023, Mendelian randomisation, NASEM's
   never-drinker comparisons). Settled by: a randomised trial, which was attempted (MACH15) and
   stopped over industry funding. The Surgeon General's cancer advisory is on firmer ground than the
   heart question.
7. **Which diet is best for weight loss, and is the carbohydrate-insulin model right?** Ludwig and
   colleagues against Hall and colleagues, each in their own words (Part B 2). Head-to-head trials
   show small differences.
8. **The 2025-2030 DGA.** A dispute about evidence (protein, full-fat dairy, saturated fat), about
   process (the committee report set aside) and about values. Describe, date and do not adjudicate
   the process question; report the empirical parts as in 1 and 5.
9. **Blue Zones.** Contested empirical, at preprint level on the critical side.

## Best existing free resources (to link, not duplicate)

- WHO fact sheets: healthy diet (revised 26 January 2026), sodium reduction (11 May 2026), obesity.
- The NHS Eatwell Guide page and NHS pages on eating disorders, vitamins, pregnancy diet.
- Canada's Food Guide (2019), the clearest government guide with "limit highly processed foods".
- The Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2025-2030 at realfood.gov, and the 2020-2025 edition for
  comparison.
- NIH Office of Dietary Supplements fact sheets (consumer versions).
- Harvard T.H. Chan School's *The Nutrition Source* (a named party in several disputes; use it as a
  position, not as neutral ground).
- Cancer Research UK's pages on meat and cancer, with absolute risks (check the mismatch in Part D 4).
- Cochrane plain-language summaries for Hooper 2020 and the vitamin D reviews.
- Wageningen's edX course *Nutrition and Health* and Stanford's Coursera *Introduction to Food and
  Health* as free courses a reader could take next.
- Beat (UK) and ANAD (US) for eating disorders; NHS 111.

---

## Safety-critical guidance

Taken from Part F sections 4 and 5, with wording in those sections.

- **Eating disorders.** Any weight-loss content can harm a reader with an eating disorder. The
  course needs a standing callout, on the lessons that discuss weight and dieting at least, naming
  where to go (NHS, Beat in the UK; ANAD in the US, noting it is peer support; a doctor) and NICE
  NG69's warning signs in its own words. NEDA's helpline closed in 2023 and must not be listed.
- **When nutrition stops being self-help**: pregnancy and trying to conceive, children, diabetes
  (especially on insulin or other glucose-lowering drugs, which the ADA says must be reduced "to prevent hypoglycemia" on a low-carbohydrate diet; Part E 7),
  chronic kidney disease (protein and potassium; the salt-substitute guideline excludes it),
  medications that interact with food or supplements (warfarin and vitamin K, potassium-sparing
  diuretics and potassium salt substitutes), unintentional weight loss, and older adults at risk of
  malnutrition.
- **Supplements.** Beta-carotene in smokers; vitamin D and calcium and kidney stones; herbal and
  dietary supplement liver injury; adulterated products (Tucker 2018).
- **Pregnancy.** Folic acid before conception; the FDA/EPA fish advice; foods to avoid; caffeine
  limit of 200 mg a day (NHS; ACOG).
- **The education-not-advice note once per course** (standards 2.5), in lesson 1.

---

## Open concerns about scope

- **The course is about evidence, not a diet plan.** It must not hand a reader a meal plan or a
  calorie target. The project should be a reading and a decision, as in Sleep and Mental Fitness.
- **Micronutrient physiology is thin here by design.** The field's intro courses spend a third of
  their time on vitamins and minerals; this course covers them only where evidence and decisions
  turn on them (supplements, deficiency, pregnancy). Say so in lesson 1.
- **Obesity medicine, GLP-1 drugs and bariatric surgery** are medical and named only briefly.
- **Food insecurity, cost and culture** matter to what people can eat (Hall 2019's own limitation;
  the DGA's "budgetary considerations") and are acknowledged rather than researched.
- **Children, pregnancy and clinical nutrition** are out of scope beyond the safety pointers.
- **Recency.** The 2026 DGA, the WHO meat Q&A of 23 September 2026, Steen 2026 and Dicken 2025 are
  all within a year. Quarantine the dated claims so a later session can revise them without
  re-opening every lesson (the Using AI Effectively lesson 11 pattern).
- **Gaps each part names** for a later pass: the J Nutr 2026 critique of the DGA; the AHA 2026
  guidance statement; SACN 2019 on saturated fat; KDOQI protein figures; the EFSA opinions
  themselves; a Cochrane review of intermittent fasting; NICE's diet evidence review. Stage 2 should
  check whether any lesson depends on one of these before it is drafted.

# The six research parts

Each part below is the research pass as written on 2026-09-24, with its headings moved down one
level. Section numbers inside a part are that part's own ("Part C 10" means section 10 of Part C).
Each part ends with its own appendix of whole abstracts.


## Part A: How nutrition science knows things, plus textbooks and syllabi

Research notes for Stage 1 of "Nutrition: The Evidence". Compiled 2026-09-24.

### How to read these notes

- **"Abstract read via Europe PMC"** means I pulled the abstract as JSON from the Europe PMC REST API
  with curl and read it in full. Every such abstract is pasted whole, by script, in the appendix at
  the end (A1 to A27). Quotations from abstracts in the body below are copied from that same text.
- **"Full text read"** means I pulled the article's HTML or PDF with curl and read the named sections
  myself. Quotations from full texts were copied from the curl output, not from a summarising tool.
- **"Search-summary level only"** means I saw it only through a web search result summary or the
  WebFetch tool, which runs pages through a small model that can paraphrase. Nothing at that level is
  put inside quotation marks as the author's words.
- Everything not in quotation marks is my summary.
- Two official sources could not be read directly: the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements site
  (Cloudflare block) and NCBI Bookshelf (browser/reCAPTCHA check, which I did not try to get past).
  The National Academies' OpenBook pages for the 2002/2005 macronutrient DRI report are scanned page
  images, so no text could be extracted. Workarounds are described in section 9.

---

### Summary of what matters most (my synthesis)

1. **The critique (Ioannidis, Archer) and the defence (Satija/Willett/Hu, Subar) agree on more than
   either side's rhetoric suggests.** Both accept that self-reported energy intake is badly
   under-reported. Subar et al. 2015, written by the NCI dietary-assessment establishment, says in
   terms "do not use self-reported energy intake as a measure of true energy intake." Where they part
   is on what follows: Archer says memory-based data are "pseudoscientific and inadmissible"; Subar,
   Freedman and Satija say the error is characterised by biomarker studies, is smaller for
   energy-adjusted nutrients and for ranking people than for absolute amounts, and can be corrected
   for.
2. **Recovery biomarkers give hard numbers on the error.** Pooled across five US validation studies
   (Freedman 2014), FFQ-reported energy correlates with true (doubly labelled water) energy at only
   0.21; protein density (protein as a share of energy) does better at 0.41. Average under-reporting
   of energy was 28% on an FFQ and 15% on a single 24-hour recall. OPEN (Subar 2003) found FFQs
   under-reported energy by roughly a third, but "little underreporting of the percentage of energy
   from protein."
3. **The beta-carotene story is the cleanest teaching example of observational promise reversed by
   trials.** Three large RCTs (ATBC 1994, CARET 1996, Physicians' Health Study 1996) found no benefit,
   and in the two smoker-heavy trials, harm (lung cancer incidence up 18% in ATBC; RR 1.28 in CARET).
   Ioannidis 2018 notes that cohort meta-analyses still show serum beta-carotene with RR of death
   0.69 (0.59 to 0.80), i.e. biomarker measurement did not rescue the observational inference.
4. **But the reverse lesson is also in the record.** Schwingshackl et al. 2021 (BMJ), a
   meta-epidemiological study of 97 matched diet-disease pairs, found that on average RCT and cohort
   bodies of evidence agree closely (ratio of risk ratios 1.09, 95% CI 1.04 to 1.14), and that most
   disagreement comes from the two designs asking different questions, most often supplements in the
   trial versus nutrient status in the cohort. That is exactly the beta-carotene pattern, and it is
   the point Satija et al. make about RCTs and cohorts "answering very different questions."
5. **WHI Dietary Modification is the key "huge RCT" case, and both camps read it.** 48,835 women,
   8.1 years, nonsignificant results for breast cancer (HR 0.91, 0.83 to 1.01) and CVD (HR 0.98,
   0.92 to 1.05). Satija et al. argue it "failed to test its original hypothesis" because most women
   did not reach the 20% fat target. Its own investigators kept following it: at 19.6 years median,
   deaths from breast cancer were significantly lower in the intervention arm (HR 0.79, 0.64 to 0.97;
   Chlebowski 2020). The trial's reading is still contested.
6. **Recent changes a lesson must reflect:**
   - The **2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans** (released January 2026) set "Protein serving
     goals: 1.2–1.6 grams of protein per kilogram of body weight per day". The **DRI RDA for protein
     remains 0.80 g/kg/day**; the DGA is not a DRI revision. A 2026 J Nutr Perspective (Pasiakos et
     al.) calls the new protein guidance "insufficiently comprehensive." This gap between the DRI and
     the DGA is new and should be taught explicitly.
   - The **National Academies' 2024 letter report "Rethinking the AMDR"** concluded that "the approach
     used to derive the AMDRs is not consistent with current evidence-based standards" and
     recommended removing the AMDR from future macronutrient DRI reviews. The AMDR values (45 to 65%
     carbohydrate, 20 to 35% fat, 10 to 35% protein for adults) still stand until a new review, but a
     lesson should not present them as settled science.
   - **Kevin Hall**, whose NIH metabolic-ward trials are the model of the tightly controlled end of
     nutrition research, left NIH in April 2025 citing censorship of his ultra-processed food findings
     (search-summary level only: US News, Axios, STAT, CNN reports seen in search results, not read).
   - **PREDIMED** (the flagship Mediterranean diet RCT) was retracted and republished in 2018 after
     randomisation irregularities. Satija et al. 2015 cite the original version. Ioannidis 2018 says
     the republished study "should no longer be considered a randomized trial"; the republished paper
     itself reports intention-to-treat estimates adjusted with propensity scores and results "similar
     after the omission of 1588 participants" whose assignments departed from protocol. Two readings.

---

### 1. The critique of nutritional epidemiology

#### 1.1 Ioannidis JPA. The Challenge of Reforming Nutritional Epidemiologic Research. JAMA. 2018;320(10):969-970. doi:10.1001/jama.2018.11025. PMID 30422271.

- **What I read:** full text (2 pages) as a PDF of the JAMA online-first version ("JAMA Published
  online August 23, 2018"), hosted at
  statmodeling.stat.columbia.edu/wp-content/uploads/2018/08/jama_Ioannidis_2018_vp_180095.pdf
  (Andrew Gelman's blog site; the PDF carries JAMA's copyright footer and a "Downloaded From" stamp,
  so it is a reprint of the publisher's PDF, not a preprint). JAMA's own page is paywalled. Text
  extracted with pdftotext; words hyphenated across line breaks were rejoined.
- **Design:** Viewpoint (opinion). No abstract.
- **Argument (my summary):** Almost every food shows a statistically significant association with
  mortality in cohort meta-analyses; the implied effect sizes are implausible; nutrients are
  intercorrelated so one real cause produces many spurious associations; confounding cannot be fully
  handled; cohorts publish selectively in "slices"; single-nutrient findings have mostly failed in
  trials. He proposes open cohort data, reporting of all measured nutritional factors with
  standardised analyses, avoiding causal language and policy advocacy from observational results, and
  more large pragmatic trials of diet patterns.
- **Verbatim quotes (from the PDF):**
  - "the emerging picture of nutritional epidemiology is difficult to reconcile with good scientific
    principles. The field needs radical reform."
  - "In recent updated meta-analyses of prospective cohort studies, almost all foods revealed
    statistically significant associations with mortality risk."
  - "Assuming the meta-analyzed evidence from cohort studies represents life span–long causal
    associations, for a baseline life expectancy of 80 years, eating 12 hazelnuts daily (1 oz) would
    prolong life by 12 years (ie, 1 year per hazelnut)"
  - "These implausible estimates of benefits or risks associated with diet probably reflect almost
    exclusively the magnitude of the cumulative biases in this type of research, with extensive
    residual confounding and selective reporting."
  - "no currently available cohort includes sufficient information to address confounding in
    nutritional associations."
  - "Indeed, self-reported data have error, but there is no guarantee it is nondifferential." (The
    superscript reference numeral 6, citing Archer 2013, after "error," is omitted.)
  - On beta-carotene: "The relative risk of death for the highest vs lowest group of beta carotene
    levels in serum or plasma was 0.69 (95% CI, 0.59-0.80). Even when measurement error is mitigated
    with biochemical assays (as in this example), nutritional epidemiology remains intrinsically
    unreliable."
  - "some prolific cohorts (eg, European Prospective Investigation Into Cancer and Nutrition, Nurses’
    Health Study) have yielded more than 1000 articles each."
  - On trials: "Large pragmatic trials for more complex diet patterns also may yield largely negative
    results. Nevertheless, their outcomes may help inform nutritional guidelines with some pragmatic
    “intention-to-eat” data."
- *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 09's Stage 4:* the PREDIMED passage, from the same PDF (local copy,
  text extracted with pdftotext), read in full. Verbatim, with the reference numeral 9 after
  "republished" omitted: PREDIMED "was recently retracted and republished after it was realized that
  there were multiple subversions of randomization. Findings from the reanalysis showed results
  similar to those of the initially reported findings; however, the study should no longer be
  considered a randomized trial. Regardless, the trial showed no survival benefit." So he grants
  that the result held and disputes what kind of evidence it now is.
- **Other claims in the piece worth checking before a lesson uses them:** the hazelnut, coffee,
  mandarin, egg and bacon life-expectancy figures are his extrapolations from Schwingshackl 2017
  (AJCN) and Poole 2017 (BMJ), explicitly offered as a reductio, not as findings. Ioannidis's funding
  statement names the Laura and John Arnold Foundation (METRICS) and a gift from Sue and Bob
  O'Donnell; no conflicts reported.
- **Follow-up exchange:** letters in JAMA 2019;321(3):309-310 by Boeing H and Schwingshackl L
  ("Reforming Nutritional Epidemiologic Research", doi:10.1001/jama.2018.18652) and Ioannidis's reply
  (doi:10.1001/jama.2018.18676). **NOT READ**: metadata only via Europe PMC, no abstract.

#### 1.2 Schoenfeld JD, Ioannidis JP. Is everything we eat associated with cancer? A systematic cookbook review. Am J Clin Nutr. 2013;97(1):127-134. doi:10.3945/ajcn.112.047142. PMID 23193004.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A1).
- **Design:** systematic review of the literature on 50 ingredients drawn at random from recipes in
  a cookbook (the abstract does not name the cookbook).
- **Key numbers:** 40 of 50 ingredients (80%) had studies on cancer risk; of 264 single-study
  assessments, 191 (72%) concluded increased (103) or decreased (88) risk; "75% of the risk estimates
  had weak (0.05 > P ≥ 0.001) or no statistical (P > 0.05) significance." Median RRs in studies
  claiming increased risk 2.20 (IQR 1.60, 3.44) and decreased risk 0.52 (0.39, 0.66). Meta-analyses
  (n = 36) were more conservative, with RRs "on average null (median: 0.96; IQR: 0.85, 1.10)."
- **Conclusion quote:** "Many single studies highlight implausibly large effects, even though evidence
  is weak. Effect sizes shrink in meta-analyses."
- **Teaching value:** the cleanest single illustration of publication and reporting bias in the
  field. Note that it concerns what studies *claim*, not whether foods cause cancer.

#### 1.3 Archer E, Hand GA, Blair SN. Validity of U.S. nutritional surveillance: National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey caloric energy intake data, 1971-2010. PLoS One. 2013;8(10):e76632. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0076632. PMID 24130784. PMCID PMC3793920.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A2). A correction was published
  (doi:10.1371/annotation/c313df3a-52bd-4cbe-af14-6676480d1a43); I did not read what it corrects.
- **Design:** secondary analysis of NHANES I (1971-1974) to 2009-2010; 28,993 men and 34,369 women
  aged 20 to 74. Compared reported energy intake (rEI) with estimated basal metabolic rate and with
  estimated total energy expenditure (TEE, predicted from equations, not measured by doubly labelled
  water in these participants).
- **Key numbers:** rEI/BMR 1.31 men (95% CI 1.30-1.32), 1.19 women (1.18-1.20). Disparity
  (rEI minus TEE) -281 kcal/day men (-299, -264), -365 kcal/day women (-378, -351). In obese
  participants -716 (men) and -856 (women) kcal/day.
- **Quote:** "Across the 39-year history of the NHANES, EI data on the majority of respondents (67.3%
  of women and 58.7% of men) were not physiologically plausible."
- **Limitation I note (my summary, not stated in the abstract):** TEE was estimated, not measured,
  so "implausible" depends on the prediction equations. The finding is directionally consistent with
  the doubly labelled water studies in section 3.

#### 1.4 Archer E, Pavela G, Lavie CJ. The Inadmissibility of What We Eat in America and NHANES Dietary Data in Nutrition and Obesity Research and the Scientific Formulation of National Dietary Guidelines. Mayo Clin Proc. 2015;90(7):911-926. doi:10.1016/j.mayocp.2015.04.009. PMID 26071068. PMCID PMC4527547.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A4).
- **Design:** review/argument.
- **The strongest form of the critique, in its proponents' words:** "we present evidence that M-BMs
  are fundamentally and fatally flawed owing to well-established scientific facts and analytic
  truths." And: "the subjective (ie, not publicly accessible) mental phenomena (ie, memories) from
  which M-BM data are derived cannot be independently observed, quantified, or falsified; as such,
  these data are pseudoscientific and inadmissible in scientific research." Conclusion: "M-BM data
  cannot be used to inform national dietary guidelines".
- M-BM = memory-based dietary assessment methods (their coinage).

#### 1.5 Archer E, Lavie CJ, Hill JO. The Failure to Measure Dietary Intake Engendered a Fictional Discourse on Diet-Disease Relations. Front Nutr. 2018;5:105. doi:10.3389/fnut.2018.00105. PMID 30483510. PMCID PMC6243202.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A3).
- **Design:** "brief report" / critical analysis.
- **Quote:** "Controversies regarding the putative health effects of dietary sugar, salt, fat, and
  cholesterol are not driven by legitimate differences in scientific inference from valid evidence,
  but by a fictional discourse on diet-disease relations driven by decades of deeply flawed and
  demonstrably misleading epidemiologic research."
- **Quote:** "In lieu of measuring actual dietary intake, epidemiologists collected millions of
  unverified verbal and textual reports of memories of perceptions of dietary intake." (The source
  italicises "memories of perceptions of dietary intake".)
- **Note for writers:** this is the most absolute form of the critique. Ioannidis's is more measured:
  he calls for reform, reanalysis and trials, not for discarding the data.

---

### 2. The defence, from its own proponents

#### 2.1 Satija A, Yu E, Willett WC, Hu FB. Understanding nutritional epidemiology and its role in policy. Adv Nutr. 2015;6(1):5-18. doi:10.3945/an.114.007492. PMID 25593140. PMCID PMC4288279.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A5) and **full text** from PMC (HTML via curl),
  sections "Introduction", "Can We Reliably Measure Dietary Intakes...", "What Is the Role of
  Nutritional Epidemiology in Inferring Causality?", "Is the Drug Trial Paradigm Relevant...". Tables
  1 to 3 are images/structured tables I did not extract. Sections on meta-analyses and on policy were
  skimmed only.
- **Design:** review/position paper by the Harvard Chan nutrition department leadership. Note it
  predates Ioannidis 2018 and answers his earlier writing (a 2013 BMJ piece and others) and Archer
  2013.
- **Core claim (abstract):** "These criticisms, to a large degree, stem from a misunderstanding of the
  methodologic issues of the field and the inappropriate use of the drug trial paradigm in nutrition
  research."
- **Verbatim quotes from the full text:**
  - On RCTs: "While randomized trials with hard endpoints occupy the highest position in this
    hierarchy, they are usually not the most appropriate or feasible study design to answer
    nutritional epidemiologic questions regarding long-term effects of specific foods or nutrients
    (unless they can be packaged in a pill)."
  - On why diet trials differ from drug trials: "Unlike classic drug trials, RCTs of dietary
    interventions typically cannot be blinded". Also: "Dietary interventions to promote weight loss
    routinely have dropout rates of 30–40% even after just 1 y of follow-up".
  - On WHI: "over 8 y of follow-up in the Women’s Health Initiative (WHI), the most expensive human
    study ever conducted, most of the participants randomly assigned to the low-fat group were unable
    to achieve their fat reduction target of 20%". And: "The WHI, hence, failed to test its original
    hypothesis, and its null findings were largely uninformative with respect to the causal effect of
    a low-fat dietary intervention."
  - On RCT/cohort disagreement: "an equally, and sometimes more likely, possibility is that the RCT
    and observational studies are answering very different questions. For example, an observational
    study might answer the question “are some persons in a population at increased risk because of
    low intake of nutrient X?,” whereas an RCT might answer the question “does adding more nutrient X
    to the whole population reduce risk?”"
  - On supplement trials: "High-dose vitamin and antioxidant trials mimic drug trials that examine the
    effect of isolated compounds, but these findings have been largely negative".
  - On causal inference without trials: "Well-conducted prospective cohort studies thus can be used to
    infer causality with a high degree of certainty when randomized trials of hard endpoints are
    impractical."
  - On convergence as evidence (trans fat): "a meta-analysis of randomized trials of trans fat intake
    has shown harmful effects of higher intake on intermediate endpoints, such as increased LDL
    cholesterol and reduced HDL cholesterol (49). Based on observational studies, there is a
    significant and robust association between higher intake of trans fat and increased risk of
    ischemic heart disease (IHD) (49). Taken together, these studies forge a strong case for the
    harmful effects of trans fat consumption on heart disease."
  - Candid concession: "the critical assumption of “no unmeasured or residual confounding” that is
    needed to infer causality cannot be empirically verified in observational epidemiology".
  - On diet records and measurement: "most dietary assessment methods have a component of error". On
    biomarkers: "many foods and nutrients lack sensitive or specific biomarkers". **Both of these
    were cut from their sentences; see the correction below before quoting either.**
  - *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 02's Stage 4:* **correction to the bullet above, from the full text
    (PMC HTML via curl, section "Can We Reliably Measure Dietary Intakes...", read in full).** The
    first quotation is not a free-standing concession. The whole sentence and the one after it:
    "It is not surprising, then, that most dietary assessment methods have a component of error,
    which could be random day-to-day, diurnal, and seasonal variation in an individual’s diet over
    time, or because of systematic mechanisms, such as omission of foods when collecting data.
    Nonetheless, several techniques have been developed to ascertain dietary intake from free-living
    populations, and these methods have shown good validity with use of multiple criteria." The
    second quotation is about **the limitations of biomarkers**, not of self-report, and argues that
    biomarkers cannot replace asking: "The limitations of biomarkers, however, have prevented their
    wider use. In particular, many foods and nutrients lack sensitive or specific biomarkers, their
    assessment always includes error from multiple sources, they may not be indicators of individual
    long-term intake, and obtaining and testing for biomarkers is expensive and burdensome." Read
    together they are the defence's position (the error is real, the instruments are valid for
    their purposes, and biomarkers can't do the job alone), not a concession.
  - *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 02's Stage 4:* on WHI adherence, the sentence between the two WHI
    quotations below (section "Is the Drug Trial Paradigm Relevant...", read in full): "Moreover,
    during the trial, there were no differences between the low-fat and control groups in plasma
    concentrations of HDL cholesterol and triglycerides, which are known to change on low-fat diets
    (53)." This is their objective evidence that the diet was not eaten as designed; it is the
    reason the next sentence begins "The WHI, hence, failed to test".
  - Smoking analogy: "RCTs examining this issue have found no effect on mortality, probably because of
    recidivism and insufficient follow-up time".
- **Caveat for writers:** the paper cites PREDIMED's 2013 result ("7447 men and women") as a landmark
  trial. That report was retracted and republished in 2018 (see 5.4). The Mediterranean-diet point
  survives the republication, but a lesson should cite the 2018 version.

#### 2.2 Hu FB, Willett WC. Current and Future Landscape of Nutritional Epidemiologic Research. JAMA. 2018;320(20):2073-2074. doi:10.1001/jama.2018.16166. PMID 30383165.

- **What I read:** **search-summary level only.** JAMA page paywalled and curl-blocked. WebFetch
  returned what it said were the opening paragraphs; because WebFetch can paraphrase, I do not quote
  them. Gist (my summary of the WebFetch output, not verified): nutrition research moved beyond in
  vitro, animal and short-term feeding studies, which "do not directly connect diets with long-term
  health and disease in humans"; nutritional epidemiology has had substantial effects on diets
  globally. **Do not quote until someone reads the article.** This is the direct reply to Ioannidis
  2018 and a writer with access should read it: it is the best statement of the defence in the same
  venue and year.

#### 2.3 Subar AF, Freedman LS, Tooze JA, Kirkpatrick SI, Boushey C, Neuhouser ML, Thompson FE, Potischman N, Guenther PM, Tarasuk V, Reedy J, Krebs-Smith SM. Addressing Current Criticism Regarding the Value of Self-Report Dietary Data. J Nutr. 2015;145(12):2639-2645. doi:10.3945/jn.115.219634. PMID 26468491. PMCID PMC4656907.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A6).
- **Design:** commentary, written in response to Archer's papers, by NCI and allied dietary-assessment
  researchers.
- **Position (the moderate defence):** keeps self-report data but concedes the key point on energy.
  Their seven recommendations include: "(2) do not use self-reported energy intake as a measure of
  true energy intake; (3) do use self-reported energy intake for energy adjustment of other
  self-reported dietary constituents to improve risk estimation in studies of diet-health
  associations"; "(5) design studies and conduct analyses that allow adjustment for measurement
  error"; "(6) design new epidemiologic studies to collect dietary data from both short-term (recalls
  or food records) and long-term (food-frequency questionnaires) instruments".
- **Teaching value:** shows the defenders conceding what the critics are right about, which is the
  honest centre of this debate.

#### 2.4 What would settle it (my synthesis)

- More recovery-biomarker calibration (doubly labelled water, urinary nitrogen, potassium, sodium)
  in the big cohorts, and publication of calibrated versus uncalibrated estimates side by side.
- Pre-registered, all-exposures analyses of cohort data (Ioannidis's proposal), so readers see the
  whole distribution of associations rather than selected slices.
- Long, large trials of dietary patterns with measured adherence (biomarkers), where feasible, and
  Mendelian randomisation where a good genetic instrument exists.
- Meta-epidemiology like Schwingshackl 2021, which tests empirically how often cohorts and trials
  agree once the question is matched.

---

### 3. Food frequency questionnaires, measurement error and doubly labelled water

#### 3.1 Subar AF, Kipnis V, Troiano RP, Midthune D, Schoeller DA, Bingham S, Sharbaugh CO, Trabulsi J, Runswick S, Ballard-Barbash R, Sunshine J, Schatzkin A. Using intake biomarkers to evaluate the extent of dietary misreporting in a large sample of adults: the OPEN study. Am J Epidemiol. 2003;158(1):1-13. doi:10.1093/aje/kwg092. PMID 12835280.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A7).
- **Design:** biomarker validation study, 484 men and women aged 40 to 69, Montgomery County,
  Maryland, September 1999 to March 2000. Compared FFQ and 24-hour recall (24HR) against doubly
  labelled water (energy) and urinary nitrogen (protein).
- **Key numbers (abstract):** men under-reported energy by 12-14% on 24HRs and 31-36% on FFQs; women
  by 16-20% (24HR) and 34-38% (FFQ). Protein under-reported by 11-12% (24HR) and 30-34% (FFQ) in men,
  11-15% and 27-32% in women. Under-reporters of both energy and protein: 9% of men and 7% of women on
  24HR, 35% of men and 23% of women on FFQ. "There was little underreporting of the percentage of
  energy from protein for men or women."
- **Why it matters:** doubly labelled water measures total energy expenditure, which equals intake in
  weight-stable people, without relying on memory. OPEN is the standard citation for how large the
  FFQ's error is and for why energy-adjusted (density) measures behave better than absolute ones.

#### 3.2 Freedman LS, Commins JM, Moler JE, Arab L, Baer DJ, Kipnis V, Midthune D, Moshfegh AJ, Neuhouser ML, Prentice RL, Schatzkin A, Spiegelman D, Subar AF, Tinker LF, Willett W. Pooled results from 5 validation studies of dietary self-report instruments using recovery biomarkers for energy and protein intake. Am J Epidemiol. 2014;180(2):172-188. doi:10.1093/aje/kwu116. PMID 24918187. PMCID PMC4082341.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (appendix A8).
- **Design:** pooled analysis of 5 US biomarker validation studies, 1999 to 2009. Note that Willett is
  a co-author, so this is evidence the defenders themselves produced.
- **Key numbers:** FFQ correlations with truth: energy 0.21, protein 0.29, protein density 0.41.
  Single 24-hour recall: 0.26, 0.40, 0.36; three recalls averaged: 0.31, 0.49, 0.46. "The average rate
  of under-reporting of energy intake was 28% with a FFQ and 15% with a single 24-hour recall".
  "body mass index strongly predicts under-reporting of energy and protein intakes."
- **Teaching value:** under-reporting that rises with BMI is *differential* error, which is the
  specific worry Ioannidis raises ("no guarantee it is nondifferential"). This is the empirical link
  between the critique and the data.

---

### 4. Other designs: metabolic wards, Mendelian randomisation, GRADE

#### 4.1 Controlled feeding / metabolic ward studies (Kevin Hall, NIH)

Three abstracts read via Europe PMC (appendix A9 to A11), used here as examples of the design:

- **Hall KD et al. Ultra-Processed Diets Cause Excess Calorie Intake and Weight Gain: An Inpatient
  Randomized Controlled Trial of Ad Libitum Food Intake. Cell Metab. 2019;30(1):67-77.e3.
  doi:10.1016/j.cmet.2019.05.008. PMID 31105044.** 20 weight-stable adults, NIH Clinical Center,
  crossover, 2 weeks each diet, meals matched for presented calories, energy density, macronutrients,
  sugar, sodium and fiber, eaten ad libitum. Energy intake 508 ± 106 kcal/day higher on the
  ultra-processed diet (p = 0.0001); +0.9 ± 0.3 kg on ultra-processed vs -0.9 ± 0.3 kg on unprocessed.
  An erratum was published (Cell Metab 2020;32(4):690, doi:10.1016/j.cmet.2020.08.014); I did not
  read what it corrects.
- **Hall KD et al. Energy expenditure and body composition changes after an isocaloric ketogenic diet
  in overweight and obese men. Am J Clin Nutr. 2016;104(2):324-333. doi:10.3945/ajcn.116.133561.** 17
  men on metabolic wards, 4 weeks baseline high-carbohydrate diet then 4 weeks isocaloric ketogenic
  diet (not randomised; single-arm sequential design). "The isocaloric KD was not accompanied by
  increased body fat loss but was associated with relatively small increases in EE that were near the
  limits of detection".
- **Hall KD et al. Effect of a plant-based, low-fat diet versus an animal-based, ketogenic diet on ad
  libitum energy intake. Nat Med. 2021;27(2):344-353. doi:10.1038/s41591-020-01209-1.** 20 adults,
  inpatient crossover, 2 weeks each. Low-fat diet led to 689 ± 73 kcal/day less intake over 2 weeks.
  "Therefore, the predictions of the carbohydrate-insulin model were inconsistent with our
  observations."
- **What the design buys and costs (my summary):** near-total control of what is eaten and precise
  measurement (chambers, doubly labelled water, DXA); but tiny samples, days to weeks of duration,
  artificial settings, and outcomes that are intake, weight or energy expenditure rather than disease.
  It is the mirror image of the cohort study: strong on internal validity, weak on duration and
  hard endpoints.
- **Recent change (search-summary level only):** Hall left NIH in April 2025, stating that he
  experienced censorship of his ultra-processed food findings under HHS leadership. Seen in search
  results from US News (2025-04-18), Axios (2025-04-17), CNN (2025-04-16) and STAT (2025-05-16); not
  read. If a lesson mentions it, read a primary report first.

#### 4.2 Mendelian randomisation in nutrition

- **Wade KH, Yarmolinsky J, Giovannucci E, et al. Applying Mendelian randomization to appraise
  causality in relationships between nutrition and cancer. Cancer Causes Control.
  2022;33(5):631-652. doi:10.1007/s10552-022-01562-1. PMID 35274198. PMCID PMC9010389.** Abstract read
  via Europe PMC (A12). Multi-author review including Davey Smith, Burgess and Giovannucci. Quote: "MR
  can be implemented through instrumental variable (IV) analysis and, when robustly performed, is
  generally less prone to confounding, reverse causation and measurement error than conventional
  observational methods and has different sources of bias". Also that MR can "help prioritise or
  deprioritise the evaluation of specific nutritional factors as intervention targets in randomised
  controlled trials." **Best single review to cite.**
- **Larsson SC. Mendelian randomization as a tool for causal inference in human nutrition and
  metabolism. Curr Opin Lipidol. 2021;32(1):1-8. doi:10.1097/mol.0000000000000721.** Abstract read (A13).
  Quote: "Mendelian randomization studies have confirmed some but challenged other nutrition-disease
  associations recognized by traditional observational studies."
- **Schatzkin A, Abnet CC, Cross AJ, et al. Mendelian randomization: how it can--and cannot--help
  confirm causal relations between nutrition and cancer. Cancer Prev Res. 2009;2(2):104-113.
  doi:10.1158/1940-6207.capr-08-0070.** Abstract read (A14). Useful for concrete instruments: "LCT
  (lactase), ALDH2 (aldehyde dehydrogenase), and HFE (hemochromatosis), proxies, respectively, for
  dairy product intake, alcoholic beverage drinking, and serum iron levels." And the caveat: "Sample
  size requirements for Mendelian randomization studies of nutrition and cancer are shown to be
  potentially daunting".
- **Teaching note (my summary):** MR works where a gene variant shifts lifelong exposure (lactase
  persistence and milk; ALDH2 and alcohol). For most foods there is no good genetic instrument, and
  pleiotropy (the variant acting through another route) is the key threat.

#### 4.3 GRADE applied to nutrition (brief; another agent covers red meat content)

- **Johnston BC, Zeraatkar D, Han MA, et al. Unprocessed Red Meat and Processed Meat Consumption:
  Dietary Guideline Recommendations From the Nutritional Recommendations (NutriRECS) Consortium. Ann
  Intern Med. 2019;171(10):756-764. doi:10.7326/m19-1621. PMID 31569235.** Abstract read (A15). The
  abstract notes "This article has been corrected." Method as described: 5 de novo systematic reviews
  (4 on health effects, 1 on values and preferences), "GRADE methods to rate the certainty of evidence
  for each outcome and to move from evidence to recommendations", a 14-member panel including 3
  community members from 7 countries, and "Considerations of environmental impact or animal welfare did
  not bear on the recommendations." Result: "weak recommendation, low-certainty evidence" to continue
  current consumption.
- **Why it matters methodologically (my summary):** GRADE starts observational evidence at "low"
  certainty, so nutrition questions answered mainly by cohorts will rarely rise above low, however
  consistent the cohorts. NutriRECS weighed that low certainty against small absolute effects and
  people's preferences. Critics argue this penalises a field where long trials are impossible, which
  is the Satija argument applied to grading.
- **Schwingshackl L, Knüppel S, Schwedhelm C, et al. Perspective: NutriGrade: A Scoring System to
  Assess and Judge the Meta-Evidence of Randomized Controlled Trials and Cohort Studies in Nutrition
  Research. Adv Nutr. 2016;7(6):994-1004. doi:10.3945/an.116.013052.** Abstract read (A16). An
  alternative to GRADE designed for nutrition, scoring items including "funding bias", "effect size"
  and "dose-response" out of 10 (high 8-10, moderate 6-7.99, low 4-5.99, very low 0-3.99).
- **Schwingshackl L, Balduzzi S, Beyerbach J, et al. Evaluating agreement between bodies of evidence
  from randomised controlled trials and cohort studies in nutrition research: meta-epidemiological
  study. BMJ. 2021;374:n1864. doi:10.1136/bmj.n1864. PMID 34526355. PMCID PMC8441535.** Abstract read
  (A17). 97 diet-disease outcome pairs. Pooled ratio of risk ratios (RCT vs cohort) 1.09 (95% CI 1.04
  to 1.14; I² = 68%; 95% prediction interval 0.81 to 1.46). "When the type of intake or exposure
  between both types of evidence was identical, the estimates were similar." "The prediction interval
  indicated that the difference could be much more substantial, in either direction." **This is the
  best empirical answer to "do cohorts and trials agree?" and it partly vindicates each side.**

  *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 01's Stage 4:* **Two passages of the full text read**, the Methods
  paragraph on how the pairs were compared and the first paragraph of the Results, fetched with curl
  from Europe PMC's fullTextXML for PMC8441535 and stripped of markup by script. The rest of the full
  text was not read. They change how the abstract's 1.09 may be taught, in two ways. First, it pools
  only the pairs with binary outcomes, not all 97. Second, the authors say the ratio shows a
  difference between the two bodies of evidence, not which one finds the bigger effect, and give an
  example in which less benefit and more harm in the trials produce the same ratio. Verbatim (a
  superscript reference numeral, 30, after "ratio of risk ratios," is omitted from the first):
  - "Binary outcomes were expressed as ratio of risk ratios, while continuous outcomes were
    expressed as differences of mean differences. By using the BoE of cohort studies as the
    reference group, we examined the pooled estimate to determine a relatively larger or smaller
    estimate from the BoE of randomised controlled trials (that is, effect of BoE of trials > effect
    of BoE of cohort studies, or effect of BoE of trials < effect of BoE of cohort studies)."
  - "For example, a risk ratio from randomised controlled trials of 0.95 and a risk ratio from
    cohort studies of 0.90 would result in a ratio of risk ratios of 1.06; whereas a risk of 1.00 in
    cohort studies compared with a risk ratio of 1.06 in randomised controlled trials would also
    result in a ratio of risk ratios of 1.06. Therefore, the ratio of risk ratios should not be
    interpreted as larger or smaller treatment effects in one type of study (eg, randomised
    controlled trials), but only as differences between the two BoEs; and the direction of
    difference depends on direction of effect of the underlying BoEs."
  - Results: "Of the identified 97 diet-disease outcome pairs, 83 were included in the meta-analysis
    (71 binary, 12 continuous)."

---

### 5. The Women's Health Initiative Dietary Modification Trial

#### 5.1 Prentice RL, Caan B, Chlebowski RT, et al. Low-fat dietary pattern and risk of invasive breast cancer: the Women's Health Initiative Randomized Controlled Dietary Modification Trial. JAMA. 2006;295(6):629-642. doi:10.1001/jama.295.6.629. PMID 16467232. NCT00000611.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A18).
- **Design:** RCT at 40 US centres, 1993 to 2005. 48,835 postmenopausal women aged 50 to 79;
  intervention 40% (n = 19,541), comparison 60% (n = 29,294). Goals: total fat to 20% of energy,
  vegetables and fruit at least 5 servings/day, grains at least 6 servings/day. Comparison group
  "were not asked to make dietary changes."
- **Adherence:** difference between groups in percentage of energy from fat was 10.7% at year 1 and
  8.1% at year 6 (i.e. well short of the full contrast the design aimed for).
- **Result:** 655 (0.42%/year) vs 1072 (0.45%/year) invasive breast cancers over 8.1 years; "hazard
  ratio, 0.91; 95% confidence interval, 0.83-1.01". Conclusion: "a low-fat dietary pattern did not
  result in a statistically significant reduction in invasive breast cancer risk over an 8.1-year
  average follow-up period."
- **Limitations the source states:** secondary analyses "suggest a lower hazard ratio among adherent
  women" and more benefit in women with high baseline fat intake; authors call for longer follow-up.

#### 5.2 Howard BV, Van Horn L, Hsia J, et al. Low-fat dietary pattern and risk of cardiovascular disease: the Women's Health Initiative Randomized Controlled Dietary Modification Trial. JAMA. 2006;295(6):655-666. doi:10.1001/jama.295.6.655. PMID 16467234.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A19).
- By year 6, fat intake 8.2% of energy lower in intervention; saturated fat down only 2.9%. LDL fell
  3.55 mg/dL. CHD HR 0.97 (0.90-1.06), stroke 1.02 (0.90-1.15), CVD 0.98 (0.92-1.05). "Trends toward
  greater reductions in CHD risk were observed in those with lower intakes of saturated fat or trans
  fat or higher intakes of vegetables/fruits."
- **Teaching point (my summary):** the trial tested "reduce total fat", not "replace saturated with
  unsaturated fat". It is often cited as evidence that dietary fat does not matter for heart disease,
  which overstates what it tested.

#### 5.3 Chlebowski RT, Aragaki AK, Anderson GL, et al. Dietary Modification and Breast Cancer Mortality: Long-Term Follow-Up of the Women's Health Initiative Randomized Trial. J Clin Oncol. 2020;38(13):1419-1428. doi:10.1200/jco.19.00435. PMID 32031879. PMCID PMC7193750.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A20).
- After 19.6 years median cumulative follow-up: deaths after breast cancer HR 0.85 (0.74 to 0.96);
  deaths from breast cancer HR 0.79 (0.64 to 0.97; P = .02). Absolute annualised risks are small
  (0.037% vs 0.047%). Note eligibility: women with baseline fat intake "≥ 32% of energy".
- **Caution (my note):** a secondary/long-term outcome, emerging after the intervention period, with
  many outcomes examined across WHI's history. Present as suggestive, not as settling the question.

#### 5.4 Two readings of WHI (for the lesson)

- **"Huge null trial" reading:** the largest, most expensive diet trial found no significant effect on
  its primary outcomes. (Prentice 2006; Howard 2006 abstracts.)
- **"Failed to test the hypothesis" reading:** adherence fell short of target and the contrast was
  diluted (Satija et al. 2015, quoted above).
- **"Slow real effect" reading:** long-term follow-up shows breast cancer mortality benefit
  (Chlebowski 2020).
- Each is advanced by serious researchers; the lesson should present all three.

#### 5.5 PREDIMED, retracted and republished (context for diet RCTs)

- **Estruch R, Ros E, Salas-Salvadó J, et al. Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease with a
  Mediterranean Diet Supplemented with Extra-Virgin Olive Oil or Nuts. N Engl J Med.
  2018;378(25):e34. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1800389. PMID 29897866.** Abstract read (A24). 7447
  participants; the investigators "identified protocol deviations, including enrollment of household
  members without randomization, assignment to a study group without randomization of some
  participants at 1 of 11 study sites". Revised HRs 0.69 (0.53-0.91) olive oil and 0.72 (0.54-0.95)
  nuts vs control. Another agent may cover PREDIMED's content; included here as a methods case.

---

### 6. Reversals: beta-carotene

Background claim (quoted from ATBC's own abstract): "Epidemiologic evidence indicates that diets
high in carotenoid-rich fruits and vegetables, as well as high serum levels of vitamin E
(alpha-tocopherol) and beta carotene, are associated with a reduced risk of lung cancer."

#### 6.1 The Alpha-Tocopherol, Beta Carotene Cancer Prevention Study Group. The effect of vitamin E and beta carotene on the incidence of lung cancer and other cancers in male smokers. N Engl J Med. 1994;330(15):1029-1035. doi:10.1056/NEJM199404143301501. PMID 8127329.

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A21).
- **Design:** randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 2x2 factorial; 29,133 male smokers aged 50
  to 69, south-western Finland; alpha-tocopherol 50 mg/day, beta-carotene 20 mg/day, both, or
  placebo; 5 to 8 years.
- **Results:** 876 lung cancers. Beta-carotene: lung cancer incidence change +18% (95% CI 3 to 36%).
  Total mortality 8% higher (1 to 16%) with beta-carotene. Vitamin E: no effect on lung cancer (-2%,
  -14 to 12%); fewer prostate cancers; more haemorrhagic stroke deaths.
- **Quote:** "this trial raises the possibility that these supplements may actually have harmful as
  well as beneficial effects."

#### 6.2 Omenn GS, Goodman GE, Thornquist MD, et al. Effects of a combination of beta carotene and vitamin A on lung cancer and cardiovascular disease. N Engl J Med. 1996;334(18):1150-1155. doi:10.1056/NEJM199605023341802. PMID 8602180. (CARET)

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A22).
- **Design:** multicentre, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled; 18,314 smokers, former smokers
  and asbestos-exposed workers; 30 mg beta-carotene plus 25,000 IU retinyl palmitate daily.
- **Results:** 388 lung cancers in 73,135 person-years (mean 4.0 years). Lung cancer RR 1.28 (1.04 to
  1.57); death from any cause RR 1.17 (1.03 to 1.33); lung cancer death 1.46 (1.07 to 2.00); CVD
  death 1.26 (0.99 to 1.61). "the randomized trial was stopped 21 months earlier than planned".

#### 6.3 Hennekens CH, Buring JE, Manson JE, et al. Lack of effect of long-term supplementation with beta carotene on the incidence of malignant neoplasms and cardiovascular disease. N Engl J Med. 1996;334(18):1145-1149. doi:10.1056/NEJM199605023341801. PMID 8602179. (Physicians' Health Study)

- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (A23).
- **Design:** 22,071 US male physicians aged 40 to 84, beta-carotene 50 mg on alternate days vs
  placebo, 1982 to 1995 (about 12 years); 11% current smokers.
- **Result:** malignant neoplasms 1273 vs 1293, RR 0.98 (0.91 to 1.06). "12 years of supplementation
  with beta carotene produced neither benefit nor harm".
- **Note:** Willett and Stampfer are co-authors. The Harvard group ran one of the trials that
  overturned the observational hope, which is worth saying in a lesson about how the field corrects
  itself.

#### 6.4 How to read the reversal (my synthesis)

The cohorts measured beta-carotene in food or blood, which marks a diet rich in fruit and
vegetables and, plausibly, non-smoking and other healthy habits. The trials gave high-dose isolated
beta-carotene pills, often to smokers. So the reversal shows both residual confounding (the
Ioannidis reading) and a mismatch between the question asked of cohorts and the one asked of trials
(the Satija and Schwingshackl reading). Both lessons are true and the course should teach both.

  "Weight Management: Overweight, Obesity and Underweight" with Highlight "The Latest and Greatest

  Highlight 1 was "Nutrition Information and Misinformation"; Highlight 2 was "Vegetarian Diets";
  Highlight 3 was "Common Digestive Problems".
- **Shape:** nutrition basics and diet planning, digestion, the three macronutrients, energy

  food safety, and global hunger. Research methods are handled in Highlight 1 (misinformation), not a
  dedicated chapter.

#### 7.2 Collene AL, Spees C. Wardlaw's Contemporary Nutrition. McGraw Hill; "2026 Release" (Evergreen). ISBN 9781265687328.

- **What I read:** **search-summary level only.** Oxford Academic (academic.oup.com/book/27443)

  URL seen in search: "24-Hour Recall and Diet Record Methods" at academic.oup.com/book/27443/chapter/197312776.
  A search summary gave this chapter list (unverified, do not treat as exact wording): 1 Overview of
  Nutritional Epidemiology; 2 Foods and Nutrients; 3 Nature of Variation in Diet; 4 24-Hour Recall
  and Diet Record Methods; 5 Food Frequency Methods; 6 Reproducibility and Validity of
  Food-Frequency Questionnaires; 7 Recall of Remote Diet; 8 Biochemical Indicators of Dietary Intake;
  9 Anthropometric Measures and Body Composition; 10 Assessment of Physical Activity in Nutritional
  Epidemiology; 11 Implications of Total Energy Intake for Epidemiologic Analyses; 12 Correction for
  the Effects of Measurement Error; 13 Issues in Analysis and Presentation of Dietary Data; 14
  Genetics in Dietary Analyses (title uncertain); 15 Nutrition Monitoring and Surveillance; 16 Policy
  Applications; later chapters not captured.
- **Why it matters:** the field's standard methods text, by the field's leading defender. Chapters 11
  and 12 (energy adjustment and measurement-error correction) are the technical core of the Subar
  2015 and Satija 2015 replies to the critics.

### 8. Syllabi

#### 8.1 Stanford Introduction to Food and Health (Coursera; instructor Maya Adam, MD; Stanford Online)

- **What I read:** coursera.org/learn/food-and-health (curl), page as of 2026-09-24. 5 modules, about
  6 hours. Sequence, verbatim module and video titles:
  - Module 1, "Background on Food, Nutrients, and Contemporary Eating Trends": A Sociocultural History
    of Obesity; Macronutrient Structure & Metabolism; Carbohydrate-rich Foods & the Glycemic Index;
    Animal & Plant-based Proteins; Dietary Fats & Their Effects on Human Health; Why Are Highly
    Processed Foods Generally Less Healthy?; Trends in Sugar Consumption & Recommendations; The Case
    for Cooking; A Case Study: Middle-aged Pre-diabetic Man; A Step-wise Approach to Behavior Change.
  - Module 2, "Future Directions in Health - Part I": Eat Food. Not Too Much. Mostly Plants.; three
    cooking videos (Fundamental Ingredients, Increasing Vegetable Intake, Sensible Substitutions).
  - Module 3, "Future Directions in Health - Part 2": Constructing a Healthy Plate; Shopping in a
    Supermarket; Reading Nutrition Labels; The Importance of Moderation in Maintaining a Healthy Diet.
  - Module 4, "Cooking Workshop" (10 recipe videos); Module 5, "Recommended Optional Resources".
- **Stance:** explicitly food-first and anti-reductionist: the description says it "will shift the
  focus away from reductionist discussions about nutrients and move, instead, towards practical
  discussions about real food and the environment in which we consume it." Popular (rating 4.8 from
  10,633 ratings) but light on evidence and methods.

#### 8.2 Wageningen University, "Nutrition and Health: Macronutrients and Overnutrition" (edX, NUTR101x; Prof. Sander Kersten)

- **What I read:** the official course syllabus PDF on edX's CDN (dated 12/13/2018, for the 1T2018
  run) via curl. About 50 hours; "The level of the course is introductory. The prerequisites are
  basic biology." Module sequence verbatim:
  1. Basic Principles ("the basics of nutritional science and ... the different study designs that
     are used in nutrition research")
  2. Carbohydrates
  3. Carbohydrates and Health (sugar)
  4. Lipids
  5. Lipids and Health (cardiovascular disease, fatty acids and cholesterol)
  6. Proteins and Health
  7. Energy homeostasis sand [sic] energy balance
  8. Weight management
  9. Nutrition and Sports
- A companion course, "Micronutrients and Malnutrition", completes a two-course Professional
  Certificate "Food, Nutrition and Health" (from the same PDF).
- **Note:** study designs come in module 1, before any nutrient. That is the order this course
  should copy. The current edX page may differ from the 2018 syllabus; I did not read the current
  page.

#### 8.3 UC Berkeley NST 10, Introduction to Human Nutrition (Fall 2019)

- **What I read:** the Fall 2019 syllabus PDF, archived by a Berkeley student organisation
  (tbp.studentorg.berkeley.edu/syllabi/1151/download), via curl. Course Director Marc Hellerstein;
  with Joseph Napoli and William Evans. Textbook: Smolin & Grosvenor, Nutrition: Science and
  Applications, 4th edition. Lecture sequence (my condensed transcription of the schedule table):
  1. Introduction: the modern field of nutrition and health
  2. Health consequences of obesity and related conditions
  3. Why do diets fail? Energy balance and weight control: what works, do macronutrients matter
  4. Nature of nutrients: macronutrients and micronutrients
  5. Digestion, absorption and metabolism of macronutrients
  6. Lipids I and II
  7. Calorie deficiency: effects on metabolism, growth, reproduction; lifespan
  8. Protein/calorie deficiency: stunting in children, interaction with infection
  9. Exam 1
  10. Dietary carbohydrates: insulin resistance and glycemic control
  11. Protein metabolism, nutrition and exercise
  12. Changing nutritional needs with aging
  13. Exercise in the prevention and treatment of chronic disease
  14. Muscle biology and the physiology of exercise and sport
  15. Natural history of nutrition-related chronic disease (1): insulin resistance, type 2 diabetes,
      fatty liver disease
  16. Exam 2
  17. Natural history of nutrition-related chronic disease (2): metabolic syndrome, cardiovascular
      disease
  18. Fat soluble vitamins; Water soluble vitamins
  19. Eating disorders and reproductive health; bone, calcium and osteoporosis
  20. Nutrition and pregnancy
  21. Dietary recommendations
  22. Natural history of nutrition-related chronic disease (3): diet and carcinogenesis; prevention of
      cancer
  Section topics include "The DRIs", "Nutrition Labels", "Food Insecurity", "Nutrition Research",
  "Protein & Vegetarianism", "Popular diets", "Dietary Supplements", "Food Technology". A three-day
  diet analysis project used USDA SuperTracker (since retired).
- **Note:** opens with obesity and energy balance, not with nutrient chemistry; physiology and chronic
  disease heavy; research methods appear only as one discussion-section topic.

#### 8.4 Pattern across the three (my synthesis)

All three put macronutrients before micronutrients and end on chronic disease or practical eating.
Only Wageningen puts study design first. None devotes a unit to measurement error or to the
critique/defence debate. That is a gap this course can fill.
