The 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans (DGA) introduced substantial controversy by diverging from long-standing practices and many scientific recommendations of the 2025 Dietary Guidelines Advisory Committee (DGAC). The final guidelines promote dietary protein intake of 1.2 to 1.6 g/kg/d. These recommendations rely on a rapid systematic review of randomized controlled trials focused on weight-management outcomes and, to a lesser extent, a narrative review on nutrient adequacy. Recognizing protein as a component of a healthy diet is consistent with recent editions and a strength of the 2025-2030 DGA. However, the 2025-2030 DGA emphasizes a single dimension of the evidence base and gives limited attention to the relationship between protein intake and risk for, or management of, diet-related chronic disease, including cardiovascular disease, type 2 diabetes, chronic kidney disease, cancer, and bone disease. The scientific basis underlying the new protein guidance was, in our assessment, insufficiently comprehensive while also departing from the DGA process to be food based and not nutrient based in the context of protein. These recommendations also lack sufficient clarity to distinguish between varying dietary macronutrient strategies for managing body weight in individuals with overweight or obesity and the specific protein requirements linked to the physiological roles of individual amino acids. The Scientific Foundation for the 2025-2030 DGA states that the recommendations were developed independent of equity and inclusion, raising practical concerns with how new higher protein targets will be interpreted and applied across diverse populations and federal nutrition programs. In this Perspective, we summarize the new protein recommendations, compare them with past DGA editions, the Scientific Report of the 2025 DGAC, and current population-level protein intake patterns, as well as highlight strengths and areas needing additional clarity or research. Central to our perspective is the view that evidence derived from weight loss studies does not provide a sufficient scope of evidence to support a universal dietary protein recommendation for all Americans across the lifespan.


---

## Part B: Energy balance, weight, and diets for weight loss

Research notes for "Nutrition: The Evidence", Stage 1. Compiled 2026-09-24.

### How these notes were gathered

- Abstracts were fetched as JSON from the Europe PMC REST API with curl (not WebFetch), so every
  quoted abstract sentence is copied from the API response. HTML tags were stripped; superscripts
  appear as `^` (for example "kg m^-2"). The full abstracts are in the Appendix, generated by script
  from the same responses, so nothing in the appendix was retyped by hand.
- Where a paper is open access in PubMed Central, I fetched the full text XML from NCBI efetch with
  curl and read the sections named in each entry. Citation brackets and figure references were
  stripped by the script, which is why some quoted sentences have "()" or doubled spaces where a
  reference number used to be.
- Guideline pages (WHO, NICE) were fetched with curl and the HTML converted to text by script.
- Unless a line is inside quotation marks, it is my summary.
- Nothing here came from WebSearch or WebFetch summaries.

---

### 1. Hall 2019: ultra-processed vs unprocessed diet, inpatient crossover RCT

**Citation.** Hall KD, Ayuketah A, Brychta R, Cai H, Cassimatis T, Chen KY, Chung ST, Costa E,
Courville A, Darcey V, Fletcher LA, Forde CG, Gharib AM, Guo J, Howard R, Joseph PV, McGehee S,
Ouwerkerk R, Raisinger K, Rozga I, Stagliano M, Walter M, Walter PJ, Yang S, Zhou M.
"Ultra-Processed Diets Cause Excess Calorie Intake and Weight Gain: An Inpatient Randomized
Controlled Trial of Ad Libitum Food Intake." *Cell Metabolism* 2019;30(1):67-77.e3.
doi:10.1016/j.cmet.2019.05.008. PMID 31105044. PMCID PMC7946062.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC (appendix). Full text via PMC: introduction, the diet
design paragraph, the results on energy intake, energy density, eating rate, appetite ratings,
hormones and glucose tolerance, "Limitations of Study", and the conclusion.

**Design.** Randomized crossover, inpatient at the NIH Clinical Center. 20 weight-stable adults,
mean age 31.2, mean BMI 27. Two weeks on each diet, one straight after the other, no washout.
Meals matched for presented calories, energy density, macronutrients, sugar, sodium and fibre.
Eat as much or as little as you like.

**Key numbers (abstract).** Energy intake was higher on the ultra-processed diet by
"508 ± 106 kcal/day; p = 0.0001", from more carbohydrate (280 ± 54 kcal/day) and fat
(230 ± 53 kcal/day) but not protein (-2 ± 12 kcal/day). Participants gained
"0.9 ± 0.3 kg (p = 0.009) during the ultra-processed diet and losing 0.9 ± 0.3 kg (p = 0.007)
during the unprocessed diet". Weight change correlated with intake, r = 0.8. The values are
mean ± SE (the abstract says "mean ± SE" for age and BMI).

**Details from the full text that matter for teaching.**
- The matching was on *presented* food. The paper says the diets still differed in the share of
  added to total sugar ("~54% vs 1%"), insoluble to total fibre ("~16% versus 77%"), saturated to
  total fat ("~34% vs 19%") and omega-6 to omega-3 ratio ("~11:1 vs. 5:1").
- Energy density of what was actually eaten differed: "1.36±0.02 kcal/g vs. 1.09±0.02 kcal/g".
  The authors explain the presented meals looked matched only because fibre supplements were
  dissolved in beverages served with the ultra-processed meals, and say "the ~85% higher energy
  density of the non-beverage foods in the ultra-processed versus unprocessed diets () likely
  contributed to the observed excess energy intake".
- People ate faster on the ultra-processed diet: "(17±1 kcal/min; p<0.0001)" faster.
- Palatability was *not* rated differently: "Participants did not report significant differences in
  the pleasantness (4.8±3.1; p=0.13) or familiarity (2.7±4.6; p=0.57) of the meals". Hunger,
  fullness and satisfaction ratings did not differ either.
- Protein leverage: the authors calculate it "could potentially explain at most ~50% of the
  observed energy intake differences".
- Hormones: PYY (an appetite-suppressing hormone) rose and ghrelin fell on the unprocessed diet.
- Cost: ingredients for 2000 kcal/day were estimated at "$106 versus $151" per week for
  ultra-processed versus unprocessed.

**Limitations the paper states (full text, "Limitations of Study").**
- "Our study was not designed to identify the cause of the observed differences in energy
  intake."
- No weight-maintenance run-in and no washout.
- "Finally, the inpatient environment of the metabolic ward makes it difficult to generalize our
  results to free-living conditions."
- Meals were free and not chosen by the participants, so the study says nothing about cost and
  convenience in real choices.
- Closing caution: policies "should be sensitive to the time, skill, expense, and effort required
  to prepare meals from minimally processed foods".

**What it establishes (my summary).** Under controlled conditions, over two weeks, a diet made
mostly of ultra-processed foods led people to eat about 500 kcal/day more and gain weight, even
though the menus offered similar nutrients. It does not establish *why*: energy density of the
solid food, eating rate and protein content are candidate explanations the authors themselves
raise. It is one small, short trial.

#### 1a. A longer recent trial: Dicken 2025

**Citation.** Dicken SJ, Jassil FC, Brown A, Kalis M, Stanley C, Ranson C, Ruwona T, Qamar S,
Buck C, Mallik R, Hamid N, Bird JM, Brown A, Norton B, Gandini Wheeler-Kingshott CAM, Hamer M,
van Tulleken C, Hall KD, Fisher A, Makaronidis J, Batterham RL. "Ultraprocessed or minimally
processed diets following healthy dietary guidelines on weight and cardiometabolic health: a
randomized, crossover trial." *Nature Medicine* 2025;31(10):3297-3308.
doi:10.1038/s41591-025-03842-0. PMID 40760353. PMCID PMC12532614.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC only.

**Design.** 2 × 2 crossover feeding trial in England, 55 adults (BMI 25 to under 40, habitual UPF
intake at least 50% of calories), two 8-week ad libitum diets that both followed the UK Eatwell
Guide, one minimally processed and one ultra-processed. The abstract says participants "were
provided with" the diets; I did not read the methods, so I cannot say whether food was eaten at
home or under supervision (the writer should check before calling it free-living).
50 in the intention-to-treat sample.

**Key numbers.** Both diets produced weight loss: MPF "-2.06 (95% confidence interval (CI), -2.99,
-1.13)" percent and UPF "-1.05 (95% CI, -1.98, -0.13)" percent. Difference "-1.01 (95% CI, -1.87,
-0.14), P = 0.024".

**Why it matters (my summary).** It is the first longer, larger test and it points the same way as
Hall 2019, but the effect is smaller, and it shows that even a UPF diet that follows national
guidelines led to weight loss relative to baseline. It is new (2025) and worth naming as the
current state of the question.

---

### 2. The carbohydrate-insulin model (CIM) versus the energy balance model (EBM)

This is a genuinely contested question among credentialed researchers. The two sides are given
here in their own words, then the key experiments and the fight over one of them.

#### 2a. The proponents' statement: Ludwig 2021

**Citation.** Ludwig DS, Aronne LJ, Astrup A, de Cabo R, Cantley LC, Friedman MI, Heymsfield SB,
Johnson JD, King JC, Krauss RM, Lieberman DE, Taubes G, Volek JS, Westman EC, Willett WC, Yancy WS,
Ebbeling CB. "The carbohydrate-insulin model: a physiological perspective on the obesity
pandemic." *American Journal of Clinical Nutrition* 2021;114(6):1873-1885.
doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqab270. PMID 34515299. PMCID PMC8634575.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC. Full text via PMC: introduction, the section on dietary
drivers, the mechanism section on glycemic load, the section on short-term studies, the response to the charge that
they "move the goalposts", the research agenda and the conclusion.

**Design.** Perspective (a theoretical argument with a review of evidence), 17 authors.

**The claim in their words.**
- "According to the CIM, increasing fat deposition in the body-resulting from the hormonal
  responses to a high-glycemic-load diet-drives positive energy balance." (abstract)
- "Conceptualizing obesity as a disorder of energy balance restates a principle of physics
  without considering the biological mechanisms that promote weight gain." (abstract)
- Full text, the two models side by side: "In the carbohydrate-insulin model (CIM), a crucial
  effect of diet is metabolic, by influencing substrate partitioning. Rapidly digestible
  carbohydrates, acting through insulin and other hormones, cause increased fat deposition, and
  thereby drive a positive energy balance."
- The proposed mechanism (my summary of the full text): a high-glycemic-load meal raises insulin,
  which pushes fuel into storage; a few hours later circulating fuel falls, the brain reads this
  as energy shortage, and hunger rises and energy expenditure may fall. They say "The CIM posits a
  state akin to “internal starvation”" and that "Studies of cellular metabolism and energy sensing
  are needed to test this hypothesis."

**Their answer to short inpatient trials (important for fairness).** They argue short feeding
studies can mislead because adaptation to a low-carbohydrate diet takes weeks: "Short-term
studies of metabolic outcomes may also produce misleading results, related to adaptive processes
after changes in macronutrients." They cite ketones not reaching steady state "until 3 wk" and
their own meta-analysis (section 2e below) finding the direction reverses after about 2.5 weeks.
Of the Ebbeling feeding study they write: "Contrary to theoretical concerns for experimental error (),
data from a large 5-mo study demonstrating increased energy expenditure when consuming a
low-carbohydrate diet during weight-loss maintenance appear robust".

**What they say would settle it (full text).** Feeding studies "of sufficient duration to
distinguish transient from chronic macronutrient effects (≥1 mo)", efficacy trials of at least a
year with strong adherence, and long cohort studies from childhood. They also ask both sides to
"depersonalize the debate, scrupulously avoiding ad hominem argument."

#### 2b. The other side's statement: Hall 2022

**Citation.** Hall KD, Farooqi IS, Friedman JM, Klein S, Loos RJF, Mangelsdorf DJ, O'Rahilly S,
Ravussin E, Redman LM, Ryan DH, Speakman JR, Tobias DK. "The energy balance model of obesity:
beyond calories in, calories out." *American Journal of Clinical Nutrition* 2022;115(5):1243-1254.
doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqac031. PMID 35134825. PMCID PMC9071483.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC only. (PMC lists the article but the publisher does not
allow XML download, so I could not read the full text by script.)

**The claim in their words (abstract).**
- The earlier Perspective "conflated and confused the principle of energy balance, a law of
  physics that is agnostic as to obesity mechanisms, with the EBM as a theoretical model of
  obesity that is firmly based on biology."
- Their EBM: "the brain is the primary organ responsible for body weight regulation operating
  mainly below our conscious awareness via complex endocrine, metabolic, and nervous system
  signals to control food intake in response to the body's dynamic energy needs as well as
  environmental influences."
- On the CIM's revision: "the new CIM can be considered a special case of the more comprehensive
  EBM but with a narrower focus on diets high in glycemic load as the primary factor responsible
  for common obesity."

**Teaching point (my summary).** Both camps accept the first law of thermodynamics. The dispute is
about causal direction and about what drives intake: the CIM says hormonal fat storage comes first
and overeating follows; the EBM says the brain regulates intake in response to many signals,
including a food environment of cheap, energy-dense, palatable food. Note that the 2021 CIM paper
itself objects that textbook EBM statements treat "all calories metabolically alike", and the 2022
EBM paper objects that the CIM paper attacked a caricature. Each side says the other misstates it.

#### 2c. The key inpatient test: Hall 2021 Nature Medicine

**Citation.** Hall KD, Guo J, Courville AB, Boring J, Brychta R, Chen KY, Darcey V, Forde CG,
Gharib AM, Gallagher I, Howard R, Joseph PV, Milley L, Ouwerkerk R, Raisinger K, Rozga I, Schick A,
Stagliano M, Torres S, Walter M, Walter P, Yang S, Chung ST. "Effect of a plant-based, low-fat diet
versus an animal-based, ketogenic diet on ad libitum energy intake." *Nature Medicine*
2021;27(2):344-353. doi:10.1038/s41591-020-01209-1. PMID 33479499. Not in PMC.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC only.

**Design.** Inpatient crossover, 20 adults, 2 weeks on each diet, ad libitum. Low-fat diet:
10.3% fat, 75.2% carbohydrate, glycemic load 85 g per 1,000 kcal. Ketogenic diet: 75.8% fat, 10.0%
carbohydrate, glycemic load 6 g per 1,000 kcal. Both minimally processed. One participant withdrew
"due to hypoglycemia during the low-carbohydrate diet".

**Key numbers.** "the low-fat diet led to 689 ± 73 kcal d^-1 less energy intake than the
low-carbohydrate diet over 2 weeks (P < 0.0001) and 544 ± 68 kcal d^-1 less over the final week
(P < 0.0001)." Conclusion: "the predictions of the carbohydrate-insulin model were inconsistent
with our observations."

**Limits (my summary, not stated in the abstract).** Two weeks per diet, which is exactly the
duration the CIM authors say is too short (section 2a). The diets differ in many ways at once
(plant versus animal, fibre, energy density), so the result speaks to the CIM's intake prediction
rather than isolating carbohydrate. I did not read the full text and so cannot report what the
paper says about weight or fat change.

#### 2d. The proponents' key test: Ebbeling 2018 BMJ, and the dispute over it

**Citation.** Ebbeling CB, Feldman HA, Klein GL, Wong JMW, Bielak L, Steltz SK, Luoto PK, Wolfe RR,
Wong WW, Ludwig DS. "Effects of a low carbohydrate diet on energy expenditure during weight loss
maintenance: randomized trial." *BMJ* 2018;363:k4583. doi:10.1136/bmj.k4583. PMID 30429127.
PMCID PMC6233655.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Randomized feeding trial, two US sites, 164 adults with BMI 25 or more. After losing
12% (within 2%) of body weight on a run-in diet, participants were randomized to high (60%),
moderate (40%) or low (20%) carbohydrate diets for 20 weeks, calories adjusted to hold weight
steady within 2 kg. Primary outcome total energy expenditure by doubly labelled water.

**Key numbers.** "a linear trend of 52 kcal/d (95% confidence interval 23 to 82) for every 10%
decrease in the contribution of carbohydrate". Low versus high carbohydrate: "209 kcal/d (91 to
326) greater" (intention to treat). Per protocol: "131 kcal/d (-6 to 267) and 278 kcal/d (144 to
411)". In the highest third of insulin secretion the low versus high difference was "308 kcal/d in
the intention-to-treat analysis and 478 kcal/d in the per protocol analysis". Conclusion:
"Consistent with the carbohydrate-insulin model, lowering dietary carbohydrate increased energy
expenditure during weight loss maintenance."

**The critique: Hall, Guo and Speakman 2019.** Hall KD, Guo J, Speakman JR. "Do low-carbohydrate
diets increase energy expenditure?" *International Journal of Obesity* 2019;43(12):2350-2354.
doi:10.1038/s41366-019-0456-3. PMID 31548574. PMCID PMC8076039.
**What I read:** full text via PMC (the whole short article; it has no abstract). They downloaded
Ebbeling's public data and code and reanalysed. Their points, quoted:
- Analysis plan: "While the original preregistered analysis plan was in place for most of the
  study's history, it was changed after all subjects had completed the trial."
- Using the original plan (anchoring to pre-weight-loss baseline): "The original preregistered
  analysis plan did not result in significant diet differences in TEE". Decreases were
  "240 ± 64 kcal/day, 322 ± 66 kcal/day, and 356 ± 67 kcal/day" for low, moderate and high
  (p = 0.43).
- Unaccounted energy: participants were given all their food, yet "the reported energy intake was
  460 ± 46 kcal/day (p < 0.0001) less than the reported TEE", suggesting off-study eating; the
  effect shrank when participants with large unaccounted energy were excluded, and "A
  statistically significant TEE diet effect (p < 0.05) required inclusion of subjects with >600
  kcal/day of unaccounted energy."
- Measurement: doubly labelled water measures CO2, and "No significant diet differences in CO2
  production were found"; so "TEE diet differences were entirely due to assumed RQ differences
  between the diets".
- Fairness note: in their own exploratory ANCOVA, TEE "was 255 ± 88 kcal/day (p = 0.004) greater
  on the low- versus high-carbohydrate diet at the study midpoint and 224 ± 81 kcal/day
  (p = 0.007) greater at the end". The critics report this themselves. They also write that they
  "cannot rule out diet differences in other components of TEE that were not quantified".

**The proponents' reply: Ebbeling 2020 J Nutr.** Ebbeling CB, Bielak L, Lakin PR, Klein GL, Wong
JMW, Luoto PK, Wong WW, Ludwig DS. "Energy Requirement Is Higher During Weight-Loss Maintenance in
Adults Consuming a Low- Compared with High-Carbohydrate Diet." *Journal of Nutrition*
2020;150(8):2009-2015. doi:10.1093/jn/nxaa150. PMID 32470981. PMCID PMC7398766.
**What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC. They answer the doubly-labelled-water objection using a
different measure: how many calories each group had to be fed to keep weight stable. "Mean EER was
higher in the Low- versus High-Carbohydrate group in models of varying covariate structure
involving ITT [ranging from 181 (95% CI: 8-353) to 246 (64-427) kcal/d; P ≤0.04]". They conclude
the data "lend qualified support for the validity of the DLW method with diets varying in
macronutrient composition". The confidence intervals are wide (lower bound as low as 8 kcal/d).

#### 2e. The two meta-analyses of energy expenditure

- **Hall and Guo 2017.** Hall KD, Guo J. "Obesity Energetics: Body Weight Regulation and the Effects
  of Diet Composition." *Gastroenterology* 2017;152(7):1718-1727.e3. doi:10.1053/j.gastro.2017.01.052.
  PMID 28193517. PMCID PMC5568065. **Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. Meta-analysis of 32
  controlled feeding studies with isocaloric swaps of carbohydrate for fat: "both energy expenditure
  (26 kcal/d; P <.0001) and fat loss (16 g/d; P <.0001) were greater with lower fat diets." The
  differences are tiny in practical terms.
- **Ludwig 2021 J Nutr reanalysis.** Ludwig DS, Dickinson SL, Henschel B, Ebbeling CB, Allison DB.
  "Do Lower-Carbohydrate Diets Increase Total Energy Expenditure? An Updated and Reanalyzed
  Meta-Analysis of 29 Controlled-Feeding Studies." *Journal of Nutrition* 2021;151(3):482-490.
  doi:10.1093/jn/nxaa350. PMID 33274750. PMCID PMC7948201. **Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. Median
  trial length 4 days. In 23 shorter trials TEE was lower on lower-carbohydrate diets ("-50.0
  kcal/d; 95% CI: -77.4, -22.6 kcal/d"); in 6 longer trials it was higher ("135.4 kcal/d; 95% CI:
  72.0, 198.7 kcal/d"). Conclusion: "Lower-carbohydrate diets transiently reduce TEE, with a larger
  increase after ∼2.5 wk." Note only six longer trials, and the Ebbeling 2018 trial is one of them
  (my inference from its 20-week duration; I did not read the list of included trials).

**What would settle it (my synthesis of both sides).** Long (at least a month, ideally several)
inpatient or tightly controlled feeding trials, preregistered analysis plans followed as written,
energy expenditure measured by respiration chamber as well as doubly labelled water, and ideally
adversarial collaboration (both sides design it together). Both Ludwig 2021 and the CIM Perspective
call for exactly this kind of trial.

---

### 3. Head-to-head diet trials

#### 3a. DIETFITS: Gardner 2018 JAMA

**Citation.** Gardner CD, Trepanowski JF, Del Gobbo LC, Hauser ME, Rigdon J, Ioannidis JPA,
Desai M, King AC. "Effect of Low-Fat vs Low-Carbohydrate Diet on 12-Month Weight Loss in Overweight
Adults and the Association With Genotype Pattern or Insulin Secretion: The DIETFITS Randomized
Clinical Trial." *JAMA* 2018;319(7):667-679. doi:10.1001/jama.2018.0245. PMID 29466592.
PMCID PMC5839290.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** RCT, 609 adults aged 18 to 50, BMI 28 to 40, no diabetes; 12 months; 22 small-group
sessions emphasising diet quality. 481 (79%) completed.

**Key numbers.** Achieved diets: carbohydrate "48% vs 30%", fat "29% vs 45%". Weight change
"-5.3 kg for the HLF diet vs -6.0 kg for the HLC diet (mean between-group difference, 0.7 kg [95%
CI, -0.2 to 1.6 kg])". No interaction with genotype (P = .20) or insulin secretion (P = .47).

**What it establishes (my summary).** With a strong emphasis on food quality on both sides, the
choice between low-fat and low-carbohydrate made no meaningful difference to weight at a year, and
the hypothesis that insulin secretion (a CIM-derived prediction) would tell you which diet suits
you was not supported. A useful detail: the "low-carb" arm settled at 30% carbohydrate, far from
ketogenic.

#### 3b. Named diets network meta-analysis: Johnston 2014 JAMA

**Citation.** Johnston BC, Kanters S, Bandayrel K, Wu P, Naji F, Siemieniuk RA, Ball GD, Busse JW,
Thorlund K, Guyatt G, Jansen JP, Mills EJ. "Comparison of weight loss among named diet programs in
overweight and obese adults: a meta-analysis." *JAMA* 2014;312(9):923-933.
doi:10.1001/jama.2014.10397. PMID 25182101.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Bayesian network meta-analysis, 48 unique RCTs, 7286 people.

**Key numbers.** Versus no diet at 12 months, low-carbohydrate "7.25 kg [95% CI, 5.33 to 9.25 kg]"
and low-fat "7.27 kg [95% CI, 5.26 to 9.34 kg]". "Weight loss differences between individual diets
were minimal." Conclusion: "This supports the practice of recommending any diet that a patient
will adhere to in order to lose weight."

**Caution (my summary).** The numbers are versus no diet, not versus each other, and are 6 to 12
month results; they say nothing about 5 years.

#### 3c. Dansinger 2005 JAMA: Atkins, Ornish, Weight Watchers, Zone

**Citation.** Dansinger ML, Gleason JA, Griffith JL, Selker HP, Schaefer EJ. "Comparison of the
Atkins, Ornish, Weight Watchers, and Zone diets for weight loss and heart disease risk reduction: a
randomized trial." *JAMA* 2005;293(1):43-53. doi:10.1001/jama.293.1.43. PMID 15632335.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Single-centre RCT in Boston, 160 adults (40 per diet), BMI 27 to 42, with hypertension,
dyslipidaemia or fasting hyperglycaemia. One year.

**Key numbers.** One-year weight loss (dropouts counted as no change): Atkins 2.1 kg, Zone 3.2,
Weight Watchers 3.0, Ornish 3.3. Completion: 53%, 65%, 65%, 50%. The central finding: "Amount of
weight loss was associated with self-reported dietary adherence level (r = 0.60; P<.001) but not
with diet type (r = 0.07; P = .40)." Conclusion: "Overall dietary adherence rates were low".

**What it establishes (my summary).** Adherence, not the named diet, predicted weight loss, and
average one-year losses were modest (2 to 3 kg). The two most extreme diets (Atkins, Ornish) had
the worst completion.

---

### 4. Metabolic adaptation: Fothergill 2016, "The Biggest Loser"

**Citation.** Fothergill E, Guo J, Howard L, Kerns JC, Knuth ND, Brychta R, Chen KY, Skarulis MC,
Walter M, Walter PJ, Hall KD. "Persistent metabolic adaptation 6 years after 'The Biggest Loser'
competition." *Obesity* 2016;24(8):1612-1619. doi:10.1002/oby.21538. PMID 27136388. PMCID
PMC4989512.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC; full text via PMC, introduction and the discussion.

**Design.** Follow-up observational study, 14 of 16 original competitors, resting metabolic rate by
indirect calorimetry, body composition by DXA, at baseline, end of the 30-week competition, and 6
years.

**Key numbers (abstract).** Weight loss at the end "58.3 ± 24.9 kg" (mean ± SD); RMR fell
"610 ± 483 kcal/day". After 6 years "41.0 ± 31.3 kg of the lost weight was regained", RMR was
"704 ± 427 kcal/day below baseline" and "metabolic adaptation was -499 ± 207 kcal/day". Adaptation
at the end did not predict regain (r = -0.1), "but those subjects maintaining greater weight loss
at 6 years also experienced greater concurrent metabolic slowing (r = 0.59, P = 0.025)."

**Discussion points (full text).**
- Interpretation: "metabolic adaptation is a proportional, but incomplete, response to
  contemporaneous efforts to reduce body weight from its defended baseline or “set point” value".
- Context that is often left out: despite regain, "the mean weight loss was 11.9 ± 16.8% compared to
  baseline and 57% of the participants maintained at least 10% weight loss", which the authors
  call "quite successful at long-term weight loss compared to other lifestyle interventions".
- Limitation stated: "the extreme and public nature of this weight loss intervention makes it
  difficult to translate our results to more typical weight loss programs."
- They cite a meta-analysis of cross-sectional studies finding people who had lost weight had "a
  3–5% lower RMR" than never-lost controls.

**Caution for the writer (my summary).** n = 14, extreme and televised weight loss. The size of
adaptation here (about 500 kcal/day) should not be presented as typical. The finding that
adaptation did not predict regain runs against the popular story that a broken metabolism makes
you regain.

---

### 5. Energy expenditure across life and with exercise

#### 5a. Pontzer 2021 Science: daily energy expenditure through the life course

**Citation.** Pontzer H, Yamada Y, Sagayama H, [80+ further authors], Speakman JR, IAEA DLW
Database Consortium. "Daily energy expenditure through the human life course." *Science*
2021;373(6556):808-812. doi:10.1126/science.abe5017. PMID 34385400. PMCID PMC8370708.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC; full text via PMC (the author manuscript), all results
sections and discussion.

**Design.** Pooled doubly labelled water database, "n = 6,421; 64% female", 29 countries, ages 8
days to 95 years.

**Key findings.**
- Abstract: fat-free-mass-adjusted expenditure rises to "~50% above adult values at ~1 year;
  declines slowly to adult levels by ~20 years; remains stable in adulthood (20 to 60 years), even
  during pregnancy; then declines in older adults."
- Full text: breakpoint for decline at "63.0 y (95% CI: 60.1, 65.9)"; after 60, "Adjusted total
  expenditure declined by −0.7 ± 0.1% per year"; people in their nineties about 26% below
  middle-aged adults.
- Body size: fat-free mass "accounted for most (83%) of the variation in daily expenditure".
- Individual variation: "TEE and its components varying more than ± 20% even when controlling for
  fat free mass, fat mass, sex, and age".
- Sex: "Sex had no effect on total expenditure in multivariate models with fat free
  mass and fat mass".

**Teaching point (my summary).** Contradicts the common belief that metabolism slows in your 30s
or 40s. Size-adjusted expenditure is flat from 20 to 60. Middle-age weight gain therefore needs a
different explanation than a falling metabolic rate. Caveat: a cross-sectional database, so it
describes ages, not individuals followed over time; basal-expenditure breakpoint estimates for ages
45 to 65 are imprecise, which the authors say themselves ("the relatively small number of basal
measures for 45 – 65 y () reduces our precision").

#### 5b. Pontzer 2016 Current Biology: constrained total energy expenditure

**Citation.** Pontzer H, Durazo-Arvizu R, Dugas LR, Plange-Rhule J, Bovet P, Forrester TE,
Lambert EV, Cooper RS, Schoeller DA, Luke A. "Constrained Total Energy Expenditure and Metabolic
Adaptation to Physical Activity in Adult Humans." *Current Biology* 2016;26(3):410-417.
doi:10.1016/j.cub.2015.12.046. PMID 26832439. PMCID PMC4803033.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC; full text via PMC, results and discussion.

**Design.** Cross-sectional, 332 adults in five populations (the abstract cites the METS cohort),
TEE by doubly labelled water, activity by accelerometer.

**Key findings.** "total energy expenditure was positively correlated with physical activity, but
the relationship was markedly stronger over the lower range of physical activity. For subjects in
the upper range of physical activity, total energy expenditure plateaued". Full text: the plateau
covered roughly the top 30% of the sample; "physical activity accounts for only ~7 – 9% of the
variation in total energy expenditure after controlling for anthropometric variables and
population location."

**Follow-up: Careau 2021 Current Biology.** Careau V, Halsey LG, Pontzer H, [many authors],
Speakman JR, IAEA DLW database group. "Energy compensation and adiposity in humans." *Current
Biology* 2021;31(20):4659-4666.e2. doi:10.1016/j.cub.2021.08.016. PMID 34453886. PMCID PMC8551017.
**Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. n = 1,754. "energy compensation by a typical human averages 28%
due to reduced BEE; this suggests that only 72% of the extra calories we burn from additional
activity translates into extra calories burned that day." Compensation was greater in people with
more body fat; the direction of cause is unknown.

**Teaching point (my summary).** Exercise raises energy expenditure less than simple arithmetic
predicts, and at high habitual activity may add little to the daily total. This fits the trial
findings that exercise alone produces modest weight loss (Johnston 2014 found exercise's effect
larger at 12 months than 6), and it is not an argument against exercise, whose health benefits are
separate. Caution: both Pontzer 2016 and Careau 2021 are cross-sectional; the constrained model is
debated, and neither paper is an exercise trial.

---

### 6. Intermittent fasting and time-restricted eating

#### 6a. TREAT: Lowe 2020 JAMA Internal Medicine

**Citation.** Lowe DA, Wu N, Rohdin-Bibby L, Moore AH, Kelly N, Liu YE, Philip E, Vittinghoff E,
Heymsfield SB, Olgin JE, Shepherd JA, Weiss EJ. "Effects of Time-Restricted Eating on Weight Loss
and Other Metabolic Parameters in Women and Men With Overweight and Obesity: The TREAT Randomized
Clinical Trial." *JAMA Internal Medicine* 2020;180(11):1491-1499. doi:10.1001/jamainternmed.2020.4153.
PMID 32986097. PMCID PMC7522780.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** 12-week RCT, 116 adults, BMI 27 to 43, run through an app; 16:8 (eat noon to 8 pm)
versus three structured meals. No calorie target in either arm.

**Key numbers.** Between-group difference "-0.26 kg; 95% CI, -1.30 to 0.78; P = .63". In the
in-person subset, a between-group difference in appendicular lean mass index "(-0.16 kg/m2; 95% CI,
-0.27 to -0.05; P = .005)". Conclusion: "Time-restricted eating, in the absence of other
interventions, is not more effective in weight loss than eating throughout the day."

**Note (my summary).** The lean mass signal is from a subset of 50 and is a secondary outcome;
present it as a flag, not a finding. Short trial.

#### 6b. Liu 2022 NEJM: calorie restriction with or without TRE

**Citation.** Liu D, Huang Y, Huang C, Yang S, Wei X, Zhang P, Guo D, Lin J, Xu B, Li C, He H,
He J, Liu S, Shi L, Xue Y, Zhang H. "Calorie Restriction with or without Time-Restricted Eating in
Weight Loss." *New England Journal of Medicine* 2022;386(16):1495-1504. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2114833.
PMID 35443107. Not in PMC.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** 12-month RCT in China, 139 adults with obesity; both arms calorie-restricted (1500 to
1800 kcal men, 1200 to 1500 women); one arm also ate only 8 am to 4 pm. 118 (84.9%) completed.

**Key numbers.** "-8.0 kg (95% confidence interval [CI], -9.6 to -6.4) in the time-restriction
group and -6.3 kg (95% CI, -7.8 to -4.7) in the daily-calorie-restriction group". Difference
"-1.8 kg; 95% CI, -4.0 to 0.4; P = 0.11". Conclusion: TRE "was not more beneficial with regard to
reduction in body weight, body fat, or metabolic risk factors than daily calorie restriction."

**Teaching point (my summary).** This is the cleanest design for the question of whether meal timing
matters once calories are equal: it isolates timing. The answer at a year was no detectable
extra benefit, though the confidence interval does not rule out a modest one.

#### 6c. Network meta-analysis: Semnani-Azad 2025 BMJ

**Citation.** Semnani-Azad Z, Khan TA, Chiavaroli L, Chen V, Bhatt HA, Chen A, Chiang N,
Oguntala J, Kabisch S, Lau DC, Wharton S, Sharma AM, Harris L, Leiter LA, Hill JO, Hu FB, Lean ME,
Kahleová H, Rahelic D, Salas-Salvadó J, Kendall CW, Sievenpiper JL. "Intermittent fasting
strategies and their effects on body weight and other cardiometabolic risk factors: systematic
review and network meta-analysis of randomised clinical trials." *BMJ* 2025;389:e082007.
doi:10.1136/bmj-2024-082007. PMID 40533200. PMCID PMC12175170. A correction was published (BMJ
2025;390:r1737); I read it via PMC and it only corrects one coauthor's affiliation.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC; correction notice via PMC.

**Design.** 99 RCTs, 6582 adults; alternate day fasting, time restricted eating, whole day fasting,
continuous energy restriction, ad libitum. GRADE certainty assessed.

**Key numbers.** "All intermittent fasting and continuous energy restriction diet strategies
reduced body weight when compared with ad-libitum diet." Versus continuous restriction, only
alternate day fasting showed an advantage, "-1.29 kg (95% CI -1.99 to -0.59), moderate certainty of
evidence", and the authors call differences between fasting strategies "trivial". In trials of 24
weeks or longer (n = 17) only the comparisons against ad libitum eating showed a difference.
Conclusion: intermittent fasting has "similar benefits to continuous energy restriction for weight
loss and cardiometabolic risk factors. Longer duration trials are needed".

**NICE on intermittent fasting.** NG246 makes no practice recommendation on intermittent fasting
for adults; it points to a research recommendation (see section 9b).

**No Cochrane review read.** I did not find or read a Cochrane review on intermittent fasting for
weight loss in this session; the BMJ network meta-analysis above is the best synthesis I read.

---

### 7. Long-term maintenance

#### 7a. Anderson 2001 AJCN meta-analysis

**Citation.** Anderson JW, Konz EC, Frederich RC, Wood CL. "Long-term weight-loss maintenance: a
meta-analysis of US studies." *American Journal of Clinical Nutrition* 2001;74(5):579-584.
doi:10.1093/ajcn/74.5.579. PMID 11684524.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Meta-analysis of 29 US studies of structured weight-loss programmes with at least 2
years of follow-up.

**Key numbers.** At 4 to 5 years, weight-loss maintenance was "7.1 kg (95% CI: 6.1, 8.1 kg)" after
very-low-energy diets and "2.0 (1.5, 2.5) kg" after hypoenergetic balanced diets; percentage of
initial loss maintained "29% (25%, 33%) and 17% (13%, 22%)". Conclusion: "Five years after
completing structured weight-loss programs, the average individual maintained a weight loss of
>3 kg and a reduced weight of >3% of initial body weight."

**Note (my summary).** Complicates both "everyone regains everything" and "diets work": on average
most weight came back, but not all. Follow-up rates at 4 to 5 years were 55.4% and 79.7%, so the
people who were measured may differ from those who were not. Old (2001), US only.

#### 7b. Look AHEAD

**Cardiovascular outcomes.** Look AHEAD Research Group, Wing RR, et al. "Cardiovascular effects of
intensive lifestyle intervention in type 2 diabetes." *New England Journal of Medicine*
2013;369(2):145-154. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1212914. PMID 23796131. PMCID PMC3791615.
**Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. 5145 adults with type 2 diabetes and overweight or obesity,
16 US centres, stopped for futility at median 9.6 years. Weight loss "8.6% vs. 0.7% at 1 year; 6.0%
vs. 3.5% at study end". Primary cardiovascular outcome "hazard ratio in the intervention group,
0.95; 95% confidence interval, 0.83 to 1.09; P=0.51". Conclusion: the intervention "did not reduce
the rate of cardiovascular events".

**Eight-year weight.** Look AHEAD Research Group. "Eight-year weight losses with an intensive
lifestyle intervention: the look AHEAD study." *Obesity* 2014;22(1):5-13. doi:10.1002/oby.20662.
PMID 24307184. PMCID PMC3904491. **Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. At year 8, intervention versus
control lost "4.7% ± 0.2% and 2.1 ± 0.2% of initial weight"; "50.3% and 35.7%, respectively, lost
≥5%"; "26.9% and 17.2%, respectively, lost ≥10%". The authors call it "clinically meaningful weight
loss (≥5%) at year 8 in 50% of patients".

**Teaching point (my summary).** The largest, longest lifestyle trial shows that sustained modest
loss is achievable for about half of people with intensive support, and that this did not reduce
heart attacks and strokes in people with type 2 diabetes over about 10 years. Both facts belong in
an honest lesson.

#### 7c. The National Weight Control Registry (a selected sample)

**Citation.** Wing RR, Phelan S. "Long-term weight loss maintenance." *American Journal of Clinical
Nutrition* 2005;82(1 Suppl):222S-225S. doi:10.1093/ajcn/82.1.222S. PMID 16002825.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**What it says.** "approximately 20% of overweight individuals are successful at long-term weight
loss when defined as losing at least 10% of initial body weight and maintaining the loss for at
least 1 y." Registry members "have lost an average of 33 kg and maintained the loss for more than
5 y" and report "high levels of physical activity ( approximately 1 h/d), eating a low-calorie,
low-fat diet, eating breakfast regularly, self-monitoring weight, and maintaining a consistent
eating pattern".

**Caution (my summary).** The registry is self-selected successes who volunteered; it cannot tell
you how common success is or whether these habits *cause* maintenance rather than accompany it.
The "eating breakfast" habit, for example, has not held up as a cause in trials (I did not fetch
a breakfast trial in this session; flag for topic owner if the lesson makes that point). The 20%
figure is the authors' estimate from other research, not a registry finding.

---

### 8. GLP-1 and related drugs (brief; a medical matter)

**STEP 1.** Wilding JPH, Batterham RL, Calanna S, Davies M, Van Gaal LF, Lingvay I, McGowan BM,
Rosenstock J, Tran MTD, Wadden TA, Wharton S, Yokote K, Zeuthen N, Kushner RF, STEP 1 Study Group.
"Once-Weekly Semaglutide in Adults with Overweight or Obesity." *New England Journal of Medicine*
2021;384(11):989-1002. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2032183. PMID 33567185.
**Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. Double-blind RCT, 1961 adults without diabetes, 68 weeks,
semaglutide 2.4 mg weekly plus lifestyle versus placebo plus lifestyle. Weight change "-14.9% in
the semaglutide group as compared with -2.4% with placebo", difference "-12.4 percentage points
(95% confidence interval [CI], -13.4 to -11.5". Most common adverse events nausea and diarrhoea.
Funded by Novo Nordisk (the manufacturer).

**What happens on stopping.** Wilding JPH, Batterham RL, Davies M, et al. "Weight regain and
cardiometabolic effects after withdrawal of semaglutide: The STEP 1 trial extension." *Diabetes,
Obesity and Metabolism* 2022;24(8):1553-1564. doi:10.1111/dom.14725. PMID 35441470. PMCID
PMC9542252. **Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. 327 participants followed a year after stopping.
Semaglutide participants "regained 11.6 (SD: 7.7)" percentage points of the 17.3% lost; "One year
after withdrawal ... participants regained two-thirds of their prior weight loss". Authors'
conclusion: "Findings confirm the chronicity of obesity and suggest ongoing treatment is required".
All extension analyses were exploratory.

**Tirzepatide, for context.** Jastreboff AM, Aronne LJ, Ahmad NN, et al. "Tirzepatide Once Weekly
for the Treatment of Obesity." *NEJM* 2022;387(3):205-216. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2206038. PMID 35658024.
**Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. 2539 adults, 72 weeks; mean weight change "-20.9% (95% CI, -21.8
to -19.9) with 15-mg doses" versus "-3.1% (95% CI, -4.3 to -1.9) with placebo". Funded by Eli Lilly.

**Recent change.** The WHO fact sheet (section 9a) says: "In December 2025, WHO published the
guideline on the use of glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) therapies for the treatment of obesity in
adults." I did not read that guideline itself.

**Teaching point (my summary).** These drugs produce losses two to three times what diet trials
typically achieve, which fits the EBM picture of appetite regulated by the brain (the drugs act on
appetite). Weight returns when they stop. A course on nutrition should mention them as where the
field has moved and refer decisions to a doctor.

---

### 9. Official definitions and guidance

#### 9a. WHO obesity and overweight fact sheet

**Source.** World Health Organization. "Obesity and overweight" fact sheet.
https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/obesity-and-overweight
**Read:** page fetched by curl and read in full, 2026-09-24. The page carries the date
"8 December 2025", which I take to be its last update (it mentions the December 2025 GLP-1
guideline, consistent with that date).

**Verbatim definitions.**
- "Overweight is a condition of excessive fat deposits."
- "Obesity is classified by the World Health Organization (WHO) as a chronic, relapsing disease
  arising from complex interactions between genetics, neurobiology, eating behaviours, access to
  healthy diet, market forces, and the broader environment."
- "For adults: overweight is a BMI greater than or equal to 25; and obesity is a BMI greater than
  or equal to 30." (The page sets these as a list; I have joined the lines.)
- "The body mass index is a surrogate marker of fatness and additional measurements, such as the
  waist circumference, can help the diagnosis of obesity."
- Children 5 to 19: overweight is "BMI-for-age greater than 1 standard deviation above the WHO
  Growth Reference median"; obesity "greater than 2 standard deviations above the WHO Growth
  Reference median".

**Key figures (verbatim).** "In 2022, 2.5 billion adults (18 years and older) were overweight. Of
these, 890 million were living with obesity." "In 2022, 43% of adults aged 18 years and over were
overweight and 16% were living with obesity." "Worldwide adult obesity has more than doubled since
1990, and adolescent obesity has quadrupled." "In 2021, higher-than-optimal BMI caused an estimated
3.7 million deaths from noncommunicable diseases".

**On causes (verbatim).** "Overweight and obesity result from an imbalance of energy intake (diet)
and energy expenditure (physical activity)." And: "Dietary and physical activity patterns are
largely the result of environmental and societal conditions that greatly constrain personal choice.
Obesity is a societal rather than an individual responsibility".

**Note for neutrality (my summary).** The "societal rather than an individual responsibility"
sentence is a normative position of WHO, not a finding; a lesson should attribute it.

#### 9b. NICE NG246 "Overweight and obesity management" (England)

**Source.** National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. *Overweight and obesity management*.
NICE guideline NG246. Published 14 January 2025; last updated 08 January 2026 (both dates printed
on the page). https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng246
**Read:** chapters "Identifying and assessing overweight, obesity and central adiposity" (adult
sections in full), "Physical activity and diet" (dietary approaches section in full) and "Update
information", fetched by curl on 2026-09-24. It replaces CG189, NG7, PH53, PH47, PH46, PH42 and
CG43.

**Update history (from the page).** January 2026 amendment on waist-to-height ratio in children
aged 5 and over. "September 2026: Non-alcoholic fatty liver disease has been renamed as metabolic
dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease." (a terminology change this month).

**Adult BMI classes (verbatim, rec 1.9.10).** "healthy weight : BMI 18.5 kg/m 2 to 24.9 kg/m 2";
"overweight: BMI 25 kg/m 2 to 29.9 kg/m 2"; "obesity class 1: BMI 30 kg/m 2 to 34.9 kg/m 2";
"obesity class 2: BMI 35 kg/m 2 to 39.9 kg/m 2"; "obesity class 3: BMI 40 kg/m 2 or more." (The
"2" is a superscript flattened by my script: read kg/m².)

**Lower thresholds for some groups (rec 1.9.11, verbatim).** "People with a South Asian, Chinese,
other Asian, Middle Eastern, Black African or African–Caribbean background are prone to central
adiposity and their cardiometabolic risk occurs at lower BMI, so use lower BMI thresholds as a
practical measure of overweight and obesity: overweight: BMI 23 kg/m 2 to 27.4 kg/m 2 obesity:
BMI 27.5 kg/m 2 or above."

**BMI's limits (verbatim).** "Use BMI as a practical measure of overweight and obesity ...
Interpret it with caution because it is not a direct measure of central adiposity." Also caution
for "adults with high muscle mass" and "people aged 65 and over, taking into account ... the
possible protective effect of having a slightly higher BMI when older."

**Waist-to-height (rec 1.9.14 and 1.9.15).** "healthy central adiposity: waist-to-height ratio 0.4
to 0.49"; "increased central adiposity: waist-to-height ratio 0.5 to 0.59"; "high central
adiposity: waist-to-height ratio 0.6 or more". Public-facing message: "explain that they should
try and keep their waist to less than half their height".

**Dietary approaches (section 1.16, verbatim excerpts).**
- 1.16.3: "Ensure that dietary approaches for adults to support overweight and obesity management
  keep the person's total energy intake below their energy expenditure (also called an energy
  deficit or calorie deficit). This could be done by lowering specific macronutrient content (for
  example, low-fat or low-carbohydrate diets) or using other methods to limit overall energy
  intake."
- 1.16.2: "Encourage people to improve their dietary intake even if this does not result in them
  losing weight, because there can be other health benefits".
- 1.16.7: "Advise people not to use restrictive diets that are nutritionally unbalanced, because
  they are ineffective in the long term and can be harmful."
- 1.16.1 lists among the things to take into account "that in many cases weight regain may
  happen."
- Low-energy diets (800 to 1,200 kcal/day) and very-low-energy diets (under 800 kcal/day): only
  within specialist services, "last no more than 12 weeks", and "Do not use low-energy diets or
  very-low-energy diets as a long-term strategy to manage obesity."
- 1.16.12 asks clinicians to discuss "that weight regain is likely to happen, and if it does it is
  not because they or their healthcare professional have 'failed'".
- Intermittent fasting: "See the NICE recommendation for research about intermittent fasting in
  adults." (No practice recommendation.)

#### 9c. The 2025 Lancet Commission on clinical obesity (a recent shift in definitions)

**Citation.** Rubino F, Cummings DE, Eckel RH, [55 further authors], Mingrone G. "Definition and
diagnostic criteria of clinical obesity." *Lancet Diabetes & Endocrinology* 2025;13(3):221-262.
doi:10.1016/S2213-8587(24)00316-4. PMID 39824205. PMCID PMC11870235.

**What I read.** The executive summary (the article's abstract block) via PMC XML. Europe PMC
returned no abstract text for it.

**Key points (verbatim).** "We recommend that BMI should be used only as a surrogate measure of
health risk at a population level, for epidemiological studies, or for screening purposes, rather
than as an individual measure of health." It defines "clinical obesity as a chronic, systemic
illness characterised by alterations in the function of tissues, organs, the entire individual, or
a combination thereof, due to excess adiposity" and "preclinical obesity as a state of excess
adiposity with preserved function of other tissues and organs". Recommendations were agreed at
"(grade of agreement 90–100%)" and endorsed by "76 organisations worldwide".

**Note (my summary).** This is a consensus commission, not a guideline body; WHO and NICE still
use BMI cut-offs as quoted above. It is the most important recent change in how obesity is being
defined and a lesson on BMI should mention it.

---

### 10. The "3500 kcal per pound" rule and why it is wrong

#### 10a. Hall 2011 Lancet

**Citation.** Hall KD, Sacks G, Chandramohan D, Chow CC, Wang YC, Gortmaker SL, Swinburn BA.
"Quantification of the effect of energy imbalance on bodyweight." *Lancet* 2011;378(9793):826-837.
doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(11)60812-X. PMID 21872751. PMCID PMC3880593.

**What I read.** Abstract via Europe PMC.

**Key points (abstract).** "the bodyweight response to a change of energy intake is slow, with half
times of about 1 year." "adults with greater adiposity have a larger expected weight loss for the
same change of energy intake". The US epidemic corresponds to "A small persistent average daily
energy imbalance gap between intake and expenditure of about 30 kJ per day", but the
"maintenance energy gap" (the rise in intake needed to sustain the higher weight) "has amounted to
about 0·9 MJ per day". (30 kJ is about 7 kcal; 0.9 MJ is about 215 kcal: my conversions.)

**The rule of thumb that comes from this work (my summary, not quoted).** Hall's group has
popularised a rough approximation that each 10 kcal/day sustained change in intake leads eventually
to about 1 lb change, with half reached in about a year. I did not read a source stating that
figure in this session, so the writer should not use it unless it is sourced separately.

#### 10b. Thomas 2013 and Hall and Chow 2013

**Thomas DM, Martin CK, Lettieri S, Bredlau C, Kaiser K, Church T, Bouchard C, Heymsfield SB.** "Can
a weight loss of one pound a week be achieved with a 3500-kcal deficit? Commentary on a commonly
accepted rule." *International Journal of Obesity* 2013;37(12):1611-1613. doi:10.1038/ijo.2013.51.
PMID 23628852. PMCID PMC4024447. **Read:** abstract via Europe PMC. "the model commonly referred
to as the 3500-kcal rule grossly overestimates actual weight loss", tested against "seven weight
loss experiments conducted in confinement". They offer downloadable dynamic-model tools
(pbrc.edu).

**Hall KD, Chow CC.** "Why is the 3500 kcal per pound weight loss rule wrong?" *International Journal
of Obesity* 2013;37(12):1614. doi:10.1038/ijo.2013.112. PMID 23774459. PMCID PMC3859816.
**Read:** full text via PMC (a one-page letter; no abstract exists). The best short statement of
the error:
- "The most serious error of the 3500-kcal rule is its failure to account for dynamic changes in
  energy balance that occur during an intervention."
- "the reader may be led to the erroneous conclusion that the deficiency of the 3500-kcal rule is
  the numerical value ‘3500’."
- "The 3500-kcal rule was motivated by calculating that a pound of adipose tissue stores
  approximately 3500 kcal. A more accurate accounting of body composition changes demonstrated
  that this value is appropriate for modest weight changes in overweight and obese people, but is
  an overestimate in others. However, using a ‘corrected’ numerical value for the energy content
  of lost tissue does not repair the 3500-kcal rule without also accounting for the ΔEB dynamics."

**Teaching point (my summary).** The 3500 figure is roughly right for the energy in a pound of lost
tissue. What is wrong is assuming a daily deficit stays constant: as you lose weight, you burn less
(a smaller body costs less to run, plus some adaptation), so the deficit shrinks and weight loss
slows toward a plateau. The rule predicts steady loss forever; bodies level off. Also note that the
Hall/Guo/Speakman critique in 2d uses "an assumed energy equivalent of 7700 kcal per kg", which is
the same number in metric (3500 kcal/lb ≈ 7700 kcal/kg), consistent with Hall and Chow's point that
the number itself is not the problem.

---

### Things that contradict common belief (for lesson hooks)

1. Metabolic rate, adjusted for body size, does not decline between 20 and 60 (Pontzer 2021).
2. Low-fat versus low-carb makes little difference to weight at a year when both emphasise food
   quality (DIETFITS; Johnston 2014). Adherence predicts loss better than diet type (Dansinger).
3. In the Biggest Loser follow-up, metabolic slowing did not predict who regained; the people who
   kept most weight off had the *most* slowing. And most participants still kept at least 10% off.
4. The 3500 kcal figure is not the error in the 3500 kcal rule; the static deficit is.
5. Time-restricted eating did not beat plain calorie restriction when calories were matched (Liu
   2022).
6. Look AHEAD: sustained modest weight loss did not reduce cardiovascular events in type 2
   diabetes.
7. In Hall 2019, people did not rate the ultra-processed meals as more pleasant, yet ate 500 kcal a
   day more of them.
8. More exercise raises daily energy use less than arithmetic predicts (Pontzer 2016, Careau 2021).

### Gaps and things I did not do

- No Cochrane review on intermittent fasting was found or read.
- Hall 2022 EBM and Hall 2021 Nature Medicine: abstracts only.
- No source read for the 10 kcal/day per pound rule of thumb.
- I did not read the WHO December 2025 GLP-1 guideline, only the fact sheet's sentence about it.
- NICE's rationale and evidence review F (effectiveness of different diets) were not read.
- USDA/HHS Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030 on weight were not checked (outside this topic's list; the
  CIM paper quotes the 2020-2025 edition).

---

## Appendix: complete abstracts, verbatim

Generated by script from Europe PMC REST API JSON (resultType=core), 2026-09-24. HTML tags
stripped; `<sup>x</sup>` rendered as `^x`. Section headings that Europe PMC encodes as tags (for
example "Objective", "Results") appear inline. Where a paper has no abstract (Hall and Chow 2013;
Hall, Guo and Speakman 2019; Rubino 2025) it is marked, and the Rubino executive summary is given
from PMC instead.


#### A1. The carbohydrate-insulin model: a physiological perspective on the obesity pandemic.

Ludwig DS, Aronne LJ, Astrup A, de Cabo R, Cantley LC, Friedman MI, Heymsfield SB, Johnson JD, King JC, Krauss RM, Lieberman DE, Taubes G, Volek JS, Westman EC, Willett WC, Yancy WS, Ebbeling CB.. The American journal of clinical nutrition 2021 vol 114 issue 6 pages 1873-1885. DOI 10.1093/ajcn/nqab270 PMID: 34515299 PMCID: PMC8634575

> According to a commonly held view, the obesity pandemic is caused by overconsumption of modern, highly palatable, energy-dense processed foods, exacerbated by a sedentary lifestyle. However, obesity rates remain at historic highs, despite a persistent focus on eating less and moving more, as guided by the energy balance model (EBM). This public health failure may arise from a fundamental limitation of the EBM itself. Conceptualizing obesity as a disorder of energy balance restates a principle of physics without considering the biological mechanisms that promote weight gain. An alternative paradigm, the carbohydrate-insulin model (CIM), proposes a reversal of causal direction. According to the CIM, increasing fat deposition in the body-resulting from the hormonal responses to a high-glycemic-load diet-drives positive energy balance. The CIM provides a conceptual framework with testable hypotheses for how various modifiable factors influence energy balance and fat storage. Rigorous research is needed to compare the validity of these 2 models, which have substantially different implications for obesity management, and to generate new models that best encompass the evidence.

#### A2. The energy balance model of obesity: beyond calories in, calories out.

Hall KD, Farooqi IS, Friedman JM, Klein S, Loos RJF, Mangelsdorf DJ, O'Rahilly S, Ravussin E, Redman LM, Ryan DH, Speakman JR, Tobias DK.. The American journal of clinical nutrition 2022 vol 115 issue 5 pages 1243-1254. DOI 10.1093/ajcn/nqac031 PMID: 35134825 PMCID: PMC9071483

> A recent Perspective article described the "carbohydrate-insulin model (CIM)" of obesity, asserting that it "better reflects knowledge on the biology of weight control" as compared with what was described as the "dominant energy balance model (EBM)," which fails to consider "biological mechanisms that promote weight gain." Unfortunately, the Perspective conflated and confused the principle of energy balance, a law of physics that is agnostic as to obesity mechanisms, with the EBM as a theoretical model of obesity that is firmly based on biology. In doing so, the authors presented a false choice between the CIM and a caricature of the EBM that does not reflect modern obesity science. Here, we present a more accurate description of the EBM where the brain is the primary organ responsible for body weight regulation operating mainly below our conscious awareness via complex endocrine, metabolic, and nervous system signals to control food intake in response to the body's dynamic energy needs as well as environmental influences. We also describe the recent history of the CIM and show how the latest "most comprehensive formulation" abandons a formerly central feature that required fat accumulation in adipose tissue to be the primary driver of positive energy balance. As such, the new CIM can be considered a special case of the more comprehensive EBM but with a narrower focus on diets high in glycemic load as the primary factor responsible for common obesity. We review data from a wide variety of studies that address the validity of each model and demonstrate that the EBM is a more robust theory of obesity than the CIM.

#### A3. Effect of a plant-based, low-fat diet versus an animal-based, ketogenic diet on ad libitum energy intake.

Hall KD, Guo J, Courville AB, Boring J, Brychta R, Chen KY, Darcey V, Forde CG, Gharib AM, Gallagher I, Howard R, Joseph PV, Milley L, Ouwerkerk R, Raisinger K, Rozga I, Schick A, Stagliano M, Torres S, Walter M, Walter P, Yang S, Chung ST.. Nature medicine 2021 vol 27 issue 2 pages 344-353. DOI 10.1038/s41591-020-01209-1 PMID: 33479499 PMCID: None

> The carbohydrate-insulin model of obesity posits that high-carbohydrate diets lead to excess insulin secretion, thereby promoting fat accumulation and increasing energy intake. Thus, low-carbohydrate diets are predicted to reduce ad libitum energy intake as compared to low-fat, high-carbohydrate diets. To test this hypothesis, 20 adults aged 29.9 ± 1.4 (mean ± s.e.m.) years with body mass index of 27.8 ± 1.3 kg m^-2 were admitted as inpatients to the National Institutes of Health Clinical Center and randomized to consume ad libitum either a minimally processed, plant-based, low-fat diet (10.3% fat, 75.2% carbohydrate) with high glycemic load (85 g 1,000 kcal^-1) or a minimally processed, animal-based, ketogenic, low-carbohydrate diet (75.8% fat, 10.0% carbohydrate) with low glycemic load (6 g 1,000 kcal^-1) for 2 weeks followed immediately by the alternate diet for 2 weeks. One participant withdrew due to hypoglycemia during the low-carbohydrate diet. The primary outcomes compared mean daily ad libitum energy intake between each 2-week diet period as well as between the final week of each diet. We found that the low-fat diet led to 689 ± 73 kcal d^-1 less energy intake than the low-carbohydrate diet over 2 weeks (P < 0.0001) and 544 ± 68 kcal d^-1 less over the final week (P < 0.0001). Therefore, the predictions of the carbohydrate-insulin model were inconsistent with our observations. This study was registered on ClinicalTrials.gov as NCT03878108 .

#### A4. Effects of a low carbohydrate diet on energy expenditure during weight loss maintenance: randomized trial.

Ebbeling CB, Feldman HA, Klein GL, Wong JMW, Bielak L, Steltz SK, Luoto PK, Wolfe RR, Wong WW, Ludwig DS.. BMJ (Clinical research ed.) 2018 vol 363 issue None pages k4583. DOI 10.1136/bmj.k4583 PMID: 30429127 PMCID: PMC6233655

> Objective To determine the effects of diets varying in carbohydrate to fat ratio on total energy expenditure. Design Randomized trial. Setting Multicenter collaboration at US two sites, August 2014 to May 2017. Participants 164 adults aged 18-65 years with a body mass index of 25 or more. Interventions After 12% (within 2%) weight loss on a run-in diet, participants were randomly assigned to one of three test diets according to carbohydrate content (high, 60%, n=54; moderate, 40%, n=53; or low, 20%, n=57) for 20 weeks. Test diets were controlled for protein and were energy adjusted to maintain weight loss within 2 kg. To test for effect modification predicted by the carbohydrate-insulin model, the sample was divided into thirds of pre-weight loss insulin secretion (insulin concentration 30 minutes after oral glucose). Main outcome measures The primary outcome was total energy expenditure, measured with doubly labeled water, by intention-to-treat analysis. Per protocol analysis included participants who maintained target weight loss, potentially providing a more precise effect estimate. Secondary outcomes were resting energy expenditure, measures of physical activity, and levels of the metabolic hormones leptin and ghrelin. Results Total energy expenditure differed by diet in the intention-to-treat analysis (n=162, P=0.002), with a linear trend of 52 kcal/d (95% confidence interval 23 to 82) for every 10% decrease in the contribution of carbohydrate to total energy intake (1 kcal=4.18 kJ=0.00418 MJ). Change in total energy expenditure was 91 kcal/d (95% confidence interval -29 to 210) greater in participants assigned to the moderate carbohydrate diet and 209 kcal/d (91 to 326) greater in those assigned to the low carbohydrate diet compared with the high carbohydrate diet. In the per protocol analysis (n=120, P<0.001), the respective differences were 131 kcal/d (-6 to 267) and 278 kcal/d (144 to 411). Among participants in the highest third of pre-weight loss insulin secretion, the difference between the low and high carbohydrate diet was 308 kcal/d in the intention-to-treat analysis and 478 kcal/d in the per protocol analysis (P<0.004). Ghrelin was significantly lower in participants assigned to the low carbohydrate diet compared with those assigned to the high carbohydrate diet (both analyses). Leptin was also significantly lower in participants assigned to the low carbohydrate diet (per protocol). Conclusions Consistent with the carbohydrate-insulin model, lowering dietary carbohydrate increased energy expenditure during weight loss maintenance. This metabolic effect may improve the success of obesity treatment, especially among those with high insulin secretion. Trial registration ClinicalTrials.gov NCT02068885.

#### A5. Effect of Low-Fat vs Low-Carbohydrate Diet on 12-Month Weight Loss in Overweight Adults and the Association With Genotype Pattern or Insulin Secretion: The DIETFITS Randomized Clinical Trial.

Gardner CD, Trepanowski JF, Del Gobbo LC, Hauser ME, Rigdon J, Ioannidis JPA, Desai M, King AC.. JAMA 2018 vol 319 issue 7 pages 667-679. DOI 10.1001/jama.2018.0245 PMID: 29466592 PMCID: PMC5839290

> Importance Dietary modification remains key to successful weight loss. Yet, no one dietary strategy is consistently superior to others for the general population. Previous research suggests genotype or insulin-glucose dynamics may modify the effects of diets. Objective To determine the effect of a healthy low-fat (HLF) diet vs a healthy low-carbohydrate (HLC) diet on weight change and if genotype pattern or insulin secretion are related to the dietary effects on weight loss. Design, setting, and participants The Diet Intervention Examining The Factors Interacting with Treatment Success (DIETFITS) randomized clinical trial included 609 adults aged 18 to 50 years without diabetes with a body mass index between 28 and 40. The trial enrollment was from January 29, 2013, through April 14, 2015; the date of final follow-up was May 16, 2016. Participants were randomized to the 12-month HLF or HLC diet. The study also tested whether 3 single-nucleotide polymorphism multilocus genotype responsiveness patterns or insulin secretion (INS-30; blood concentration of insulin 30 minutes after a glucose challenge) were associated with weight loss. Interventions Health educators delivered the behavior modification intervention to HLF (n = 305) and HLC (n = 304) participants via 22 diet-specific small group sessions administered over 12 months. The sessions focused on ways to achieve the lowest fat or carbohydrate intake that could be maintained long-term and emphasized diet quality. Main outcomes and measures Primary outcome was 12-month weight change and determination of whether there were significant interactions among diet type and genotype pattern, diet and insulin secretion, and diet and weight loss. Results Among 609 participants randomized (mean age, 40 [SD, 7] years; 57% women; mean body mass index, 33 [SD, 3]; 244 [40%] had a low-fat genotype; 180 [30%] had a low-carbohydrate genotype; mean baseline INS-30, 93 μIU/mL), 481 (79%) completed the trial. In the HLF vs HLC diets, respectively, the mean 12-month macronutrient distributions were 48% vs 30% for carbohydrates, 29% vs 45% for fat, and 21% vs 23% for protein. Weight change at 12 months was -5.3 kg for the HLF diet vs -6.0 kg for the HLC diet (mean between-group difference, 0.7 kg [95% CI, -0.2 to 1.6 kg]). There was no significant diet-genotype pattern interaction (P = .20) or diet-insulin secretion (INS-30) interaction (P = .47) with 12-month weight loss. There were 18 adverse events or serious adverse events that were evenly distributed across the 2 diet groups. Conclusions and relevance In this 12-month weight loss diet study, there was no significant difference in weight change between a healthy low-fat diet vs a healthy low-carbohydrate diet, and neither genotype pattern nor baseline insulin secretion was associated with the dietary effects on weight loss. In the context of these 2 common weight loss diet approaches, neither of the 2 hypothesized predisposing factors was helpful in identifying which diet was better for whom. Trial registration clinicaltrials.gov Identifier: NCT01826591.

#### A6. Comparison of weight loss among named diet programs in overweight and obese adults: a meta-analysis.

Johnston BC, Kanters S, Bandayrel K, Wu P, Naji F, Siemieniuk RA, Ball GD, Busse JW, Thorlund K, Guyatt G, Jansen JP, Mills EJ.. JAMA 2014 vol 312 issue 9 pages 923-933. DOI 10.1001/jama.2014.10397 PMID: 25182101 PMCID: None

> Importance Many claims have been made regarding the superiority of one diet or another for inducing weight loss. Which diet is best remains unclear. Objective To determine weight loss outcomes for popular diets based on diet class (macronutrient composition) and named diet. Data sources Search of 6 electronic databases: AMED, CDSR, CENTRAL, CINAHL, EMBASE, and MEDLINE from inception of each database to April 2014. Study selection Overweight or obese adults (body mass index ≥25) randomized to a popular self-administered named diet and reporting weight or body mass index data at 3-month follow-up or longer. Data extraction and synthesis Two reviewers independently extracted data on populations, interventions, outcomes, risk of bias, and quality of evidence. A Bayesian framework was used to perform a series of random-effects network meta-analyses with meta-regression to estimate the relative effectiveness of diet classes and programs for change in weight and body mass index from baseline. Our analyses adjusted for behavioral support and exercise. Main outcomes and measures Weight loss and body mass index at 6- and 12-month follow-up (±3 months for both periods). Results Among 59 eligible articles reporting 48 unique randomized trials (including 7286 individuals) and compared with no diet, the largest weight loss was associated with low-carbohydrate diets (8.73 kg [95% credible interval {CI}, 7.27 to 10.20 kg] at 6-month follow-up and 7.25 kg [95% CI, 5.33 to 9.25 kg] at 12-month follow-up) and low-fat diets (7.99 kg [95% CI, 6.01 to 9.92 kg] at 6-month follow-up and 7.27 kg [95% CI, 5.26 to 9.34 kg] at 12-month follow-up). Weight loss differences between individual diets were minimal. For example, the Atkins diet resulted in a 1.71 kg greater weight loss than the Zone diet at 6-month follow-up. Between 6- and 12-month follow-up, the influence of behavioral support (3.23 kg [95% CI, 2.23 to 4.23 kg] at 6-month follow-up vs 1.08 kg [95% CI, -1.82 to 3.96 kg] at 12-month follow-up) and exercise (0.64 kg [95% CI, -0.35 to 1.66 kg] vs 2.13 kg [95% CI, 0.43 to 3.85 kg], respectively) on weight loss differed. Conclusions and relevance Significant weight loss was observed with any low-carbohydrate or low-fat diet. Weight loss differences between individual named diets were small. This supports the practice of recommending any diet that a patient will adhere to in order to lose weight.

#### A7. Comparison of the Atkins, Ornish, Weight Watchers, and Zone diets for weight loss and heart disease risk reduction: a randomized trial.

Dansinger ML, Gleason JA, Griffith JL, Selker HP, Schaefer EJ.. JAMA 2005 vol 293 issue 1 pages 43-53. DOI 10.1001/jama.293.1.43 PMID: 15632335 PMCID: None

> Context The scarcity of data addressing the health effects of popular diets is an important public health concern, especially since patients and physicians are interested in using popular diets as individualized eating strategies for disease prevention. Objective To assess adherence rates and the effectiveness of 4 popular diets (Atkins, Zone, Weight Watchers, and Ornish) for weight loss and cardiac risk factor reduction. Design, setting, and participants A single-center randomized trial at an academic medical center in Boston, Mass, of overweight or obese (body mass index: mean, 35; range, 27-42) adults aged 22 to 72 years with known hypertension, dyslipidemia, or fasting hyperglycemia. Participants were enrolled starting July 18, 2000, and randomized to 4 popular diet groups until January 24, 2002. Intervention A total of 160 participants were randomly assigned to either Atkins (carbohydrate restriction, n=40), Zone (macronutrient balance, n=40), Weight Watchers (calorie restriction, n=40), or Ornish (fat restriction, n=40) diet groups. After 2 months of maximum effort, participants selected their own levels of dietary adherence. Main outcome measures One-year changes in baseline weight and cardiac risk factors, and self-selected dietary adherence rates per self-report. Results Assuming no change from baseline for participants who discontinued the study, mean (SD) weight loss at 1 year was 2.1 (4.8) kg for Atkins (21 [53%] of 40 participants completed, P = .009), 3.2 (6.0) kg for Zone (26 [65%] of 40 completed, P = .002), 3.0 (4.9) kg for Weight Watchers (26 [65%] of 40 completed, P < .001), and 3.3 (7.3) kg for Ornish (20 [50%] of 40 completed, P = .007). Greater effects were observed in study completers. Each diet significantly reduced the low-density lipoprotein/high-density lipoprotein (HDL) cholesterol ratio by approximately 10% (all P<.05), with no significant effects on blood pressure or glucose at 1 year. Amount of weight loss was associated with self-reported dietary adherence level (r = 0.60; P<.001) but not with diet type (r = 0.07; P = .40). For each diet, decreasing levels of total/HDL cholesterol, C-reactive protein, and insulin were significantly associated with weight loss (mean r = 0.36, 0.37, and 0.39, respectively) with no significant difference between diets (P = .48, P = .57, P = .31, respectively). Conclusions Each popular diet modestly reduced body weight and several cardiac risk factors at 1 year. Overall dietary adherence rates were low, although increased adherence was associated with greater weight loss and cardiac risk factor reductions for each diet group.

#### A8. Persistent metabolic adaptation 6 years after "The Biggest Loser" competition.

Fothergill E, Guo J, Howard L, Kerns JC, Knuth ND, Brychta R, Chen KY, Skarulis MC, Walter M, Walter PJ, Hall KD.. Obesity (Silver Spring, Md.) 2016 vol 24 issue 8 pages 1612-1619. DOI 10.1002/oby.21538 PMID: 27136388 PMCID: PMC4989512

> Objective To measure long-term changes in resting metabolic rate (RMR) and body composition in participants of "The Biggest Loser" competition. Methods Body composition was measured by dual energy X-ray absorptiometry, and RMR was determined by indirect calorimetry at baseline, at the end of the 30-week competition and 6 years later. Metabolic adaptation was defined as the residual RMR after adjusting for changes in body composition and age. Results Of the 16 "Biggest Loser" competitors originally investigated, 14 participated in this follow-up study. Weight loss at the end of the competition was (mean ± SD) 58.3 ± 24.9 kg (P < 0.0001), and RMR decreased by 610 ± 483 kcal/day (P = 0.0004). After 6 years, 41.0 ± 31.3 kg of the lost weight was regained (P = 0.0002), while RMR was 704 ± 427 kcal/day below baseline (P < 0.0001) and metabolic adaptation was -499 ± 207 kcal/day (P < 0.0001). Weight regain was not significantly correlated with metabolic adaptation at the competition's end (r = -0.1, P = 0.75), but those subjects maintaining greater weight loss at 6 years also experienced greater concurrent metabolic slowing (r = 0.59, P = 0.025). Conclusions Metabolic adaptation persists over time and is likely a proportional, but incomplete, response to contemporaneous efforts to reduce body weight.

#### A9. Daily energy expenditure through the human life course.

Pontzer H, Yamada Y, Sagayama H, Ainslie PN, Andersen LF, Anderson LJ, Arab L, Baddou I, Bedu-Addo K, Blaak EE, Blanc S, Bonomi AG, Bouten CVC, Bovet P, Buchowski MS, Butte NF, Camps SG, Close GL, Cooper JA, Cooper R, Das SK, Dugas LR, Ekelund U, Entringer S, Forrester T, Fudge BW, Goris AH, et al.. Science (New York, N.Y.) 2021 vol 373 issue 6556 pages 808-812. DOI 10.1126/science.abe5017 PMID: 34385400 PMCID: PMC8370708

> Total daily energy expenditure ("total expenditure") reflects daily energy needs and is a critical variable in human health and physiology, but its trajectory over the life course is poorly studied. We analyzed a large, diverse database of total expenditure measured by the doubly labeled water method for males and females aged 8 days to 95 years. Total expenditure increased with fat-free mass in a power-law manner, with four distinct life stages. Fat-free mass-adjusted expenditure accelerates rapidly in neonates to ~50% above adult values at ~1 year; declines slowly to adult levels by ~20 years; remains stable in adulthood (20 to 60 years), even during pregnancy; then declines in older adults. These changes shed light on human development and aging and should help shape nutrition and health strategies across the life span.

#### A10. Constrained Total Energy Expenditure and Metabolic Adaptation to Physical Activity in Adult Humans.

Pontzer H, Durazo-Arvizu R, Dugas LR, Plange-Rhule J, Bovet P, Forrester TE, Lambert EV, Cooper RS, Schoeller DA, Luke A.. Current biology : CB 2016 vol 26 issue 3 pages 410-417. DOI 10.1016/j.cub.2015.12.046 PMID: 26832439 PMCID: PMC4803033

> Current obesity prevention strategies recommend increasing daily physical activity, assuming that increased activity will lead to corresponding increases in total energy expenditure and prevent or reverse energy imbalance and weight gain [1-3]. Such Additive total energy expenditure models are supported by exercise intervention and accelerometry studies reporting positive correlations between physical activity and total energy expenditure [4] but are challenged by ecological studies in humans and other species showing that more active populations do not have higher total energy expenditure [5-8]. Here we tested a Constrained total energy expenditure model, in which total energy expenditure increases with physical activity at low activity levels but plateaus at higher activity levels as the body adapts to maintain total energy expenditure within a narrow range. We compared total energy expenditure, measured using doubly labeled water, against physical activity, measured using accelerometry, for a large (n = 332) sample of adults living in five populations [9]. After adjusting for body size and composition, total energy expenditure was positively correlated with physical activity, but the relationship was markedly stronger over the lower range of physical activity. For subjects in the upper range of physical activity, total energy expenditure plateaued, supporting a Constrained total energy expenditure model. Body fat percentage and activity intensity appear to modulate the metabolic response to physical activity. Models of energy balance employed in public health [1-3] should be revised to better reflect the constrained nature of total energy expenditure and the complex effects of physical activity on metabolic physiology.

#### A11. Ultra-Processed Diets Cause Excess Calorie Intake and Weight Gain: An Inpatient Randomized Controlled Trial of Ad Libitum Food Intake.

Hall KD, Ayuketah A, Brychta R, Cai H, Cassimatis T, Chen KY, Chung ST, Costa E, Courville A, Darcey V, Fletcher LA, Forde CG, Gharib AM, Guo J, Howard R, Joseph PV, McGehee S, Ouwerkerk R, Raisinger K, Rozga I, Stagliano M, Walter M, Walter PJ, Yang S, Zhou M.. Cell metabolism 2019 vol 30 issue 1 pages 67-77.e3. DOI 10.1016/j.cmet.2019.05.008 PMID: 31105044 PMCID: PMC7946062
