Kromhout D, Keys A, Aravanis C, Buzina R, Fidanza F, Giampaoli S, Jansen A, Menotti A, Nedeljkovic S, Pekkarinen M. Food consumption patterns in the 1960s in seven countries. The American journal of clinical nutrition. 1989;49(5):889-894. doi:10.1093/ajcn/49.5.889. PMID 2718924.

At the end of the 1950s the Seven Countries Study was designed to investigate the relations between diet and cardiovascular diseases. Sixteen cohorts were selected in Finland, Greece, Italy, Japan, The Netherlands, United States, and Yugoslavia. During the 1960s food consumption data were collected from random samples of these cohorts by use of the record method. In Finland the intake of milk, potatoes, edible fats, and sugar products was very high. A similar but lower intake pattern was observed in The Netherlands. Fruit, meat, and pastry consumption was high in the United States; cereal and alcoholic drink consumption was high in Italy; and bread consumption high in Yugoslavians except for those in Belgrade. In Greece the intake of olive oil and fruit was high and the Japanese cohorts were characterized by a high consumption of fish, rice, and soy products. These differences in food consumption patterns have lessened during the past 25 y.


### A. Effect of Interventions Aimed at Reducing or Modifying Saturated Fat Intake on Cholesterol, Mortality, and Major Cardiovascular Events : A Risk Stratified Systematic Review of Randomized Trials.

Steen JP, Klatt KC, Chang Y, Guyatt GH, Zhu H, Swierz MJ, Storman D, Sun M, Zhao Y, Ge L, Thabane L, Ghosh NR, Karam G, Alonso-Coello P, Bala MM, Johnston BC. Effect of Interventions Aimed at Reducing or Modifying Saturated Fat Intake on Cholesterol, Mortality, and Major Cardiovascular Events : A Risk Stratified Systematic Review of Randomized Trials. Annals of internal medicine. 2026;179(2):242-255. doi:10.7326/annals-25-02229. PMID 41397264.

**Background.** Debates about optimal saturated fat advice continue.

**Purpose.** To systematically summarize randomized trial data on reducing or modifying saturated fat intake on cholesterol, mortality, and major cardiovascular events.

**Data sources.** MEDLINE, Embase, and Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials from inception to July 2025.

**Study selection.** Eligible trials enrolled adults with or without cardiovascular disease and studied the effect of reducing or modifying saturated fat intake.

**Data extraction.** Standard Cochrane methods.

**Data synthesis.** There were 17 eligible trials (66 337 participants). Risk stratified evidence provides low to moderate certainty that reducing saturated fat intake may result in a reduction in all-cause mortality (risk ratio [RR], 0.96 [95% CI, 0.88 to 1.06]), cardiovascular mortality (RR, 0.93 [CI, 0.77 to 1.11]), nonfatal myocardial infarction (MI) (RR, 0.86 [CI, 0.70 to 1.06]), and fatal and nonfatal stroke (RR, 0.83 [CI, 0.58 to 1.19]). For persons at low baseline cardiovascular risk, absolute reductions were below our thresholds of importance (5 and 10 per 1000 persons followed over 5 years for fatal and nonfatal outcomes, respectively); for those at high risk, the benefits were above our thresholds, suggesting there may be important absolute reductions. The effects were more pronounced when replacing saturated fat with polyunsaturated fat for nonfatal MI (RR, 0.75 [CI, 0.58 to 0.99]; P for interaction = 0.05; moderate credibility of subgroup effect based on Instrument to assess the Credibility of Effect Modification Analyses assessments).

**Limitations.** Data were limited on the replacement of saturated fat with monounsaturated fat or protein. Trials varied considerably in their efficacy in reducing saturated fat intake and in their replacement macronutrients and concomitant dietary interventions, and new trials are needed to clarify uncertainty.

**Conclusion.** For persons at low cardiovascular risk, reducing or modifying saturated fat intake has little or no benefit over a period of 5 years. Among persons at high cardiovascular risk, low- to moderate-certainty evidence was found for important reductions in mortality and major cardiovascular events, particularly for MI, with respect to replacing saturated fat with polyunsaturated fat.

**Primary funding source.** None. (PROSPERO: CRD42023387377).


### A. Saturated Fat Restriction for Cardiovascular Disease Prevention: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.

Yamada S, Shirai T, Inaba S, Inoue G, Torigoe M, Fukuyama N. Saturated Fat Restriction for Cardiovascular Disease Prevention: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials. JMA journal. 2025;8(2):395-407. doi:10.31662/jmaj.2024-0324. PMID 40416032.

**Background.** The recommendation to limit dietary saturated fat intake is primarily drawn from observational studies rather than randomized controlled trials of cardiovascular disease prevention. Thus, we aimed to investigate the efficacy of saturated fat reduction in preventing mortality and cardiovascular diseases.

**Methods.** In this systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials, Cochrane CENTRAL, PubMed, and Ichu-shi databases were searched for articles up to April 2023. Randomized controlled trials on saturated fat reduction to prevent cardiovascular diseases were selected. Cardiovascular and all-cause mortality and cardiovascular outcomes were evaluated. Changes in electrocardiography or coronary angiography findings were excluded because they could be evaluated arbitrarily. Two or more reviewers independently extracted and assessed the data. A random-effects meta-analysis was performed.

**Results.** Nine eligible trials with 13,532 participants were identified (2 were primary and 7 were secondary prevention studies). No significant differences in cardiovascular mortality (relative risk [RR] = 0.94, 95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.75-1.19), all-cause mortality (RR = 1.01, 95% CI: 0.89-1.14), myocardial infarction (RR = 0.85, 95% CI: 0.71-1.02), and coronary artery events (RR = 0.85, 95% CI: 0.65-1.11) were observed between the intervention and control groups. However, owing to limited reported cases, the impact of stroke could not be evaluated.

**Conclusions.** The findings indicate that a reduction in saturated fats cannot be recommended at present to prevent cardiovascular diseases and mortality. Clinical trials are needed to evaluate the effects of saturated fat reduction under the best possible medical care, including statin administration.

**Systematic review registration number.** This systematic review and meta-analysis was registered with the International Prospective Register of Systematic Reviews (CRD42023428498).


### A. Fatty Acids, Their Food Sources, and Cardiovascular Disease: Current Evidence and Controversies Surrounding the 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans.

Sun Q, Hu FB. Fatty Acids, Their Food Sources, and Cardiovascular Disease: Current Evidence and Controversies Surrounding the 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans. The Journal of nutrition. 2026;156(8):101669. doi:10.1016/j.tjnut.2026.101669. PMID 42288139.

The 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans affirm the recommendation of limiting SFA intake to within 10% of total energy intake but simultaneously recommend the intake of red meat and full-fat dairy rich in SFA, leading to public confusion. This review summarizes evidence from short-term feeding trials, longitudinal observational studies, and long-term dietary interventions that examined SFA, in comparison with other fatty acids and carbohydrates, in relation to blood lipids and coronary artery disease (CAD) risk. Overall, the knowledge base clearly suggests that SFA, when isocalorically replacing cis MUFA and cis PUFA, leads to elevated levels of atherogenic low-density lipoprotein cholesterol. Consistently, longitudinal observational studies and long-term high-quality dietary intervention trials demonstrated that isocalorically replacing SFA with PUFA led to a lower risk of developing CAD. Conversely, when SFAs are replaced by refined carbohydrates, the effects on blood lipids and CAD risk become more neutral. Evidence from feeding trials and observational studies also suggests that animal fat, including dairy fat, leads to adverse lipid profiles and elevated CAD risk when compared with nontropical plant-based oils rich in MUFA and PUFA. A higher consumption of red and processed meats is also associated with a higher CAD risk, especially when compared with nuts, legumes, and whole grains. The health impact of dairy products may be influenced by their fat content, vary among dairy products, and depend on the consumption levels. Overall, the evidence supports the conclusion that a heart-healthy diet should emphasize healthful sources of PUFA and MUFA, such as nontropical plant oils, nuts, and seeds; healthful sources of carbohydrate and protein, such as fresh fruits and vegetables, whole grains, legumes, and dark-meat fish; and moderate intake of low-fat and fermented dairy products, while deemphasizing excess intake of red and processed meats and full-fat dairy products.


### A. Dietary fat quality, plasma atherogenic lipoproteins, and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease: An overview of the rationale for dietary recommendations for fat intake.

Christensen JJ, Arnesen EK, Rundblad A, Telle-Hansen VH, Narverud I, Blomhoff R, Bogsrud MP, Retterstøl K, Ulven SM, Holven KB. Dietary fat quality, plasma atherogenic lipoproteins, and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease: An overview of the rationale for dietary recommendations for fat intake. Atherosclerosis. 2024;389(None):117433. doi:10.1016/j.atherosclerosis.2023.117433. PMID 38219649.

The scientific evidence supporting the current dietary recommendations for fat quality keeps accumulating; however, a paradoxical distrust has taken root among many researchers, clinicians, and in parts of the general public. One explanation for this distrust may relate to an incomplete overview of the totality of the evidence for the link between fat quality as a dietary exposure, and health outcomes such as atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD). Therefore, the main aim of the present narrative review was to provide a comprehensive overview of the rationale for dietary recommendations for fat intake, limiting our discussion to ASCVD as outcome. Herein, we provide a core framework - a causal model - that can help us understand the evidence that has accumulated to date, and that can help us understand new evidence that may become available in the future. The causal model for fat quality and ASCVD is comprised of three key research questions (RQs), each of which determine which scientific methods are most appropriate to use, and thereby which lines of evidence that should feed into the causal model. First, we discuss the link between low-density lipoprotein (LDL) particles and ASCVD (RQ1); we draw especially on evidence from genetic studies, randomized controlled trials (RCTs), epidemiology, and mechanistic studies. Second, we explain the link between dietary fat quality and LDL particles (RQ2); we draw especially on metabolic ward studies, controlled trials (randomized and non-randomized), and mechanistic studies. Third, we explain the link between dietary fat quality, LDL particles, and ASCVD (RQ3); we draw especially on RCTs in animals and humans, epidemiology, population-based changes, and experiments of nature. Additionally, the distrust over dietary recommendations for fat quality may partly relate to an unclear understanding of the scientific method, especially as applied in nutrition research, including the process of developing dietary guidelines. We therefore also aimed to clarify this process. We discuss how we assess causality in nutrition research, and how we progress from scientific evidence to providing dietary recommendations.


---

## Part D: salt, sugar, ultra-processed food, red and processed meat

Research notes for "Nutrition: The Evidence", Stage 1. Compiled 2026-09-24.

### How this was gathered, and how to read it

- **Abstracts** were retrieved as raw JSON from the Europe PMC REST API with a script (no summarising model in the path), so every abstract quoted below is verbatim. Every one of them is pasted whole in the Appendix at the end.
- **Guideline and agency pages** (WHO, IARC, WCRF, NASEM, Cancer Research UK, Harvard Nutrition Source, US Dietary Guidelines) were downloaded with curl/urllib and converted to plain text by a script, again with no summarising model, so quotes from them are verbatim from the page text. Line breaks in PDF extractions have been joined; wording is unchanged.
- Where I could only see a search-engine summary or a WebFetch model summary, I say so and **do not put the text in quotation marks**.
- Everything outside quotation marks is my own summary.
- Pages that blocked retrieval: acpjournals.org (Annals of Internal Medicine full texts, the NutriRECS correction, the Carroll and Doherty editorial) returned HTTP 403; NCBI Bookshelf returned a reCAPTCHA page (not bypassed); the Lancet Oncology Bouvard 2015 page returned 403; WHO IRIS PDFs returned a JavaScript shell. Where that matters it is flagged.

---

### 1. Salt (sodium)

#### 1.1 WHO sodium fact sheet ("Sodium reduction")

- URL: https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/salt-reduction
- What I read: the whole page, downloaded as text. **Page date shown: "11 May 2026"** (so it has been revised recently; earlier versions gave different intake and death figures).
- Type: agency fact sheet summarising WHO guidance (the 2012 "Guideline: sodium intake for adults and children" is listed under its related resources; I could not retrieve the 2012 guideline PDF itself).

Verbatim:

- "For adults, WHO recommends less than 2000 mg/day of sodium (equivalent to less than 5 g/day salt), or just under a teaspoon."
- "For children aged 2–15 years, WHO recommends adjusting the adult dose downward based on their energy requirements. This recommendation for children does not address the period of exclusive breastfeeding (0–6 months) or complementary feeding with continued breastfeeding (6–24 months)."
- "The global mean intake of adults was 4278 mg/day sodium (equivalent to 11 g/day salt) in 2021 (1) . This is more than double the World Health Organization recommendation for adults of less than 2000 mg/day sodium (equivalent to < 5 g/day salt, or approximately one teaspoon)."
- "An estimated 1.7 million deaths each year were associated with consuming too much sodium in 2023 (2) ." (Reference 2 is the GBD results database, 2025.)
- "If choosing to use table salt, WHO suggests replacing regular table salt with lower-sodium salt substitutes that contain potassium."
- "All salt that is consumed should be iodized ( fortified with iodine ), which is essential for healthy brain development in the foetus and young child and optimizing mental function in general."
- "Sodium deficiency is extremely unlikely in healthy individuals."
- "1 gram of salt is equivalent to 400 mg of sodium" / "5 grams of salt is equivalent to 2000 mg of sodium"
- On the target: "In 2013, the World Health Assembly agreed on 9 global voluntary targets for the prevention and control of NCDs, which include a halt to the rise in diabetes and obesity and a 30% relative reduction in the intake of salt by 2025. ... The timeline for achieving the targets was extended to 2030 by a World Health Assembly decision in 2019 to ensure its alignment with the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development."
- "In 2023, the first Global report on sodium intake reduction was published based on the Sodium Country Score Card ."
- "for every US$ 1 invested in scaling up sodium reduction interventions, there will be a return of at least US$ 12."

Notes: the "2023 update" the task mentioned is, on the evidence of this page, the 2023 Global report on sodium intake reduction (a monitoring report), not a revised intake guideline. The adult target is unchanged at <2 g sodium / <5 g salt. What is new since 2023 is the 2025 salt-substitute guideline (next item).

#### 1.2 WHO guideline on lower-sodium salt substitutes (January 2025) (NEW)

- WHO. Use of lower-sodium salt substitutes: WHO guideline. Geneva: WHO; 2025. ISBN 9789240105591. https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789240105591
- What I read: the WHO publication landing page (short description only), plus the George Institute's "A summary for policy makers" PDF (https://www.georgeinstitute.org/sites/default/files/2025-05/lsss_guideline_summary_final.pdf) read in full. I could not read the guideline itself (NCBI Bookshelf copy NBK612035 was blocked by a CAPTCHA). **Caution:** the George Institute ran SSaSS (Neal is at the George Institute), so this summary comes from an interested party; check wording against the WHO document before quoting it as WHO's.
- Release date: a search summary gives 27 January 2025 (not verified on a WHO page).

Verbatim from the George Institute summary:

- "GUIDELINE RECOMMENDATION: If choosing to use table salt, WHO suggests replacing regular table salt with lower-sodium salt substitutes that contain potassium."
- "In adults, the use of lower-sodium salt substitutes that contain potassium, compared to regular table salt, has led to reductions in blood pressure and cardiovascular disease risk, with no increased risk of hyperkalaemia (a high level of potassium in the blood) amongst the general population."
- "There is, however, a rationale for increased risk of hyperkalaemia if lower-sodium salt substitutes that contain potassium are consumed in excess among people with chronic kidney disease, using other potassium supplements or potassium-sparing diuretics."

Search-summary level only (NOT verbatim-checked): the recommendation is conditional; it applies to adults in general populations, not pregnant women or children, and excludes people with kidney impairment or conditions compromising potassium excretion; the underlying review covered 26 RCTs and over 35,000 people and found blood pressure reductions of about 4.76 mmHg systolic and 2.43 mmHg diastolic.

#### 1.3 Sacks FM et al. 2001, DASH-Sodium trial

- Sacks FM, Svetkey LP, Vollmer WM, Appel LJ, Bray GA, Harsha D, Obarzanek E, Conlin PR, Miller ER, Simons-Morton DG, Karanja N, Lin PH; DASH-Sodium Collaborative Research Group. Effects on blood pressure of reduced dietary sodium and the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) diet. N Engl J Med. 2001;344(1):3-10. doi:10.1056/NEJM200101043440101. PMID 11136953.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC (full abstract in Appendix).
- Design: randomised controlled feeding trial, 412 participants, diet (control vs DASH) randomised in parallel; three sodium levels for 30 days each in random order (crossover within diet).
- Key numbers (verbatim): "Reducing the sodium intake from the high to the intermediate level reduced the systolic blood pressure by 2.1 mm Hg (P<0.001) during the control diet and by 1.3 mm Hg (P=0.03) during the DASH diet. Reducing the sodium intake from the intermediate to the low level caused additional reductions of 4.6 mm Hg during the control diet (P<0.001) and 1.7 mm Hg during the DASH diet (P<0.01)." And: "As compared with the control diet with a high sodium level, the DASH diet with a low sodium level led to a mean systolic blood pressure that was 7.1 mm Hg lower in participants without hypertension, and 11.5 mm Hg lower in participants with hypertension."
- Limitation the authors state: "Long-term health benefits will depend on the ability of people to make long-lasting dietary changes and the increased availability of lower-sodium foods."
- My summary: this establishes that sodium reduction lowers blood pressure under controlled feeding, in people with and without hypertension, with the effect of sodium larger on a typical US diet than on DASH. It is a blood pressure (surrogate) trial, 30-day periods; it does not measure heart attacks, strokes or deaths. The abstract gives P values, not confidence intervals.

#### 1.4 He FJ, Li J, MacGregor GA. 2013 BMJ, Cochrane meta-analysis

- He FJ, Li J, Macgregor GA. Effect of longer term modest salt reduction on blood pressure: Cochrane systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials. BMJ. 2013;346:f1325. doi:10.1136/bmj.f1325. PMID 23558162.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: systematic review and meta-analysis of 34 RCTs (3230 participants), at least four weeks' duration.
- Key numbers (verbatim): "the mean change in urinary sodium (reduced salt v usual salt) was -75 mmol/24 h (equivalent to a reduction of 4.4 g/day salt), and with this reduction in salt intake, the mean change in blood pressure was -4.18 mm Hg (95% confidence interval -5.18 to -3.18, I(2)=75%) for systolic blood pressure and -2.06 mm Hg (-2.67 to -1.45, I(2)=68%) for diastolic blood pressure." Hypertensives: "-5.39 mm Hg (-6.62 to -4.15, I(2)=61%)" systolic; normotensives: "-2.42 mm Hg (-3.56 to -1.29, I(2)=66%)". "A 100 mmol reduction in 24 hour urinary sodium (6 g/day salt) was associated with a fall in systolic blood pressure of 5.8 mm Hg (2.5 to 9.2, P=0.001)".
- Also: "Salt reduction is associated with a small physiological increase in plasma renin activity, aldosterone, and noradrenaline and no significant change in lipid concentrations."
- Authors' conclusion (the proponent position on lower targets): "The current recommendations to reduce salt intake from 9-12 to 5-6 g/day will have a major effect on blood pressure, but a further reduction to 3 g/day will have a greater effect and should become the long term target for population salt intake."
- Note: substantial heterogeneity (I² 61 to 75% for systolic). He and MacGregor lead the advocacy group Action on Salt / World Action on Salt; this is an intellectual stake worth naming, not a hidden one.

#### 1.5 Neal B et al. 2021 NEJM, SSaSS salt substitute trial

- Neal B, Wu Y, Feng X, et al. Effect of Salt Substitution on Cardiovascular Events and Death. N Engl J Med. 2021;385(12):1067-1077. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2105675. PMID 34459569.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: open-label cluster-randomised trial, 600 villages in rural China, 20,995 people with prior stroke or aged 60+ with high blood pressure; mean follow-up 4.74 years. Substitute was 75% sodium chloride, 25% potassium chloride.
- Key numbers (verbatim), which also give **absolute rates**: "The rate of stroke was lower with the salt substitute than with regular salt (29.14 events vs. 33.65 events per 1000 person-years; rate ratio, 0.86; 95% confidence interval [CI], 0.77 to 0.96; P = 0.006), as were the rates of major cardiovascular events (49.09 events vs. 56.29 events per 1000 person-years; rate ratio, 0.87; 95% CI, 0.80 to 0.94; P<0.001) and death (39.28 events vs. 44.61 events per 1000 person-years; rate ratio, 0.88; 95% CI, 0.82 to 0.95; P<0.001)." Hyperkalaemia: "3.35 events vs. 3.30 events per 1000 person-years; rate ratio, 1.04; 95% CI, 0.80 to 1.37; P = 0.76".
- My arithmetic (not the authors'): stroke, about 4.5 fewer events per 1000 person-years; death, about 5.3 fewer per 1000 person-years. In a high-risk, high-salt, older population.
- Significance for the course: this is the hard-outcome RCT the salt dissenters said was missing. Caveats a lesson should state: the intervention changed potassium as well as sodium, so it cannot isolate sodium; the population was high-risk and mostly (72.6%) had a prior stroke; open-label.

#### 1.6 The dissent: O'Donnell, Mente, Yusuf (PURE)

**O'Donnell M et al. 2014 NEJM (PURE)**

- O'Donnell M, Mente A, Rangarajan S, et al; PURE Investigators. Urinary sodium and potassium excretion, mortality, and cardiovascular events. N Engl J Med. 2014;371(7):612-623. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1311889. PMID 25119607.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: prospective cohort; 101,945 people in 17 countries; a single morning fasting urine sample used to estimate 24-hour excretion; mean follow-up 3.7 years; 3317 composite events.
- Key numbers (verbatim): "As compared with an estimated sodium excretion of 4.00 to 5.99 g per day (reference range), a higher estimated sodium excretion (≥ 7.00 g per day) was associated with an increased risk of the composite outcome (odds ratio, 1.15; 95% confidence interval [CI], 1.02 to 1.30)". "As compared with the reference range, an estimated sodium excretion that was below 3.00 g per day was also associated with an increased risk of the composite outcome (odds ratio, 1.27; 95% CI, 1.12 to 1.44)."
- Conclusion (verbatim): "an estimated sodium intake between 3 g per day and 6 g per day was associated with a lower risk of death and cardiovascular events than was either a higher or lower estimated level of intake."
- Note: this is the J-curve (or U-curve) claim. Note that 3 g sodium is already above WHO's 2 g target, so on this reading the WHO target lies in the "higher risk" zone.

**Mente A et al. 2016 Lancet**

- Mente A, O'Donnell M, Rangarajan S, et al. Associations of urinary sodium excretion with cardiovascular events in individuals with and without hypertension: a pooled analysis of data from four studies. Lancet. 2016;388(10043):465-475. doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(16)30467-6. PMID 27216139.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: pooled analysis of four prospective studies (PURE, EPIDREAM, ONTARGET, TRANSCEND); 133,118 people in 49 countries; median 4.2 years.
- Key numbers (verbatim): in hypertensives, sodium "of 7 g/day or more ... hazard ratio [HR] 1.23 [95% CI 1.11-1.37]" and "less than 3 g/day ... 1.34 [1.23-1.47]" versus 4-5 g/day. In normotensives, ≥7 g/day "HR 0.90 [95% CI 0.76-1.08]" (no association) and <3 g/day "HR 1.26 [95% CI 1.10-1.45]".
- Interpretation (verbatim): "These data suggest that lowering sodium intake is best targeted at populations with hypertension who consume high sodium diets."

**O'Donnell M et al. 2020 European Heart Journal (their best statement of the case)**

- O'Donnell M, Mente A, Alderman MH, et al. Salt and cardiovascular disease: insufficient evidence to recommend low sodium intake. Eur Heart J. 2020;41(35):3363-3373. doi:10.1093/eurheartj/ehaa586. PMID 33011774.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. Review/opinion piece with many co-signatories (including Messerli, Mancia, Oparil, McCarron).
- Verbatim: "These guidelines have been developed without effective interventions to achieve sustained low sodium intake in free-living individuals, without a feasible method to estimate sodium intake reliably in individuals, and without high-quality evidence that low sodium intake reduces cardiovascular events (compared with moderate intake)."
- Verbatim: "We contend that current evidence, despite methodological limitations, suggests that most of the world's population consume a moderate range of dietary sodium (2.3-4.6g/day; 1-2 teaspoons of salt) that is not associated with increased cardiovascular risk, and that the risk of cardiovascular disease increases when sodium intakes exceed 5 g/day."
- Notice the concession, verbatim: "it is reasonable, based upon observational studies, to suggest a population-level mean target of <5 g/day in populations with mean sodium intake of >5 g/day, while awaiting the results of large randomized controlled trials of sodium reduction on incidence of cardiovascular events and mortality."
- My summary: both sides agree that very high intakes are harmful and that blood pressure falls when sodium falls. The dispute is narrow: whether going below roughly 2.3 to 3 g sodium a day brings further benefit or harm, and whether the guideline targets are achievable for individuals.

#### 1.7 The critique of spot-urine methods, and the 24-hour-urine evidence

**Cook NR, Appel LJ, Whelton PK. 2016 JACC (TOHP follow-up)**

- Cook NR, Appel LJ, Whelton PK. Sodium Intake and All-Cause Mortality Over 20 Years in the Trials of Hypertension Prevention. J Am Coll Cardiol. 2016;68(15):1609-1617. doi:10.1016/j.jacc.2016.07.745. PMID 27712772. PMCID PMC5098805.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: post-trial follow-up of TOHP I and II (sodium-reduction RCTs); multiple 24-hour urine collections; median 24 years of death follow-up.
- Randomised comparison (verbatim): "251 deaths occurred, representing a nonsignificant 15% lower risk in the active intervention (hazard ratio [HR]: 0.85; 95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.66 to 1.09; p = 0.19)."
- Observational (non-intervention participants, n=2,974, 272 deaths), verbatim: "There was a direct linear association between average sodium intake and mortality, with an HR of 0.75, 0.95, and 1.00 (references) and 1.07 (p trend = 0.30) for <2,300, 2,300 to <3,600, 3,600 to <4,800, and ≥4,800 mg/24 h, respectively; and with an HR of 1.12 per 1,000 mg/24 h (95% CI: 1.00 to 1.26; p = 0.05). There was no evidence of a J-shaped or nonlinear relationship."
- Note: the randomised result is not statistically significant; the linear trend is borderline (p = 0.05, CI touching 1.00). A lesson should report it as supporting, not proving.

**He FJ, Ma Y, Campbell NRC, MacGregor GA, Cogswell ME, Cook NR. 2019 Hypertension**

- He FJ, Ma Y, Campbell NRC, MacGregor GA, Cogswell ME, Cook NR. Formulas to Estimate Dietary Sodium Intake From Spot Urine Alter Sodium-Mortality Relationship. Hypertension. 2019;74(3):572-580. doi:10.1161/HYPERTENSIONAHA.119.13117. PMID 31352828.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: methodological re-analysis of TOHP data comparing measured 24-hour sodium with spot-urine formula estimates (Kawasaki, Tanaka, INTERSALT). Kawasaki is the formula PURE used (my note from background knowledge; the abstract does not name PURE).
- Verbatim: "All estimated values, including those with constant sodium concentration, were systematically biased with overestimation at lower levels and underestimation at higher levels. There was a significant linear association between the average measured sodium intake (ie, gold standard method) and mortality. This relationship was altered by using the estimated sodium intakes. There appeared to be a J- or U-shaped relationship for the average estimated sodium by all formulas."
- Conclusion (verbatim): "These results demonstrate that inaccurate estimates of sodium cannot be used in association studies, particularly as the formulas per se seem to be related to mortality independent of sodium."

**Ma Y et al. 2022 NEJM (multiple 24-hour urines, six cohorts)**

- Ma Y, He FJ, Sun Q, et al. 24-Hour Urinary Sodium and Potassium Excretion and Cardiovascular Risk. N Engl J Med. 2022;386(3):252-263. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2109794. PMID 34767706. PMCID PMC9153854.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: individual-participant meta-analysis of six cohorts, 10,709 generally healthy adults, at least two 24-hour urines each, median 8.8 years, 571 events.
- Verbatim: "Each daily increment of 1000 mg in sodium excretion was associated with an 18% increase in cardiovascular risk (hazard ratio, 1.18; 95% CI, 1.08 to 1.29), and each daily increment of 1000 mg in potassium excretion was associated with an 18% decrease in risk (hazard ratio, 0.82; 95% CI, 0.72 to 0.94)." Top vs bottom quartile of sodium: "1.60 (95% confidence interval [CI], 1.19 to 2.14)".
- Absolute framing given in the abstract: "incidence rate, 5.9 per 1000 person-years".
- Median sodium was 3270 mg, 10th to 90th percentile 2099 to 4899, so this study has little data below 2 g either.

**What would settle it:** a large RCT randomising people to a low (under about 2 g sodium) versus moderate (about 3 to 4.5 g) intake with hard outcomes, and sustained adherence verified by repeated 24-hour urine collections. The dissenters themselves call for exactly this. SSaSS is the closest yet but tests a sodium-potassium swap, not low sodium alone.

#### 1.8 NASEM 2019 Dietary Reference Intakes for Sodium and Potassium

- National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine. Dietary Reference Intakes for Sodium and Potassium. Washington, DC: The National Academies Press; 2019. doi:10.17226/25353. PMID 30844154.
- Read: the report's Summary chapter on nap.nationalacademies.org (https://nap.nationalacademies.org/read/25353/chapter/2), including Table S-2. Also the Europe PMC record (abstract, which does not give numbers).
- Verbatim: "Our DRI report provides a sodium intake level as an AI, and with sodium, the report establishes the first Chronic Disease Risk Reduction Intake (CDRR) level." (from chapter 1, preface)
- Verbatim: "the committee found there was insufficient evidence of risk of excess sodium or potassium intake within the healthy populations to establish a UL for either nutrient."
- Verbatim: "The committee concludes there is moderate to high strength of evidence for both a causal relationship and an intake–response relationship between sodium and several interrelated chronic disease indicators: cardiovascular disease, hypertension, systolic blood pressure, and diastolic blood pressure."
- Adult AI: "the committee established the sodium AIs for adults 19 years of age and older at 1,500 mg/d (65 mmol/d)."
- Table S-2, adults 19 to >70: CDRR "Reduce intakes if above 2,300 mg/day". Children 1-3: "Reduce intakes if above 1,200 mg/day"; 4-8: 1,500; 9-13: 1,800; 14-18: 2,300.
- Verbatim on why the line is at 2,300: "The sodium CDRR is the lowest level of intake for which there was sufficient strength of evidence to characterize a chronic disease risk reduction. Further reductions in sodium intake below the CDRR have demonstrated a lowering effect on blood pressure, but the effect on chronic disease risk could not be characterized."
- Verbatim: "Across the trials, no deficiency symptoms were reported. Furthermore, there was insufficient evidence that low sodium intakes are associated with other potential harmful health effects."
- My summary: NASEM sits between WHO and the dissenters. It set the chronic-disease threshold at 2.3 g (not 2 g, not 1.5 g), explicitly because the evidence below that was insufficient for hard outcomes, and it found no evidence of harm from low intakes in trials (which is a direct disagreement with PURE's J-curve, though it does not name PURE in the passages I read).

#### 1.9 US Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030 (NEW, released January 2026)

- Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025–2030. PDF at https://cdn.realfood.gov/DGA.pdf. Read: the whole PDF (a short consumer-facing document, about 2,700 words). Release date from search results: early January 2026 (not verified on the document).
- Verbatim: "The general population, ages 14 and above, should consume less than 2,300 mg per day of sodium. Highly active individuals may benefit from increased sodium intake to offset sweat losses." Children: "Ages 1–3: less than 1,200 mg per day", "Ages 4–8: less than 1,500 mg per day", "Ages 9–13: less than 1,800 mg per day".
- Note: matches the NASEM CDRR; the sweat-loss sentence is new.

#### 1.10 Absolute-risk framing for salt

- SSaSS: stroke 29.14 vs 33.65 per 1000 person-years; death 39.28 vs 44.61 per 1000 person-years (verbatim above).
- Ma 2022: baseline cardiovascular incidence 5.9 per 1000 person-years.
- I found no Cancer-Research-UK-style lifetime absolute risk statement for salt.

---

### 2. Sugar

#### 2.1 WHO 2015 guideline on sugars intake

- WHO news release, 4 March 2015: https://www.who.int/news/item/04-03-2015-who-calls-on-countries-to-reduce-sugars-intake-among-adults-and-children. Read: the whole release as text. The guideline document itself (Guideline: Sugars intake for adults and children, WHO 2015) was not retrieved.
- Verbatim: "A new WHO guideline recommends adults and children reduce their daily intake of free sugars to less than 10% of their total energy intake. A further reduction to below 5% or roughly 25 grams (6 teaspoons) per day would provide additional health benefits."
- Definition, verbatim: "Free sugars refer to monosaccharides (such as glucose, fructose) and disaccharides (such as sucrose or table sugar) added to foods and drinks by the manufacturer, cook or consumer, and sugars naturally present in honey, syrups, fruit juices and fruit juice concentrates."
- Exclusion, verbatim: "The WHO guideline does not refer to the sugars in fresh fruits and vegetables, and sugars naturally present in milk, because there is no reported evidence of adverse effects of consuming these sugars."
- Strength, verbatim: "Based on the quality of supporting evidence, these recommendations are ranked by WHO as “strong”. This means they can be adopted as policy in most situations." (This refers to the <10% recommendation.)
- <5%, verbatim: "Given the nature of existing studies, the recommendation of reducing intake of free sugars to below 5% of total energy is presented as “conditional” in the WHO system for issuing evidence-based guidance."
- The evidence for <5%, verbatim: "Only three national population-wide studies allow a comparison of dental caries with sugars intakes of less than 5% of total energy intake versus more than 5% but less than 10% of total energy intake." And: "These population-based ecological studies were conducted during a period when sugars availability dropped dramatically from 15kg per person per year before the Second World War to a low of 0.2kg per person per year in 1946. This “natural experiment”, which demonstrated a reduction in dental caries, provides the basis for the recommendation that reducing the intake of free sugars below 5% of total energy intake would provide additional health benefits in the form of reduced dental caries."
- Branca quote, verbatim: "We have solid evidence that keeping intake of free sugars to less than 10% of total energy intake reduces the risk of overweight, obesity and tooth decay,"
- Useful examples, verbatim: "1 tablespoon of ketchup contains around 4 grams (around 1 teaspoon) of free sugars. A single can of sugar-sweetened soda contains up to 40 grams (around 10 teaspoons) of free sugars."
- Note for the lesson: the <10% line rests on weight and dental-caries evidence; the <5% line rests on dental caries from wartime ecological data. It is not a claim about diabetes or heart disease.

#### 2.2 Te Morenga L, Mallard S, Mann J. BMJ 2012/2013

- Te Morenga L, Mallard S, Mann J. Dietary sugars and body weight: systematic review and meta-analyses of randomised controlled trials and cohort studies. BMJ. 2013;346:e7492. doi:10.1136/bmj.e7492. PMID 23321486. (Europe PMC records the year as 2012, the online-first date; the volume is 2013. Cite as BMJ 2013;346:e7492, published online January 2013 per the volume; confirm if the exact date matters.)
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. This review was commissioned by WHO for the 2015 guideline (my background knowledge; not stated in the abstract).
- Design: SR/MA of 30 RCTs and 38 cohort studies.
- Verbatim: "In trials of adults with ad libitum diets (that is, with no strict control of food intake), reduced intake of dietary sugars was associated with a decrease in body weight (0.80 kg, 95% confidence interval 0.39 to 1.21; P<0.001); increased sugars intake was associated with a comparable weight increase (0.75 kg, 0.30 to 1.19; P=0.001). Isoenergetic exchange of dietary sugars with other carbohydrates showed no change in body weight (0.04 kg, -0.04 to 0.13)."
- Children: "these trials showed no overall change in body weight" (low compliance); cohort SSB: "the odds ratio for being overweight or obese increased was 1.55 (1.32 to 1.82)".
- Conclusion, verbatim: "The change in body fatness that occurs with modifying intakes seems to be mediated via changes in energy intakes, since isoenergetic exchange of sugars with other carbohydrates was not associated with weight change."
- Key teaching point: the review WHO relied on found that sugar affects weight **through calories**, not uniquely. This is the calm middle position and it undercuts the "uniquely toxic" view on weight specifically.

#### 2.3 Sugar-sweetened beverages (Malik and colleagues)

**Malik VS et al. 2010 Diabetes Care**

- Malik VS, Popkin BM, Bray GA, Després JP, Willett WC, Hu FB. Sugar-sweetened beverages and risk of metabolic syndrome and type 2 diabetes: a meta-analysis. Diabetes Care. 2010;33(11):2477-2483. doi:10.2337/dc10-1079. PMID 20693348.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. Meta-analysis of 11 prospective cohorts (310,819 participants, 15,043 T2D cases).
- Verbatim: "individuals in the highest quantile of SSB intake (most often 1-2 servings/day) had a 26% greater risk of developing type 2 diabetes than those in the lowest quantile (none or <1 serving/month) (relative risk [RR] 1.26 [95% CI 1.12-1.41])." Metabolic syndrome: "the pooled RR was 1.20 [1.02-1.42]."

**Malik VS et al. 2013 AJCN**

- Malik VS, Pan A, Willett WC, Hu FB. Sugar-sweetened beverages and weight gain in children and adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Am J Clin Nutr. 2013;98(4):1084-1102. doi:10.3945/ajcn.113.058362. PMID 23966427.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. 32 articles; cohorts and RCTs.
- Verbatim: "one daily serving increment of SSBs was associated with a 0.06 (95% CI: 0.02, 0.10) and 0.05 (95% CI: 0.03, 0.07)-unit increase in BMI in children and 0.22 kg (95% CI: 0.09, 0.34 kg) and 0.12 kg (95% CI: 0.10, 0.14 kg) weight gain in adults over 1 y in random- and fixed-effects models, respectively." RCTs in adults adding SSBs: "0.85 kg; 95% CI: 0.50, 1.20 kg".
- Note: the RCT result in children, "-0.17 (95% CI: -0.39, 0.05)", crosses zero in the random-effects model. The abstract prints the fixed-effects CI as "(95% CI: -0.22, -0.2)", which looks like a typo in the source (probably -0.02); do not quote that figure without checking the full text.

#### 2.4 The contested question: is sugar uniquely harmful, beyond its calories?

**Proponent: Lustig RH. 2013 Advances in Nutrition**

- Lustig RH. Fructose: it's "alcohol without the buzz". Adv Nutr. 2013;4(2):226-235. doi:10.3945/an.112.002998. PMID 23493539. PMCID PMC3649103.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. Review/opinion, peer-reviewed journal.
- Verbatim: "Sugar (i.e., fructose-containing mixtures) has been vilified by nutritionists for ages as a source of "empty calories," no different from any other empty calorie. However, fructose is unlike glucose. In the hypercaloric glycogen-replete state, intermediary metabolites from fructose metabolism overwhelm hepatic mitochondrial capacity, which promotes de novo lipogenesis and leads to hepatic insulin resistance, which drives chronic metabolic disease."
- Verbatim: "Thus, fructose can exert detrimental health effects beyond its calories and in ways that mimic those of ethanol, its metabolic cousin."
- Note the qualifier in his own words: "In the hypercaloric glycogen-replete state". The mechanism claim is conditional on overfeeding, which matters when comparing with Sievenpiper's isocaloric finding below.
- Also: Lustig RH, Schmidt LA, Brindis CD. Public health: The toxic truth about sugar. Nature. 2012;482(7383):27-29. doi:10.1038/482027a. Comment piece; no abstract available in Europe PMC; NOT READ.

**Proponent's trial: Lustig RH et al. 2016 Obesity**

- Lustig RH, Mulligan K, Noworolski SM, Tai VW, Wen MJ, Erkin-Cakmak A, Gugliucci A, Schwarz JM. Isocaloric fructose restriction and metabolic improvement in children with obesity and metabolic syndrome. Obesity (Silver Spring). 2016;24(2):453-460. doi:10.1002/oby.21371. PMID 26499447. PMCID PMC4736733. (Authors as printed in the Europe PMC record.)
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: 43 children, 9 days, no control group; sugar cut from 28% to 10% of energy, replaced with starch.
- Verbatim: "Reductions in diastolic blood pressure (-5 mmHg; P = 0.002), lactate (-0.3 mmol/L; P < 0.001), triglyceride, and LDL-cholesterol (-46% and -0.3 mmol/L; P < 0.001) were noted." "Weight reduced by 0.9 ± 0.2 kg (P < 0.001)". "Post hoc sensitivity analysis demonstrates that results in the subcohort that did not lose weight (n = 10) were directionally consistent."
- Limitations visible in the abstract (my reading): no control arm, 9 days, children lost weight despite the "isocaloric" design, and the weight-stable subgroup was only 10 children analysed post hoc.

**Proponent's ecological analysis: Basu S, Yoffe P, Hills N, Lustig RH. 2013 PLoS One**

- doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0057873. PMID 23460912. Read: abstract.
- Verbatim: "every 150 kcal/person/day increase in sugar availability (about one can of soda/day) was associated with increased diabetes prevalence by 1.1% (p <0.001)". Ecological (country-level availability, not individual intake).

**Point/counterpoint: Bray GA, Popkin BM vs Kahn R, Sievenpiper JL. Diabetes Care 2014**

- Bray GA, Popkin BM. Dietary sugar and body weight: have we reached a crisis in the epidemic of obesity and diabetes?: health be damned! Pour on the sugar. Diabetes Care. 2014;37(4):950-956. doi:10.2337/dc13-2085. PMID 24652725. Read: abstract.
  - Verbatim: "Drinking two 16-ounce SSBs per day for 6 months induced features of the metabolic syndrome and fatty liver." "Reducing intake of soft drinks is associated with less weight gain."
- Kahn R, Sievenpiper JL. Dietary sugar and body weight: have we reached a crisis in the epidemic of obesity and diabetes?: we have, but the pox on sugar is overwrought and overworked. Diabetes Care. 2014;37(4):957-962. doi:10.2337/dc13-2506. PMID 24652726. Read: abstract.
  - Verbatim (the critic's best statement): "we argue that there is no clear or convincing evidence that any dietary or added sugar has a unique or detrimental impact relative to any other source of calories on the development of obesity or diabetes. Sugar is purely a highly palatable source of energy; because it has no other property that appears to contribute to our nutritional well-being, it is not an essential food for most of us. For those who wish to reduce energy consumption, ingesting less sugar is a good place to start. However, doing so does not automatically portend any clinical benefit."
- Conflict note: Sievenpiper has disclosed past funding and honoraria from food and beverage industry bodies in various papers (my background knowledge; I did not read this paper's disclosure statement). Check the disclosure before a lesson mentions it.

**Critic's trial evidence: Sievenpiper JL et al. 2012 Annals**

- Sievenpiper JL, de Souza RJ, Mirrahimi A, et al. Effect of fructose on body weight in controlled feeding trials: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Ann Intern Med. 2012;156(4):291-304. doi:10.7326/0003-4819-156-4-201202210-00007. PMID 22351714. (First authors as printed in the Europe PMC record; the list continues with Yu ME, Carleton AJ, Beyene J, Chiavaroli L, Di Buono M, Jenkins AL, Leiter LA, Wolever TM, Kendall CW, Jenkins DJ.)
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Verbatim: "Fructose had no overall effect on body weight in isocaloric trials (mean difference, -0.14 kg [95% CI, -0.37 to 0.10 kg] for fructose compared with nonfructose carbohydrate). High doses of fructose in hypercaloric trials (+104 to 250 g/d, +18% to 97% of total daily energy intake) lead to significant increases in weight (mean difference, 0.53 kg [CI, 0.26 to 0.79 kg] with fructose)."
- Its own limitations, verbatim: "Most trials had methodological limitations and were of poor quality. The weight-increasing effect of fructose in hypercaloric trials may have been attributable to excess energy rather than fructose itself." Studies "tended to be small (<15 participants), short (<12 weeks), and of low quality."

**An industry-funded attack on the sugar guidelines: Erickson J et al. 2017 Annals**

- Erickson J, Sadeghirad B, Lytvyn L, Slavin J, Johnston BC. The Scientific Basis of Guideline Recommendations on Sugar Intake: A Systematic Review. Ann Intern Med. 2017;166(4):257-267. doi:10.7326/M16-2020. PMID 27992898.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Verbatim conclusion: "Guidelines on dietary sugar do not meet criteria for trustworthy recommendations and are based on low-quality evidence."
- Funding, verbatim: "Primary funding source: Technical Committee on Dietary Carbohydrates of the North American branch of the International Life Sciences Institute." And the stated limitation: "The authors conducted the study independent of the funding source, which is primarily supported by the food and agriculture industry."
- Why it matters for this course: Bradley Johnston, the senior author here, is also the lead of the 2019 NutriRECS meat guideline (section 4). Both use GRADE to rate nutrition evidence "low certainty". A lesson can present this as a methodological school (GRADE applied strictly to nutrition) that has attracted industry funding, while still engaging with its argument on the merits.

**What would settle the sugar question:** long-term isocaloric trials (months to years) in free-living people comparing sugar with starch at matched energy, with hard or strong intermediate outcomes (liver fat, incident diabetes). The short feeding trials so far point to calories as the main driver of weight; the unresolved part is liver fat and metabolic effects at high intakes and whether liquid sugar behaves differently.

#### 2.5 Non-sugar sweeteners: WHO 2023 guideline

- WHO news release, 15 May 2023: https://www.who.int/news/item/15-05-2023-who-advises-not-to-use-non-sugar-sweeteners-for-weight-control-in-newly-released-guideline. Read: whole release as text.
- Verbatim: "The World Health Organization (WHO) has released a new guideline on non-sugar sweeteners (NSS), which recommends against the use of NSS to control body weight or reduce the risk of noncommunicable diseases (NCDs)."
- Verbatim: "The recommendation is based on the findings of a systematic review of the available evidence which suggests that use of NSS does not confer any long-term benefit in reducing body fat in adults or children. Results of the review also suggest that there may be potential undesirable effects from long-term use of NSS, such as an increased risk of type 2 diabetes, cardiovascular diseases, and mortality in adults."
- Scope, verbatim: "The recommendation applies to all people except individuals with pre-existing diabetes and includes all synthetic and naturally occurring or modified non-nutritive sweeteners ... Common NSS include acesulfame K, aspartame, advantame, cyclamates, neotame, saccharin, sucralose, stevia and stevia derivatives." It does not apply to "low-calorie sugars and sugar alcohols (polyols)".
- Conditional, verbatim: "Because the link observed in the evidence between NSS and disease outcomes might be confounded by baseline characteristics of study participants and complicated patterns of NSS use, the recommendation has been assessed as conditional, following WHO processes for developing guidelines."
- Branca, verbatim: "NSS are not essential dietary factors and have no nutritional value. People should reduce the sweetness of the diet altogether, starting early in life, to improve their health."
- Teaching point: WHO itself says the harm signal may be confounding (reverse causation: people at risk switch to diet drinks). The recommendation is "don't expect them to help", not "they are proven harmful".

#### 2.6 Aspartame: IARC 2B and JECFA ADI (July 2023)

- WHO/IARC/JECFA joint news release, 14 July 2023: https://www.who.int/news/item/14-07-2023-aspartame-hazard-and-risk-assessment-results-released. Read: whole release as text.
- Verbatim: "Citing “limited evidence” for carcinogenicity in humans, IARC classified aspartame as possibly carcinogenic to humans (IARC Group 2B) and JECFA reaffirmed the acceptable daily intake of 40 mg/kg body weight."
- Verbatim: "IARC classified aspartame as possibly carcinogenic to humans (Group 2B) on the basis of limited evidence for cancer in humans (specifically, for hepatocellular carcinoma, which is a type of liver cancer). There was also limited evidence for cancer in experimental animals and limited evidence related to the possible mechanisms for causing cancer."
- Verbatim: "JECFA concluded that the data evaluated indicated no sufficient reason to change the previously established acceptable daily intake (ADI) of 0–40 mg/kg body weight for aspartame. The committee therefore reaffirmed that it is safe for a person to consume within this limit per day. For example, with a can of diet soft drink containing 200 or 300 mg of aspartame, an adult weighing 70kg would need to consume more than 9–14 cans per day to exceed the acceptable daily intake, assuming no other intake from other food sources."
- Hazard vs risk, verbatim: "IARC classifications reflect the strength of scientific evidence as to whether an agent can cause cancer in humans, but they do not reflect the risk of developing cancer at a given exposure level."
- Branca, verbatim: "The assessments of aspartame have indicated that, while safety is not a major concern at the doses which are commonly used, potential effects have been described that need to be investigated by more and better studies."
- JECFA's Sanaa, verbatim: "JECFA also considered the evidence on cancer risk, in animal and human studies, and concluded that the evidence of an association between aspartame consumption and cancer in humans is not convincing,"

#### 2.7 US Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030 on sugar and sweeteners (NEW)

- Source as in 1.9. Verbatim: "While no amount of added sugars or non-nutritive sweeteners is recommended or considered part of a healthy or nutritious diet, one meal should contain no more than 10 grams of added sugars." And: "Limit foods and beverages that include artificial flavors, petroleum-based dyes, artificial preservatives, and low-calorie non-nutritive sweeteners." And: "Avoid sugar-sweetened beverages, such as sodas, fruit drinks, and energy drinks."
- Note: the previous US guideline was "less than 10% of calories from added sugars"; the new document (as read) states a per-meal cap instead and I did not find a percentage-of-calories limit in the consumer PDF. The fuller scientific report may differ; not checked.

#### 2.8 Absolute-risk framing for sugar

- None found in the sources read. Te Morenga gives absolute weight changes (0.75 to 0.80 kg), which is itself an absolute framing.

---

### 3. Ultra-processed food (UPF)

#### 3.1 Monteiro CA et al. 2019, the NOVA definition

- Monteiro CA, Cannon G, Levy RB, Moubarac JC, Louzada ML, Rauber F, Khandpur N, Cediel G, Neri D, Martinez-Steele E, Baraldi LG, Jaime PC. Ultra-processed foods: what they are and how to identify them. Public Health Nutr. 2019;22(5):936-941. doi:10.1017/S1368980018003762. PMID 30744710. PMCID PMC10260459. (Authors as printed in the Europe PMC record.)
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. Commentary.
- Verbatim: "Ultra-processed foods are defined within the NOVA classification system, which groups foods according to the extent and purpose of industrial processing."
- Verbatim: "Processes and ingredients used to manufacture ultra-processed foods are designed to create highly profitable (low-cost ingredients, long shelf-life, emphatic branding), convenient (ready-to-consume), hyper-palatable products liable to displace all other NOVA food groups, notably unprocessed or minimally processed foods."
- The practical test, verbatim: "A practical way to identify an ultra-processed product is to check to see if its list of ingredients contains at least one item characteristic of the NOVA ultra-processed food group, which is to say, either food substances never or rarely used in kitchens (such as high-fructose corn syrup, hydrogenated or interesterified oils, and hydrolysed proteins), or classes of additives designed to make the final product palatable or more appealing (such as flavours, flavour enhancers, colours, emulsifiers, emulsifying salts, sweeteners, thickeners, and anti-foaming, bulking, carbonating, foaming, gelling and glazing agents)."
- Note: the definition includes purpose ("designed to create highly profitable ... products"), which critics say is not a physical property of a food.

#### 3.2 Lane MM et al. 2024 BMJ umbrella review

- Lane MM, Gamage E, Du S, et al. Ultra-processed food exposure and adverse health outcomes: umbrella review of epidemiological meta-analyses. BMJ. 2024;384:e077310. doi:10.1136/bmj-2023-077310. PMID 38418082. PMCID PMC10899807.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: umbrella review of 45 pooled analyses (n=9,888,373); all observational (cohort, case-control, cross-sectional).
- Verbatim: "convincing evidence (class I) supported direct associations between greater ultra-processed food exposure and higher risks of incident cardiovascular disease related mortality (risk ratio 1.50, 95% confidence interval 1.37 to 1.63; GRADE=very low) and type 2 diabetes (dose-response risk ratio 1.12, 1.11 to 1.13; moderate), as well as higher risks of prevalent anxiety outcomes (odds ratio 1.48, 1.37 to 1.59; low) and combined common mental disorder outcomes (odds ratio 1.53, 1.43 to 1.63; low)."
- Class II, verbatim: "all cause mortality (risk ratio 1.21, 1.15 to 1.27; low)".
- Quality, verbatim: "Overall, using the GRADE framework, 22 pooled analyses were rated as low quality, with 19 rated as very low quality and four rated as moderate quality."
- Teaching point: "convincing" in the authors' credibility scheme and "very low" in GRADE were given to the same finding (CVD mortality). The two grading systems measure different things (statistical robustness vs risk of confounding). A lesson can use this as a worked example of how the same data get two verdicts.

#### 3.3 The critics

**Gibney MJ, Forde CG, Mullally D, Gibney ER. 2017 AJCN**

- Gibney MJ, Forde CG, Mullally D, Gibney ER. Ultra-processed foods in human health: a critical appraisal. Am J Clin Nutr. 2017;106(3):717-724. doi:10.3945/ajcn.117.160440. PMID 28793996. (Note: the task gave the DOI stem as 116.146084; the correct DOI per Europe PMC is 10.3945/ajcn.117.160440.)
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Verbatim: "We believe that there is no evidence to uphold the view that UPFDs give rise to hyperpalatable foods associated with a quasi-addictive effect and that the prevailing European Union and US data fail to uphold the assertion that UPFDs, which dominate energy intake, give rise to dietary patterns that are low in micronutrients. With regard to the use of the NOVA food classification in the development of food-based dietary guidelines, we show that the very broad definition of UPFDs makes this impossible."
- Verbatim: "On balance, therefore, there seems to be little advantage from the use of the NOVA classification compared with the current epidemiologic approach, which relies on the linkage of nutrient intakes to chronic disease with subsequent identification of foods that merit consideration in public health nutrition strategies."

**Astrup A, Monteiro CA. 2022 AJCN debate**

- YES side: Monteiro CA, Astrup A. Does the concept of "ultra-processed foods" help inform dietary guidelines, beyond conventional classification systems? YES. Am J Clin Nutr. 2022;116(6):1476-1481. doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqac122. PMID 35670127.
- NO side: Astrup A, Monteiro CA. ... NO. Am J Clin Nutr. 2022;116(6):1482-1488. doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqac123. PMID 35670128.
- Author order verified from Europe PMC: YES paper "Monteiro CA, Astrup A"; NO paper "Astrup A, Monteiro CA".
- Read: both abstracts via Europe PMC.
- YES, verbatim: "This recommendation is supported by systematic reviews and meta-analyses of nationally representative dietary surveys and long-term cohort studies." "Although more research should be done to identify these mechanisms, existing evidence is sufficient to recommend the avoidance of ultra-processed foods to optimize health and policies to support and make feasible this recommendation."
- NO, verbatim: "The Nova classification of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) rests on poorly defined food processes and the presence of food additives from a chemically heterogeneous group, easily leading to misclassification." "Observational studies link dietary intake of UPFs with obesity, but none have demonstrated independent associations after controlling for likely confounders." On Hall 2019: "A highly cited randomized controlled feeding study that compared a UPF diet with an unprocessed diet showed a rapidly weaning effect on energy intake that can be entirely explained by more conventional and quantifiable dietary factors, including energy density, intrinsic fiber, glycemic load, and added sugar." Conclusion: "the Nova classification adds little to existing nutrient profiling systems; characterizes several healthy, nutrient-dense foods as unhealthy; and is counterproductive to solve the major global food production challenges."

**Braesco V et al. 2022 EJCN, reliability of NOVA assignment**

- Braesco V, Souchon I, Sauvant P, Haurogné T, Maillot M, Féart C, Darmon N. Ultra-processed foods: how functional is the NOVA system? Eur J Clin Nutr. 2022;76(9):1245-1253. doi:10.1038/s41430-022-01099-1. PMID 35314769. PMCID PMC9436773.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Design: online survey; French food and nutrition specialists assigned foods to NOVA groups; 120 marketed foods with ingredient lists (159 evaluators) and 111 generic foods without (177 evaluators).
- Verbatim: "Fleiss' κ was 0.32 and 0.34 for the marketed foods (n = 159 evaluators) and generic foods (n = 177 evaluators), respectively."
- Conclusion, verbatim: "Although assignments were more consistent for some foods than others, overall consistency among evaluators was low, even when ingredient information was available. These results suggest current NOVA criteria do not allow for robust and functional food assignments."
- Note: 90 of 120 marketed foods clustered with 91% of assignments to NOVA 4, so the disagreement is concentrated in borderline foods. A lesson should say that, since the headline kappa can suggest more chaos than there is.

#### 3.4 Hall KD et al. 2019 Cell Metabolism (covered by another agent; noted only)

- Hall KD, Ayuketah A, Brychta R, et al. Ultra-Processed Diets Cause Excess Calorie Intake and Weight Gain: An Inpatient Randomized Controlled Trial of Ad Libitum Food Intake. Cell Metab. 2019;30(1):67-77.e3. doi:10.1016/j.cmet.2019.05.008. PMID 31105044. Abstract retrieved (in Appendix) for completeness: 20 adults, 2 weeks each diet, energy intake "508 ± 106 kcal/day" higher on the UPF diet. Astrup's NO paper (above) is the main published counter-reading of it.

#### 3.5 Recent developments (2025-2026) (NEW)

- **Lancet Series on UPF, November 2025.** Monteiro CA, Louzada ML, Steele-Martinez E, et al. Ultra-processed foods and human health: the main thesis and the evidence. Lancet. 2025;406(10520):2667-2684. doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(25)01565-X. PMID 41270766. Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
  - Verbatim: "The totality of the evidence supports the thesis that displacement of long-established dietary patterns by ultra-processed foods is a key driver of the escalating global burden of multiple diet-related chronic diseases."
  - Verbatim, the evidence base claimed: "more than 100 prospective studies, meta-analyses, randomised controlled trials, and mechanistic studies, covering adverse outcomes across nearly all organ systems."
  - Companion policy paper: Scrinis G, Popkin BM, et al. Lancet. 2025;406(10520):2685-2702. doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(25)01566-1 (abstract in Appendix). Accompanying Lancet editorial titled "Ultra-processed foods: time to put health before profit" (doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(25)02322-0; not read).
- **Letters in response, Lancet 2026;407(10532):946-950** (titles only seen, NOT READ): letters from Ludwig DS; Kuhnle GGC; Jacobs D and Sampson R; Campos T and Esturo A; Dos Santos Rahal L et al; and "Authors' reply" from Monteiro CA, Rezende LFM, Baker P, Nestle M, Corvalán C, Popkin BM. Kuhnle has published critiques of NOVA before (background knowledge); Ludwig's letter could be a useful third position. Worth fetching in a later pass if a lesson covers the current state of the debate.
- **US Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030** (source as 1.9), first US guideline to target processing explicitly. Verbatim: "Avoid highly processed packaged, prepared, ready-to-eat, or other foods that are salty or sweet, such as chips, cookies, and candy that have added sugars and sodium (salt). Instead, prioritize nutrient-dense foods and home-prepared meals." It uses "highly processed", not NOVA's "ultra-processed", and gives no formal definition in the consumer PDF I read.

#### 3.6 What would settle the UPF question

Longer controlled-feeding trials in which the UPF and minimally processed diets are matched not only on presented nutrients (as Hall 2019 did) but also on energy density, texture/eating rate and hyper-palatability, to see whether any effect of "processing" remains. Plus a validated, reproducible classification (Braesco shows the current one is not). Both sides of the AJCN debate accept that mechanisms are not settled.

#### 3.7 Absolute-risk framing for UPF

- None found in the sources read. Lane 2024 reports only relative measures.

---

### 4. Red and processed meat

#### 4.1 IARC Monograph evaluation (2015)

**Bouvard V et al. 2015 Lancet Oncology**

- Bouvard V, Loomis D, Guyton KZ, Grosse Y, Ghissassi FE, Benbrahim-Tallaa L, Guha N, Mattock H, Straif K; International Agency for Research on Cancer Monograph Working Group. Carcinogenicity of consumption of red and processed meat. Lancet Oncol. 2015;16(16):1599-1600. doi:10.1016/S1470-2045(15)00444-1. PMID 26514947.
- Read: **NOT READ.** No abstract (it is a two-page news summary); the Lancet page returned 403. Author list as printed in the Europe PMC record. The numbers below are taken from IARC's own press release and Q&A instead, which say the same thing.

**IARC Press Release No. 240, 26 October 2015**

- https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/pr240_E.pdf. Read: whole PDF, text extracted.
- Verbatim: "a Working Group of 22 experts from 10 countries convened by the IARC Monographs Programme classified the consumption of red meat as probably carcinogenic to humans (Group 2A), based on limited evidence that the consumption of red meat causes cancer in humans and strong mechanistic evidence supporting a carcinogenic effect."
- Verbatim: "Processed meat was classified as carcinogenic to humans (Group 1), based on sufficient evidence in humans that the consumption of processed meat causes colorectal cancer."
- Verbatim: "The experts concluded that each 50 gram portion of processed meat eaten daily increases the risk of colorectal cancer by 18%."
- Straif, verbatim: "For an individual, the risk of developing colorectal cancer because of their consumption of processed meat remains small, but this risk increases with the amount of meat consumed,"
- Verbatim: "The IARC Working Group considered more than 800 studies".
- Wild, verbatim: "At the same time, red meat has nutritional value."

**IARC Q&A (2015 PDF)**

- https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/Monographs-QA_Vol114.pdf. Read: whole PDF, text extracted.
- Verbatim: "Limited evidence means that a positive association has been observed between exposure to the agent and cancer but that other explanations for the observations (technically termed chance, bias, or confounding) could not be ruled out."
- On tobacco and asbestos, verbatim: "No, processed meat has been classified in the same category as causes of cancer such as tobacco smoking and asbestos (IARC Group 1, carcinogenic to humans), but this does NOT mean that they are all equally dangerous. The IARC classifications describe the strength of the scientific evidence about an agent being a cause of cancer, rather than assessing the level of risk."
- Verbatim: "An analysis of data from 10 studies estimated that every 50 gram portion of processed meat eaten daily increases the risk of colorectal cancer by about 18%." (Note "about 18%" here vs "18%" in the press release.) Red meat: "if the association of red meat and colorectal cancer were proven to be causal, data from the same studies suggest that the risk of colorectal cancer could increase by 17% for every 100 gram portion of red meat eaten daily."
- Burden, verbatim: "about 34 000 cancer deaths per year worldwide are attributable to diets high in processed meat." And, for red meat if causal, "50 000 cancer deaths per year worldwide". Comparison, verbatim: "These numbers contrast with about 1 million cancer deaths per year globally due to tobacco smoking, 600 000 per year due to alcohol consumption, and more than 200 000 per year due to air pollution."
- Cooking, verbatim: "there were not enough data for the IARC Working Group to reach a conclusion about whether the way meat is cooked affects the risk of cancer."

**WHO Q&A page (UPDATED 23 September 2026, i.e. yesterday)** (NEW)

- https://www.who.int/news-room/questions-and-answers/item/cancer-carcinogenicity-of-the-consumption-of-red-meat-and-processed-meat. Read: whole page as text. **Page date: "23 September 2026 | Questions and answers".**
- It keeps the classification wording and the 18% / 17% figures, but differs from the 2015 IARC PDF in ways a lesson should know:
  - It **no longer contains** the tobacco/asbestos Q&A or the 34,000 / 50,000 deaths figures (searched the page text: zero matches for "tobacco", "asbestos", "34 000").
  - New, verbatim: "The risk increases with the amount of red or processed meat consumed, but the data available for evaluation did not permit a conclusion at the time of the original assessment about whether a safe level exists. WHO is currently developing a guideline on the optimal intake of animal-source foods, which will include recommendations on the amount of red and processed meat to consume."
  - New, verbatim: "Meat can be part of a healthy diet and provides nutrients that are important for good health. However, many national dietary guidelines recommend limiting processed meat and red meat because higher intakes are linked to an increased risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes and some cancers."
  - Verbatim: "Group 2A is the second highest hazard classification, meaning there is evidence of carcinogenicity but that it is not definitive."
- Implication: a WHO guideline on animal-source foods is in development and may land during the life of the course. Flag in QUEUE-type notes to recheck.

#### 4.2 NutriRECS 2019 (Johnston BC et al., Annals of Internal Medicine)

**The guideline**

- Johnston BC, Zeraatkar D, Han MA, et al. Unprocessed Red Meat and Processed Meat Consumption: Dietary Guideline Recommendations From the Nutritional Recommendations (NutriRECS) Consortium. Ann Intern Med. 2019;171(10):756-764. doi:10.7326/M19-1621. PMID 31569235.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC. Full text blocked (403).
- Note: the abstract begins, verbatim, "This article has been corrected. The original version (PDF) is appended to this article as a Supplement." All five papers carry this line.
- Recommendations, verbatim: "The panel suggests that adults continue current unprocessed red meat consumption (weak recommendation, low-certainty evidence). Similarly, the panel suggests adults continue current processed meat consumption (weak recommendation, low-certainty evidence)."
- Verbatim: "A panel of 14 members, including 3 community members, from 7 countries voted on the final recommendations. Strict criteria limited the conflicts of interest among panel members. Considerations of environmental impact or animal welfare did not bear on the recommendations."
- Funding: "Primary funding source: None."

**The four evidence reviews and the values review (all Ann Intern Med 2019;171(10); abstracts read via Europe PMC)**

1. Zeraatkar D et al. Red and Processed Meat Consumption and Risk for All-Cause Mortality and Cardiometabolic Outcomes: cohort SR/MA. pp 703-710. doi:10.7326/M19-0655. 61 articles, 55 cohorts, over 4 million participants. Verbatim: "Low-certainty evidence was found that a reduction in unprocessed red meat intake of 3 servings per week is associated with a very small reduction in risk for cardiovascular mortality, stroke, myocardial infarction (MI), and type 2 diabetes." Conclusion: "The magnitude of association between red and processed meat consumption and all-cause mortality and adverse cardiometabolic outcomes is very small, and the evidence is of low certainty."
2. Han MA et al. Reduction of Red and Processed Meat Intake and Cancer Mortality and Incidence: cohort SR/MA. pp 711-720. doi:10.7326/M19-0699. 118 articles, 56 cohorts, over 6 million participants. Verbatim: "each intake reduction of 3 servings of processed meat per week was associated with very small decreases in overall cancer mortality over a lifetime; prostate cancer mortality; and incidence of esophageal, colorectal, and breast cancer."
3. Zeraatkar D et al. Effect of Lower Versus Higher Red Meat Intake on Cardiometabolic and Cancer Outcomes: SR of RCTs. pp 721-731. doi:10.7326/M19-0622. Verbatim: "Of 12 eligible trials, a single trial enrolling 48 835 women provided the most credible, though still low-certainty, evidence that diets lower in red meat may have little or no effect on all-cause mortality (hazard ratio [HR], 0.99 [95% CI, 0.95 to 1.03])". Colorectal cancer "(HR, 1.04 [CI, 0.90 to 1.20])". Own limitation: "There were few trials, most addressing only surrogate outcomes, with heterogeneous comparators and small gradients in red meat consumption between lower versus higher intake groups." (The single trial is the Women's Health Initiative dietary modification trial per the Harvard critique below; the abstract does not name it.)
4. Vernooij RWM et al. Patterns of Red and Processed Meat Consumption and Risk for Cardiometabolic and Cancer Outcomes: cohort SR/MA. pp 732-741. doi:10.7326/M19-1583. Verbatim: "Low- or very-low-certainty evidence suggests that dietary patterns with less red and processed meat intake may result in very small reductions in adverse cardiometabolic and cancer outcomes."
5. Valli C et al. Health-Related Values and Preferences Regarding Meat Consumption: A Mixed-Methods Systematic Review. pp 742-755. doi:10.7326/M19-1326. Verbatim: "Low-certainty evidence suggests that omnivores are attached to meat and are unwilling to change this behavior when faced with potentially undesirable health effects."

(First authors verified from Europe PMC records. Note from the author lists: Patrick J Stover, dean of Texas A&M AgriLife, is an author of the guideline paper; John Sievenpiper is an author of the Vernooij patterns review, and later publicly disagreed with the guideline, see 4.3.)

- My summary of the NutriRECS logic: the reviews find the same direction of association as everyone else, but rate it "very small" per 3 servings a week and "low certainty" under GRADE (because the evidence is observational), and the panel then weighs this against people's stated attachment to meat. The recommendation follows from the grading and the values review, not from a finding of no association.

**Supporting editorial (NOT READ)**

- Carroll AE, Doherty TS. Meat Consumption and Health: Food for Thought. Ann Intern Med. 2019;171(10):767-768. doi:10.7326/M19-2620. No abstract; page blocked. From memory only: it defended the reviews and argued the absolute risk reductions are small. Do not quote until read.

#### 4.3 The criticism

**Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, The Nutrition Source, 30 September 2019**

- "New “guidelines” say continue red meat consumption habits, but recommendations contradict evidence". https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/2019/09/30/flawed-guidelines-red-processed-meat/. Read: whole page as text. Contains a correction note dated 2 October 2019.
- Verbatim: "The new guidelines are not justified as they contradict the evidence generated from their own meta-analyses ."
- On GRADE, verbatim: "This should come as no surprise, since GRADE criteria were mainly developed for evaluating evidence from drug trials. Unlike drugs, dietary, lifestyle, and environmental factors are typically not amenable to large, long-term randomized clinical trials."
- On effect size, verbatim: "First, the effect estimates may seem small because the unit of exposure (3 servings/week) is small." And: "dietary patterns with a moderate reduction in red and processed meat consumption were associated with lower total mortality by 13% (95% Confidence interval 8% to 18%), CVD mortality by 14% (6% to 21%), cancer mortality by 11% (4% to 17%), and type 2 diabetes risk by 24% (14% to 32%)."
- On the RCT review, verbatim: "The authors discarded one of these studies (Lyon Heart Study) and based their conclusions almost entirely on the other study, the Women’s Health Initiative, but this was a trial of a low fat dietary pattern, not red meat reduction."
- On the panel, verbatim: "these recommendations are not based on consensus of the panel because three panel members actually voted against their own recommendations."
- Sievenpiper (co-author of one of the reviews), quoted on the page, verbatim: "Unfortunately, the leadership of the paper chose to play up the low certainty of evidence by GRADE as opposed to the protective associations that directly support current recommendations to lower meat intake…"

**Qian F, Riddle MC, Wylie-Rosett J, Hu FB. 2020 Diabetes Care**

- Red and Processed Meats and Health Risks: How Strong Is the Evidence? Diabetes Care. 2020;43(2):265-271. doi:10.2337/dci19-0063. PMID 31959642. PMCID PMC6971786.
- Read: abstract via Europe PMC.
- Verbatim: "These recommendations are not justified, in large part because of the flawed methodologies used to review and grade nutritional evidence."
- Verbatim: "Applying NutriGRADE, the evidence on the positive association between red and processed meats and type 2 diabetes was rated to be of "high quality," while the evidence on the association between red and processed meats and mortality was rated to be of "moderate quality." Another important limitation is that inadequate attention was paid to what might be replacing red meat, be it plant-based proteins, refined carbohydrates, or other foods."

**The undisclosed-conflict controversy**

- Correction notice: Correction: Nutritional Recommendations (NutriRECS) on Consumption of Red and Processed Meat. Ann Intern Med. 2020;172(3):228. doi:10.7326/L19-0822. PMID 31887739. **NOT READ** (403). Search-summary level only: a 31 December 2019 correction in which Johnston acknowledged he had not listed a Texas A&M AgriLife Research grant received within the 36-month disclosure window (the grant funded research on saturated and polyunsaturated fats).
- News reporting read in full: Washington Post story syndicated in the Grand Forks Herald, "Research group that discounted risks of red meat has ties to program backed by beef industry" (https://www.grandforksherald.com/lifestyle/research-group-that-discounted-risks-of-red-meat-has-ties-to-program-backed-by-beef-industry). Undated on the text I extracted; content places it in late 2019. Verbatim points:
  - "In April, Johnston announced that the Agriculture and Life Sciences (AgriLife) program at Texas A&M would join the NutriRECS consortium and provide "generous support" to NutriRECS."
  - Stover (AgriLife dean and NutriRECS author), verbatim: "AgriLife has done $170 million in research in 2019, $4.5 million in beef, half of that from federal sources like the USDA, the other half from industry groups. That's a small part of our portfolio," and the article reports he said the meat study work was completed before the new funding.
  - On the earlier sugar paper: "Johnston had not disclosed a conflict of interest on a similar study in 2016 in the Annals of Internal Medicine that aimed to debunk sugar consumption's association with health risks. The study had been paid for by the International Life Sciences Institute".
