## Part E: Dietary patterns and national guidelines, protein, fibre, plant-based and low-carb diets

Research notes for "Nutrition: The Evidence", Stage 1. Compiled 2026-09-24.

### How these notes were gathered (read this first)

- **Abstracts** were fetched with curl from the Europe PMC REST API (`resultType=core`) and
  pasted whole into Appendix A. Only HTML formatting tags were stripped; the text is otherwise as
  returned. Every quotation from an abstract below can be checked against Appendix A.
- **Guideline documents** were fetched with curl and converted to text locally (pdftotext for
  PDFs, a small HTML-stripping script for pages). Quotations from them are copied from that local
  text. Where curl was blocked (dietaryguidelines.gov, hhs.gov, canada.ca, ods.od.nih.gov all
  returned 403 or refused), I used an Internet Archive (web.archive.org) copy and say so.
- **WebFetch / WebSearch summaries** were used only to locate things. Anything that rests on a
  search summary rather than a document I read is marked **"search-summary level, not verified"**.
- Every sentence not in quotation marks is my summary.
- Local copies of all fetched documents are in `scratchpad/research/E_abs/` (DGA2025.pdf,
  SciFound.pdf, DGA2020.pdf, ukrec.pdf, kdigo.pdf, evert.txt, soc26 html, and so on) if a later
  stage needs to re-check a quote.

---

### 1. National guidelines

#### 1.1 Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2020-2025 (USDA and HHS, December 2020)

- **Citation:** U.S. Department of Agriculture and U.S. Department of Health and Human Services.
  *Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2020-2025.* 9th Edition. December 2020. 164 pp.
  URL: https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/sites/default/files/2020-12/Dietary_Guidelines_for_Americans_2020-2025.pdf
- **What I read:** dietaryguidelines.gov blocked curl (Akamai "Access Denied"). I downloaded the
  PDF from the Internet Archive (`web.archive.org/web/2024id_/...`), confirmed the PDF title
  metadata ("Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2020-2025", 164 pages), and read the Executive
  Summary (pp. viii-x) and the adult fibre section in full.
- **Design:** federal policy guideline based on the 2020 DGAC scientific report.
- **The four Guidelines, verbatim headings (Executive Summary, p. ix-x):**
  - "Make every bite count with the Dietary Guidelines for Americans."
  - 1: "Follow a healthy dietary pattern at every life stage."
  - 2: "Customize and enjoy nutrient-dense food and beverage choices to reflect personal
    preferences, cultural traditions, and budgetary considerations."
  - 3: "Focus on meeting food group needs with nutrient-dense foods and beverages, and stay within
    calorie limits."
  - 4: "Limit foods and beverages higher in added sugars, saturated fat, and sodium, and limit
    alcoholic beverages."
- **Core elements, verbatim:** "Vegetables of all types—dark green; red and orange; beans, peas,
  and lentils; starchy; and other vegetables"; "Fruits, especially whole fruit"; "Grains, at least
  half of which are whole grain"; "Dairy, including fat-free or low-fat milk, yogurt, and cheese,
  and/or lactose-free versions and fortified soy beverages and yogurt as alternatives"; "Protein
  foods, including lean meats, poultry, and eggs; seafood; beans, peas, and lentils; and nuts,
  seeds, and soy products"; "Oils, including vegetable oils and oils in food, such as seafood and
  nuts".
- **Limits, verbatim:**
  - "Added sugars—Less than 10 percent of calories per day starting at age 2. Avoid foods and
    beverages with added sugars for those younger than age 2."
  - "Saturated fat—Less than 10 percent of calories per day starting at age 2."
  - "Sodium—Less than 2,300 milligrams per day—and even less for children younger than age 14."
  - "Alcoholic beverages—Adults of legal drinking age can choose not to drink, or to drink in
    moderation by limiting intake to 2 drinks or less in a day for men and 1 drink or less in a
    day for women, when alcohol is consumed. Drinking less is better for health than drinking
    more."
- **Fibre:** "More than 90 percent of women and 97 percent of men do not meet recommended intakes
  for dietary fiber." The fibre goal is in its Appendix 1 (I did not extract the table cleanly).
  The ADA Standards of Care 2026 (section 7 below) uses the same DRI-based figure: "at least 14 g
  fiber per 1,000 kcal".
- **Protein:** the 2020-2025 DGA does not state a g/kg figure itself (a text search for "g/kg" and
  "0.8" found none). It refers to the Dietary Reference Intakes. The 0.8 g/kg/day RDA comes from
  the Institute of Medicine 2005 DRI report (NOT READ directly: the National Academies Press reader
  pages would not render as text). A secondary source I did read states it: the ACC expert
  analysis (1.2 below) speaks of "the current Recommended Dietary Allowance set by the National
  Academy of Sciences (NAS) from 0.8 g/kg of body weight per day in healthy adults". The HHS
  framing that the 2020-2025 DGAs "recommended 0.8 g" is loose: that number is the RDA, a minimum
  that covers nearly all healthy people, not a DGA target.

#### 1.2 Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025-2030 (released 7 January 2026)

**This is the biggest recent change in the topic and has been contested. Handle with care.**

- **Citation:** U.S. Department of Health and Human Services and U.S. Department of Agriculture.
  *Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025-2030.* January 2026. 10 pp. (plus cover).
  URL: https://cdn.realfood.gov/DGA.pdf (linked from https://realfood.gov/).
  PDF metadata: created 6 January 2026. Final page dated "January 2026". Signed by Robert F.
  Kennedy, Jr. (HHS) and Brooke L. Rollins (USDA).
- **Companion document:** *The Scientific Foundation for the Dietary Guidelines for Americans,
  2025-2030.* 90 pp. URL: https://cdn.realfood.gov/Scientific%20Report_508.pdf (PDF modified
  15 January 2026). Appendices: https://cdn.realfood.gov/Scientific%20Report%20Appendices_FINAL_1.28.26.pdf
  (not read).
- **What I read:** the whole DGA PDF (all 10 pages, via pdftotext); the realfood.gov home page in
  full; the Scientific Foundation front matter (pp. i-iv, the "Concerns with the DGAC Report" and
  the accepted/rejected table), the author list and the disclosure section (pp. ix-xi).
- **Release date:** 7 January 2026 (the AHA statement below is datelined "January 7, 2026" and
  responds to "today's release"; Healio and ADA News also date it 7 January, search-summary
  level).
- **Opening, verbatim:** "These Guidelines mark the most significant reset of federal nutrition
  policy in our nation's history. The message is simple: eat real food."
- **Key recommendations, verbatim (headings and the numeric lines):**
  - "Prioritize Protein Foods at Every Meal". "Consume a variety of protein foods from animal
    sources, including eggs, poultry, seafood, and red meat, as well as a variety of
    plant-sourced protein foods, including beans, peas, lentils, legumes, nuts, seeds, and soy."
    "Protein serving goals: 1.2–1.6 grams of protein per kilogram of body weight per day,
    adjusting as needed based on your individual caloric requirements."
  - "Consume Dairy". "When consuming dairy, include full-fat dairy with no added sugars." "Dairy
    serving goals: 3 servings per day as part of a 2,000-calorie dietary pattern".
  - "Eat Vegetables & Fruits Throughout the Day". "Vegetables: 3 servings per day"; "Fruits: 2
    servings per day". "100% fruit or vegetable juice should be consumed in limited portions or
    diluted with water."
  - "Incorporate Healthy Fats". "Healthy fats are plentiful in many whole foods, such as meats,
    poultry, eggs, omega-3–rich seafood, nuts, seeds, full-fat dairy, olives, and avocados."
    "When cooking with or adding fats to meals, prioritize oils with essential fatty acids, such
    as olive oil. Other options can include butter or beef tallow." "In general, saturated fat
    consumption should not exceed 10% of total daily calories. Significantly limiting highly
    processed foods will help meet this goal. More high-quality research is needed to determine
    which types of dietary fats best support long-term health."
  - "Focus on Whole Grains". "Prioritize fiber-rich whole grains." "Whole grains serving goals:
    2–4 servings per day".
  - "Limit Highly Processed Foods, Added Sugars, & Refined Carbohydrates". "Avoid highly
    processed packaged, prepared, ready-to-eat, or other foods that are salty or sweet, such as
    chips, cookies, and candy that have added sugars and sodium (salt)." "Avoid sugar-sweetened
    beverages, such as sodas, fruit drinks, and energy drinks." "While no amount of added sugars
    or non-nutritive sweeteners is recommended or considered part of a healthy or nutritious
    diet, one meal should contain no more than 10 grams of added sugars."
  - "Limit Alcoholic Beverages". "Consume less alcohol for better overall health." (No numeric
    drink limit, unlike 2020-2025.)
  - Sodium: "The general population, ages 14 and above, should consume less than 2,300 mg per day
    of sodium. Highly active individuals may benefit from increased sodium intake to offset sweat
    losses."
  - Middle childhood (5-10): "No amount of added sugars is recommended."
  - Chronic disease: "Individuals with certain chronic diseases may experience improved health
    outcomes when following a lower carbohydrate diet."
  - Vegetarians and vegans: "Pay careful attention to potential nutrient gaps when consuming a
    vegetarian or vegan diet." and "prioritize targeted supplementation, diversify plant protein
    sources for amino acid balance".
- **realfood.gov framing, verbatim:** "We are ending the war on protein. Every meal must
  prioritize high-quality, nutrient-dense protein from both animal and plant sources, paired with
  healthy fats from whole foods such as eggs, seafood, meats, full-fat dairy, nuts, seeds, olives,
  and avocados." "For the first time, official U.S. guidance calls on Americans to avoid highly
  processed food." The graphic is called "the New Pyramid"; the FAQ says it "prioritizes
  nutrient-dense foods and reduces reliance on highly processed products".
- **The graphic:** a pyramid replaces MyPlate. The ACC analysis says it, "although meant to be a
  symmetric triangle, can also be misinterpreted as an inverted food pyramid with steak, meat,
  ground meat, whole milk, and cheese, along with fruits and vegetables, at the top". Harvard's
  Nutrition Source calls it "a pyramid-like graphic—this time flipped on its head". (I did not
  view the image itself; these are descriptions.)

**What changed from 2020-2025 (my summary, each point checked against both PDFs):**

| Item | 2020-2025 | 2025-2030 |
|---|---|---|
| Visual | MyPlate | "New Pyramid" |
| Protein | no g/kg target in the DGA; lean meats listed | 1.2-1.6 g/kg/day, "at every meal", animal sources listed first |
| Dairy | "fat-free or low-fat" | "include full-fat dairy with no added sugars" |
| Fats | oils, "vegetable oils" | olive oil named; "butter or beef tallow" as options |
| Saturated fat | < 10% of calories | "should not exceed 10%" (kept), plus "more high-quality research is needed" |
| Added sugars | < 10% of calories; none under 2 | "no amount ... is recommended"; ≤10 g per meal; none for children to age 10 per the life-stage text |
| Processed food | not a named category | "Avoid highly processed" foods (first time) |
| Alcohol | ≤2 drinks men, ≤1 women | "Consume less alcohol"; no number |
| Sodium | < 2,300 mg | < 2,300 mg (kept), plus a line on active people |
| Low-carb | not mentioned in the Guidelines | "lower carbohydrate diet" may help "certain chronic diseases" |
| Length | 164 pp. | 10 pp. plus a 90 pp. Scientific Foundation |

**Did it follow the 2025 Dietary Guidelines Advisory Committee report? No, by its own account.**

- The Scientific Report of the 2025 DGAC was published 10 December 2024 (dietaryguidelines.gov;
  Zoë Harcombe's page gives the date and 421 pages; search-summary level for the date).
- The Scientific Foundation says, verbatim: the Administration "implemented an independent
  evidence review process to address and correct deficiencies identified in the Scientific Report
  of the 2025 Dietary Guidelines Advisory Committee (DGAC Report), which framed its analysis
  through a health equity lens." And: "the Trump Administration determined that adopting the DGAC
  Report would not meet the American public's need for objective, evidence-based nutrition
  guidance."
- Its stated objections, verbatim: "The Committee consistently advocated plant-based dietary
  patterns, deprioritized animal-sourced proteins, and favored high linoleic acid vegetable oils."
  "the DGAC proposed reorganizing protein food subgroups to prioritize beans, peas, and lentils
  while listing meats, poultry, and eggs last—a symbolic reordering lacking scientific
  justification." It also faults the DGAC for "continued longstanding recommendations for low-fat
  dairy and butter replacement" and because it "did not recommend clear limits" on highly
  processed foods.
- Its table marks these DGAC recommendations as **not** implemented: "Modify the dietary pattern
  to emphasize dietary intakes of beans, peas, and lentils while reducing intakes of red and
  processed meats"; the reordering of protein subgroups; "Continue to emphasize consumption of
  low-fat or nonfat dairy and unsaturated fats"; and that replacement of saturated fat "should
  focus on plant-based sources". Marked **partial**: "limit total saturated fat intake to less
  than 10% of calories"; "Limit consumption of red and processed meats, foods high in saturated
  fat, and salty/savory snacks". Marked **yes**: moving beans, peas and lentils into the Protein
  Foods group.
- Process: reviewers "were selected through a federal contracting process"; NIH's Office of
  Nutrition Research "coordinated an external peer review process, assigning two independent
  reviewers to each scientific review". Data compiled by Christopher Ramsden (NIA). Named review
  authors: Ty Beal, Benjamin Bikman, J. Thomas Brenna, Michael Goran, Heather Leidy, Ameer Taha,
  Jeff Volek, Daisy Zamora, Donald Layman. The document publishes disclosures; for example
  Brenna lists "National Cattlemen's Beef Association/ Texas Beef Council. Consulting and research
  grant." and a General Mills panel, among others. (Volek is also an author of the Virta trial in
  section 7.)
- The ACC analysis confirms independently that the 1.2-1.6 g/kg protein target was "a shift not
  included in the 2025 DGAC report".

**Reactions from named bodies and scientists (all read in full unless marked):**

- **American Heart Association**, statement, 7 January 2026 (newsroom.heart.org, read in full).
  Praise: "The American Heart Association welcomes the new dietary guidelines and commends the
  inclusion of several important science-based recommendations, notably the emphasis on
  increasing intake of vegetables, fruits and whole grains while limiting consumption of added
  sugars, refined grains, highly processed foods, saturated fats and sugary drinks." Concern: "we
  are concerned that recommendations regarding salt seasoning and red meat consumption could
  inadvertently lead consumers to exceed recommended limits for sodium and saturated fats, which
  are primary drivers of cardiovascular disease." And: "we encourage consumers to prioritize
  plant-based proteins, seafood and lean meats and to limit high-fat animal products including
  red meat, butter, lard and tallow, which are linked to increased cardiovascular risk." The AHA
  said it would "issue updated guidance later this year". Search results report a 2026 AHA
  Dietary Guidance scientific statement released 31 March 2026 recommending a shift from meat to
  plant protein (search-summary level, not verified; not read).
- **American College of Cardiology**, expert analysis by the Prevention of CV Disease Section's
  Nutrition & Lifestyle Work Group (Aggarwal M, Aspry KE, Kris-Etherton PM, Freeman AM, Williams
  KA Sr, Gianos E), acc.org, 27 January 2026 (read in full). Key lines: the DGA "include both
  evidence-based advances and some recommendations that diverge from those of the 2025 Dietary
  Guideline Advisory Committee." "Some of these recommendations are not evidence based. Data from
  feeding trials and/or cohort studies show that saturated fats, especially butter, and beef fat
  to a lesser extent, raise low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) levels compared with olive
  oil". "In short, evidence from feeding trials and/or cohort studies does not support promotion
  of butter or beef tallow. Moreover, if the newly recommended servings of animal protein foods
  (e.g., meats and whole-fat dairy) and proposed 'healthy fats' (e.g., butter and beef tallow) are
  incorporated into the diet, saturated fat intake will exceed 10% of daily calories." They also
  note that on dairy fat "dairy food consumption has not been associated with increased CV risk",
  so the picture differs by food.
- **American Society for Nutrition**, 9 January 2026 (nutrition.org, read in full). Praise: "ASN
  supports the broad eating pattern ... that emphasizes minimally processed, nutrient-dense foods
  and limits added sugar, sodium, and saturated fat". Concern: "departing from the established
  scientific review process undermines confidence in the DGAs and nutrition science". "There is a
  lack of transparency regarding the methods, approaches, and objectives of the newly introduced
  scientific reviews and the timeframe in which they were completed." And on saturated fat: "they
  also appear to encourage greater consumption of certain foods high in saturated fats such as
  butter and beef tallow, among others."
- **American Nutrition Association**, 9 January 2026 (theana.org, read in full). Praise: "The
  increased attention to fiber intake, microbiome health, and the role of lowered carbohydrates in
  reversing some chronic diseases marks a meaningful advancement". Concern: "the placement of red
  meat and dairy products at the 'top of the chart', appearing above foods representing a
  Mediterranean diet, which has the largest body of evidence in preventing and treating chronic
  disease."
- **Frank Hu** (chair, Department of Nutrition, Harvard T.H. Chan School), quoted in The
  Nutrition Source, 9 January 2026 (read in full): "I think the new Guidelines move in the right
  direction by reinforcing the importance of reducing added sugars and cutting back on refined
  grains and other highly processed foods ... However, there appear to be several contradictions
  within the DGAs and between the DGAs and the new pyramid." On protein: "Substantially raising
  overall protein intake without distinguishing between different protein sources may have
  unintended long-term health implications". The same piece works through a 2,000 kcal day: three
  full-fat dairy servings reach about 17 g saturated fat against a roughly 22 g limit, before
  any butter or tallow.
- **Deirdre Tobias** (Harvard; member of the 2025 DGAC), quoted there from a Harvard Chan News
  Q&A: "As of today, there has not been transparency in who wrote the new DGAs." (I read the
  quote in The Nutrition Source, not the original Q&A.)
- **Supporters' side, from its own proponents:** Nina Teicholz (science journalist, PhD 2024,
  founder of the Nutrition Coalition, author of *The Big Fat Surprise*) has argued for years that
  the DGA process leaned on outdated and modelled evidence. From the Nutrition Coalition page I
  read: the guidelines "cannot be guaranteed to reflect trustworthy advice for the urgent task of
  combating obesity, diabetes or any other chronic disease" and "on key topics—including dietary
  fat, saturated fat, and cholesterol—we concluded that the guidelines are no longer supported by
  the science, or are simply in error." She cites NASEM's 2017 reports calling on the process to
  "improve transparency" and "strengthen scientific rigor". *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 09's
  Stage 4:* the page is a signed post, "Dietary Guidelines are Unscientific, Outdated", "Written By
  Nina Teicholz", dated 8 September 2022 in its metadata (`datePublished` 2022-09-08), and it is
  about the guidelines then in force: the sentence opens "In plain terms, we found that the U.S.
  guidelines", reporting a paper she co-authored. It is not a comment on the 2025-2030 edition.
  Zoë Harcombe (PhD, low-carb
  proponent) wrote on 2026-01 (read) that the new DGAs "bore almost no resemblance to the
  Scientific Report which preceded them" and welcomed the change as a "monumental change". The
  administration's own case is the Scientific Foundation text quoted above. Note that the ASN,
  which criticised the process, also said it "supports the respected ASN members who served as
  scientific review authors".
- **Journal of Nutrition**: "A Critical Perspective on the Dietary Guidelines for Americans
  2025–2030: Methodological, Conceptual, and Communication Challenges" (J Nutr, 2026, article
  S0022-3166(26)00202-6). NOT READ: the page returned only a stub to curl. Worth fetching in
  Stage 1 proper.
- **Where both sides agree (my summary):** less added sugar, fewer refined grains and highly
  processed foods, more vegetables, fruit and whole grains, saturated fat cap kept at 10%. The
  disputes are protein amount and source, full-fat dairy, butter and tallow, the graphic, the
  missing alcohol number, and the process.
- **What would settle it:** long-term trials with hard outcomes comparing an animal-protein-led
  "real food" pattern against a plant-protein-led one at equal processing level and equal
  saturated fat cap. These do not exist; PREDIMED and CORDIOPREV are the nearest.

#### 1.3 UK: the Eatwell Guide (Public Health England 2016; now OHID) and NHS page

- **Citations:** Public Health England. *The Eatwell Guide.* 2016. GOV.UK page
  https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-eatwell-guide ("Last updated 2 January 2024",
  now published by the Office for Health Improvement and Disparities). NHS, "The Eatwell Guide",
  https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/eat-well/food-guidelines-and-food-labels/the-eatwell-guide/
  (page metadata dateModified 2025-10-09). PHE, *Government Dietary Recommendations*, August 2016
  (PDF on the same GOV.UK page).
- **What I read:** the NHS page in full; the GOV.UK index page; the Government Dietary
  Recommendations PDF tables 1-2 and their footnotes.
- **NHS page, verbatim:** "Eat at least 5 portions of a variety of fruit and vegetables a day";
  "They should make up just over a third of the food we eat each day." "Base meals on potatoes,
  bread, rice, pasta or other starchy carbohydrates"; "Starchy foods should make up just over a
  third of the food we eat. Choose higher fibre or wholegrain varieties". "Choose lean cuts of
  meat and mince, and eat less red and processed meat like bacon, ham and sausages." "Aim for at
  least 2 portions (2 x 140g) of fish every week, 1 of which should be oily". Dairy: "Try to go
  for lower-fat and lower-sugar products where possible". Fats: "Choose unsaturated oils and
  spreads, and eat in small amounts". Foods high in fat, salt and sugar "include chocolate, cakes,
  biscuits, sugary soft drinks, butter, ghee and ice cream." Fluids: "the government recommends 6
  to 8 cups or glasses a day." Fruit juice: "no more than a combined total of 150ml a day."
- **Government Dietary Recommendations, adults 19-64 (table 2):** fibre 30 g/day; salt less than
  6 g/day; free sugars less than 33 g (men) / 27 g (women), calculated at "5% food energy";
  saturated fat less than 31 g / 24 g, calculated at "11% food energy"; carbohydrate at least
  333 g / 267 g, at "50% total dietary energy"; protein 55.5 g / 45.0 g. Footnote on salt: "These
  target salt intakes do not represent ideal or optimum consumption levels, but achievable
  population goals." Fibre is from "SACN Carbohydrate and Health report (2015)".
- SACN's 2019 *Saturated fats and health* report reaffirmed a saturated fat limit (I read only
  the GOV.UK summary page, which does not state the number; the wording "no more than about 10%
  of total dietary energy" is from memory and **not verified**).

#### 1.4 Canada's Food Guide (Health Canada, January 2019)

- **Citation:** Health Canada. *Canada's Food Guide.* 2019. https://food-guide.canada.ca/
- **What I read:** canada.ca refused curl; food-guide.canada.ca redirects to canada.ca. I read the
  Internet Archive 2025 snapshots of "Healthy eating recommendations" and "Food guide snapshot" in
  full.
- **Verbatim:** "Eat plenty of vegetables and fruits, whole grain foods and protein foods. Choose
  protein foods that come from plants more often." "Choose foods with healthy fats instead of
  saturated fat". "Limit highly processed foods. If you choose these foods, eat them less often
  and in small amounts." "Make water your drink of choice". "Be aware that food marketing can
  influence your choices". Also "Healthy eating is more than the foods you eat. It is also about
  where, when, why and how you eat."
- **Plate (snapshot text description, verbatim):** "On half of the plate are vegetables and
  fruits ... On one-quarter of the plate are protein foods (lean meat, chicken, variety of nuts
  and seeds, lentils, eggs, tofu, yogurt, fish, beans). On the remaining one-quarter of the plate
  are whole grain foods". Dairy is folded into protein foods; there is no separate dairy group.
- Note: the 2019 guide already told Canadians to "Limit highly processed foods" seven years
  before the 2025-2030 DGA, which claims "For the first time, official U.S. guidance" does so.
  The US claim is about US guidance, so it is not false, but it is not a world first.

#### 1.5 WHO "Healthy diet" fact sheet

- **Citation:** World Health Organization. *Healthy diet.* Fact sheet.
  https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet. Page metadata: published /
  modified **26 January 2026** (this is a recent revision, and now includes carbohydrate and
  protein ranges that older versions lacked).
- **What I read:** the page in full via curl.
- **Verbatim:**
  - "Healthy diets come in many forms, but the four core principles of adequacy, balance,
    moderation and diversity should be the foundation of any healthy diet."
  - Carbohydrate: "although low and very low carbohydrate diets are used to treat certain
    conditions, for most people a variety of unrefined carbohydrates should represent a
    significant portion of the diet, approximately 45–75% of total daily energy."
  - "Everyone older than 10 years of age should aim for at least 400 grams of fruits and
    vegetables per day".
  - "Everyone older than 10 years of age should also aim for a daily intake of at least 25 grams
    of naturally-occurring dietary fibre as found in foods".
  - "The consumption of free sugars should be limited to less than 10% of total daily energy
    intake ... Limiting further to 5% or less of total daily energy intake may provide additional
    health benefits." "Reducing consumption of free sugars ... should be accomplished without the
    use of non-sugar sweeteners."
  - "No more than 10% of total energy intake should come from saturated fat and no more than 1%
    of total energy from trans fat of any type." It lists replacing "butter, lard and ghee with
    oils rich in polyunsaturated fat, such as soybean, canola (rapeseed), corn, safflower and
    sunflower oils".
    *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 09's Stage 4,* the rest of that sentence, from the local copy of the
    page: fat intake "can be reduced by: steaming or boiling instead of frying when cooking;
    replacing butter, lard and ghee with oils rich in polyunsaturated fat, such as soybean, canola
    (rapeseed), corn, safflower and sunflower oils; eating reduced-fat dairy foods and lean meats, or
    trimming visible fat from meat".
  - Protein: "Protein intake at 10–15% of total daily energy intake is generally sufficient to
    meet the needs of adults (approximately 50–75 grams for a person of healthy body weight
    consuming about 2000 calories per day)." "Consuming excessive amounts of protein, however,
    can place a metabolic burden on the body, particularly the kidneys." "For many adults, a
    shift towards more plant-based sources of protein may bring health benefits, particularly
    when the shift is away from red meat."
  - Salt: "In adults, salt intake should be limited to less than 5 grams per day (2 grams per day
    sodium)."
  - Processed food: "diets containing significant amounts of highly processed foods, which are
    often high in sodium, sugar or unhealthy fats, are associated with negative health outcomes."

#### 1.6 Where they agree and where they differ

My summary table. "–" means the document I read does not give a figure.

| Item | DGA 2020-25 | DGA 2025-30 | UK Eatwell / PHE | Canada 2019 | WHO (Jan 2026) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vegetables and fruit | core, half grains whole | 3 veg + 2 fruit servings | 5 a day, just over 1/3 of food | half the plate | ≥400 g/day |
| Grains / starch | ≥ half whole | 2-4 whole-grain servings; cut refined | just over 1/3; choose wholegrain | 1/4 plate, whole grain | 45-75% energy from unrefined carbohydrate |
| Protein amount | RDA 0.8 g/kg (via DRI) | 1.2-1.6 g/kg | 55.5 g men / 45 g women | – | 10-15% energy |
| Protein source | lean meats first listed | animal sources first, red meat named | eat less red and processed meat; pulses | plants "more often" | shift to plant, away from red meat |
| Dairy | fat-free or low-fat | full-fat, 3 servings | lower-fat | inside protein foods | reduced-fat dairy |
| Saturated fat | <10% energy | ≤10% energy | 11% food energy (1991 DRV) | "instead of saturated fat" | <10% energy |
| Butter | limit | "butter or beef tallow" are options | limit (HFSS list) | – | replace with PUFA oils |
| Sugars | added <10% | no amount; ≤10 g per meal | free sugars 5% | limit | free sugars <10%, ideally <5% |
| Sweeteners | allowed | "limit"; "no amount ... recommended" | – | – | don't use to cut sugar |
| Sodium / salt | <2,300 mg Na | <2,300 mg Na | <6 g salt | limit | <5 g salt (2 g Na) |
| Fibre | DRI (14 g/1000 kcal) | "fiber-rich" | 30 g | – | ≥25 g |
| Processed foods | not a category | "avoid highly processed" | HFSS outside the guide | "limit highly processed foods" | "associated with negative health outcomes" |
| Alcohol | ≤2 / ≤1 drinks | "consume less" | – | – | – (not in this fact sheet) |
| Low-carb | – | may help "certain chronic diseases" | – | – | used for "certain conditions" |

Plain reading: on vegetables, fruit, whole grains, sugar, salt and processed food the five
documents agree closely. The 2025-2030 DGA is the outlier on protein quantity, full-fat dairy,
butter and tallow, and on having no alcohol number. The other four lean towards plant protein;
the new DGA lists animal protein first.

---

### 2. The Mediterranean diet

#### 2.1 PREDIMED, 2013 (retracted) and 2018 (republished)

- **Citations:** Estruch R, Ros E, Salas-Salvadó J, et al; PREDIMED Study Investigators. Primary
  prevention of cardiovascular disease with a Mediterranean diet. *N Engl J Med*
  2013;368(14):1279-1290. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1200303. PMID 23432189. **Retracted.**
  Estruch R, Ros E, Salas-Salvadó J, et al; PREDIMED Study Investigators. Primary Prevention of
  Cardiovascular Disease with a Mediterranean Diet Supplemented with Extra-Virgin Olive Oil or
  Nuts. *N Engl J Med* 2018;378(25):e34. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1800389. PMID 29897866.
  Retraction notice: "Retraction and Republication: Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease
  with a Mediterranean Diet" *N Engl J Med* 2018, doi:10.1056/NEJMc1806491, PMID 29897867 (no
  abstract; text NOT READ).
- **What I read:** both abstracts via Europe PMC (Appendix A); Harvard Nutrition Source
  explainer (22 June 2018) in full; search summaries on the retraction date.
- **Design:** multicentre RCT in Spain, 11 sites; 7447 people aged 55-80 at high cardiovascular
  risk, no CVD at entry, 57% women. Three arms: Med diet + extra-virgin olive oil, Med diet +
  mixed nuts, control (advice to reduce dietary fat). Median follow-up 4.8 years; stopped early
  at a prespecified interim analysis. Primary end point: MI, stroke or cardiovascular death.
- **2013 numbers:** 288 events. "The multivariable-adjusted hazard ratios were 0.70 (95%
  confidence interval [CI], 0.54 to 0.92) and 0.72 (95% CI, 0.54 to 0.96) for the group assigned
  to a Mediterranean diet with extra-virgin olive oil (96 events) and the group assigned to a
  Mediterranean diet with nuts (83 events), respectively, versus the control group (109 events)."
- **Why it was retracted (2018 abstract, verbatim):** "We subsequently identified protocol
  deviations, including enrollment of household members without randomization, assignment to a
  study group without randomization of some participants at 1 of 11 study sites, and apparent
  inconsistent use of randomization tables at another site. We have withdrawn our previously
  published report and now report revised effect estimates based on analyses that do not rely
  exclusively on the assumption that all the participants were randomly assigned." Harvard's
  explainer adds that at the one site "several clinics instead of individual patients" were
  allocated. NEJM retracted and republished on 13 June 2018 (Harvard page, read).
- **How it came to light:** Carlisle JB. Data fabrication and other reasons for non-random sampling
  in 5087 randomised, controlled trials in anaesthetic and general medical journals.
  *Anaesthesia* 2017;72(8):944-952. doi:10.1111/anae.13938 (abstract read, Appendix A). The
  abstract describes the method (testing whether baseline variables look randomly distributed)
  and does not name PREDIMED; the link of Carlisle's list to PREDIMED is from secondary sources
  (Hilda Bastian's PLOS blog and others, search-summary level, not verified from Carlisle's
  supplementary list). Carlisle himself lists "Fraud, unintentional error, correlation,
  stratified allocation and poor methodology" as possible causes; nobody has alleged fraud in
  PREDIMED.
- **2018 numbers:** events 96 (3.8%) olive oil, 83 (3.4%) nuts, 109 (4.4%) control. "the hazard
  ratio was 0.69 (95% confidence interval [CI], 0.53 to 0.91) for a Mediterranean diet with
  extra-virgin olive oil and 0.72 (95% CI, 0.54 to 0.95) for a Mediterranean diet with nuts, as
  compared with the control diet. Results were similar after the omission of 1588 participants
  whose study-group assignments were known or suspected to have departed from the protocol."
- **Limitations worth teaching (my summary):** the control group's fat intake barely changed, so
  it is really "Med diet with free olive oil or nuts vs usual diet with advice"; the effect was
  driven mainly by stroke; stopped early; absolute risk difference about 1 percentage point over
  about 5 years. The Cochrane review (2.4) grades PREDIMED's evidence on CVD and total mortality
  as low quality and "little or no effect", with a stroke reduction of moderate quality.
- **Lesson:** the conclusion survived the retraction, but it became an adjusted observational
  analysis for 21% of participants. A good example of self-correction and of why "randomised"
  must be checked, not assumed.

#### 2.2 Lyon Diet Heart Study (de Lorgeril 1999)

- **Citation:** de Lorgeril M, Salen P, Martin JL, Monjaud I, Delaye J, Mamelle N. Mediterranean
  diet, traditional risk factors, and the rate of cardiovascular complications after myocardial
  infarction: final report of the Lyon Diet Heart Study. *Circulation* 1999;99(6):779-785.
  doi:10.1161/01.CIR.99.6.779. PMID 9989963.
- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (Appendix A).
- **Design:** randomised secondary-prevention trial after a first MI; Mediterranean-type diet vs
  "prudent Western-type diet"; mean follow-up 46 months. Sample size is not in the abstract; the
  Cochrane review states "605 CHD patients". The intervention included a canola-based margarine
  rich in alpha-linolenic acid (Cochrane: "supplemental canola margarine").
- **Numbers:** "CO 1 was reduced (14 events versus 44 in the prudent Western-type diet group,
  P=0.0001), as were CO 2 (27 events versus 90, P=0.0001) and CO 3 (95 events versus 180, P=0.
  0002). Adjusted risk ratios ranged from 0.28 to 0.53." Cochrane's extraction: CVD mortality HR
  0.35 (0.15 to 0.82), total mortality HR 0.44 (0.21 to 0.92), graded low quality.
- **Limitations (my summary, partly from Cochrane):** small, unblinded, few events, an interim
  analysis already showed a "striking protective effect", effect sizes implausibly large for a
  diet, the diet was assessed in only part of the sample (not verified; this is from general
  knowledge, check before use).

#### 2.3 CORDIOPREV (Delgado-Lista 2022)

- **Citation:** Delgado-Lista J, Alcala-Diaz JF, Torres-Peña JD, et al; CORDIOPREV Investigators.
  Long-term secondary prevention of cardiovascular disease with a Mediterranean diet and a
  low-fat diet (CORDIOPREV): a randomised controlled trial. *Lancet* 2022;399(10338):1876-1885.
  doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(22)00122-2. PMID 35525255.
- **What I read:** abstract via Europe PMC (Appendix A).
- **Design:** single-centre RCT, Córdoba, Spain; 1002 patients with established coronary heart
  disease, aged 20-75, 82.5% men; Mediterranean vs low-fat diet; 7 years; clinicians masked,
  participants not.
- **Numbers:** primary endpoint in 87 (Med) vs 111 (low-fat). "crude rate per 1000 person-years:
  28·1 [95% CI 27·9-28·3] in the Mediterranean diet group vs 37·7 [37·5-37·9] in the low-fat
  group, log-rank p=0·039). Multivariable-adjusted hazard ratios (HRs) of the different models
  ranged from 0·719 (95% CI 0·541-0·957) to 0·753 (0·568-0·998) in favour of the Mediterranean
  diet." In men: 16.2% vs 22.8%, HR 0.669; "than in 175 women for whom no difference was found
  between groups."
- **Limitations:** single centre; the upper CI bound nearly touches 1; no effect shown in women
  (underpowered); funded partly by olive-oil foundations ("Fundacion Patrimonio Comunal Olivarero;
  Fundacion Centro para la Excelencia en Investigacion sobre Aceite de Oliva y Salud").

#### 2.4 Cochrane review for balance (Rees 2019)

- **Citation:** Rees K, Takeda A, Martin N, et al. Mediterranean-style diet for the primary and
  secondary prevention of cardiovascular disease. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2019;3:CD009825.
  doi:10.1002/14651858.CD009825.pub3.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Conclusion, verbatim:** "Despite the relatively large number of studies included in this
  review, there is still some uncertainty regarding the effects of a Mediterranean-style diet on
  clinical endpoints and CVD risk factors for both primary and secondary prevention." Predates
  CORDIOPREV.
- **Discrepancy to flag:** this abstract says "PREDIMED (7747 randomised)", but both NEJM papers
  say 7447. Probably a Cochrane typo; use 7447.

---

### 3. DASH (Appel 1997) and DASH-Sodium (Sacks 2001)

- **Citation:** Appel LJ, Moore TJ, Obarzanek E, et al; DASH Collaborative Research Group. A
  clinical trial of the effects of dietary patterns on blood pressure. *N Engl J Med*
  1997;336(16):1117-1124. doi:10.1056/NEJM199704173361601. PMID 9099655.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Design:** controlled feeding RCT; 459 adults with SBP <160 and DBP 80-95; three weeks run-in
  on control diet, then 8 weeks of control, fruits-and-vegetables, or "combination" diet. "Sodium
  intake and body weight were maintained at constant levels."
- **Numbers, verbatim:** "The combination diet reduced systolic and diastolic blood pressure by 5.5
  and 3.0 mm Hg more, respectively, than the control diet (P<0.001 for each)". In the 133 with
  hypertension: "11.4 and 5.5 mm Hg more". In the 326 without: "3.5 mm Hg (P<0.001) and 2.1 mm Hg
  (P=0.003)."
- **Why it matters for teaching:** feeding study, so adherence is near-certain; tests a pattern,
  not a nutrient; weight and sodium held constant, so the effect is not weight loss. Limitation:
  8 weeks, blood pressure only, not events.
- **DASH-Sodium:** Sacks FM, Svetkey LP, Vollmer WM, et al. Effects on blood pressure of reduced
  dietary sodium and the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) diet. *N Engl J Med*
  2001;344(1):3-10. doi:10.1056/NEJM200101043440101. Abstract read. 412 participants. "As compared
  with the control diet with a high sodium level, the DASH diet with a low sodium level led to a
  mean systolic blood pressure that was 7.1 mm Hg lower in participants without hypertension, and
  11.5 mm Hg lower in participants with hypertension." Effects were "greater in combination than
  singly."
- ADA 2026 Standards mention DASH4D (a feeding trial in type 2 diabetes and hypertension, 2021-24)
  as further support (see 7.3).

---

### 4. Fibre and whole grains (Reynolds 2019)

- **Citation:** Reynolds A, Mann J, Cummings J, Winter N, Mete E, Te Morenga L. Carbohydrate
  quality and human health: a series of systematic reviews and meta-analyses. *Lancet*
  2019;393(10170):434-445. doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(18)31809-9. PMID 30638909. Commissioned by WHO
  (funding line lists WHO).
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A). Full text not read (no PMC copy).
- **Design:** series of systematic reviews and meta-analyses; 185 prospective studies (about 135
  million person-years) and 58 trials (4635 adults). Excluded people with chronic disease.
- **Key numbers, verbatim:** "Observational data suggest a 15-30% decrease in all-cause and
  cardiovascular related mortality, and incidence of coronary heart disease, stroke incidence and
  mortality, type 2 diabetes, and colorectal cancer when comparing the highest dietary fibre
  consumers with the lowest consumers". "Risk reduction associated with a range of critical
  outcomes was greatest when daily intake of dietary fibre was between 25 g and 29 g."
  "Dose-response curves suggested that higher intakes of dietary fibre could confer even greater
  benefit". "Similar findings for whole grain intake were observed." "Smaller or no risk
  reductions were found ... when comparing the effects of diets characterised by low rather than
  higher glycaemic index or load."
- **Certainty, verbatim:** "moderate for dietary fibre, low to moderate for whole grains, and low
  to very low for dietary glycaemic index and glycaemic load."
- **Limitation stated:** "Our findings are limited to risk reduction in the population at large
  rather than those with chronic disease."
- **Teaching point:** the trials show risk-factor changes (weight, systolic BP, cholesterol); the
  hard outcomes are all observational. The authors argue causality from dose-response. This is
  the evidence behind the WHO's 25 g and the UK's 30 g.

---

### 5. Protein

#### 5.1 The RDA
- 0.8 g/kg/day for healthy adults, Institute of Medicine 2005 DRI report. **Primary NOT READ**
  (NAP pages did not yield text). Confirmed in secondary sources read: ACC analysis (1.2); PROT-AGE
  treats it as the baseline. UK equivalent: 55.5 g/45 g per day (PHE table). WHO: 10-15% of energy.
- The RDA is set to cover the needs of nearly all healthy people (97.5%); it is not an optimum
  and not a ceiling. (My summary of the standard DRI definition.)

#### 5.2 Morton 2018 meta-analysis (protein and resistance training)
- **Citation:** Morton RW, Murphy KT, McKellar SR, Schoenfeld BJ, Henselmans M, Helms E, Aragon
  AA, Devries MC, Banfield L, Krieger JW, Phillips SM. A systematic review, meta-analysis and
  meta-regression of the effect of protein supplementation on resistance training-induced gains in
  muscle mass and strength in healthy adults. *Br J Sports Med* 2018;52(6):376-384.
  doi:10.1136/bjsports-2017-097608. PMC5867436.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Design:** 49 RCTs, 1863 participants, resistance training ≥6 weeks.
- **Numbers, verbatim:** gains in "strength-one-repetition-maximum (2.49 kg (0.64, 4.33)), FFM
  (0.30 kg (0.09, 0.52))". "Protein supplementation beyond total protein intakes of 1.62 g/kg/day
  resulted in no further RET-induced gains in FFM." Conclusion: "protein intakes at amounts
  greater than ~1.6 g/kg/day do not further contribute RET-induced gains in FFM."
- **Teaching point:** the effect is small (0.3 kg lean mass over weeks of training), applies to
  people who lift, and shrinks with age. 1.6 g/kg is a plateau for this purpose, not a target for
  the population. Note the DGA's 1.2-1.6 range tops out exactly at Morton's plateau.
  (The full paper also reports a confidence interval around the breakpoint, I believe roughly 1.03
  to 2.20 g/kg; **not verified**, full text not read.) *Superseded by the addition below, which
  verifies it.*
- *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 05's Stage 4:* the full text fetched with curl from Europe PMC
  (PMC5867436, fullTextXML) and read for the Methods, the breakpoint result and the Discussion.
  Supplementary tables not opened. Verbatim:
  - Methods, the comparison groups: "In all analyses the comparator group received an identical RET
    intervention but was non-supplemented or placebo-supplemented."
  - Results, the breakpoint: "Biphasic regression (42 study arms, 723 participants) explained more
    variation than a linear regression between the change in FFM and daily protein intake (break
    point=1.62 (1.03, 2.20) g/kg/day, slope=1.75, R2=0.19, df=36) and is presented as a segmental
    regression despite not being statistically significant (p=0.079;figure 5)".
  - Discussion: "Given that the CI of this estimate spanned from 1.03 to 2.20, it may be prudent to
    recommend ~2.2 g protein/kg/d for those seeking to maximise resistance training-induced gains
    in FFM."
  - Discussion, limitations: "in this manuscript there were only 13 studies that met our inclusion
    criteria in older (>45 years) individuals and only six of those studies reported baseline
    protein intakes with changes in FFM." The same paragraph goes on: "Given that older adults
    require more protein per day,79–81 consume less protein per day87 and that dietary protein
    ingestion and RET are effective strategies to maintain muscle mass and function with age,67
    future RET research should focus on using higher protein doses (or potentially higher
    leucine), larger sample sizes and longer interventions in ageing populations." (The numbers
    run into the words are the paper's reference markers.)

#### 5.3 Older adults: PROT-AGE (Bauer 2013)
- **Citation:** Bauer J, Biolo G, Cederholm T, et al. Evidence-based recommendations for optimal
  dietary protein intake in older people: a position paper from the PROT-AGE Study Group. *J Am
  Med Dir Assoc* 2013;14(8):542-559. doi:10.1016/j.jamda.2013.05.021.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Verbatim:** "the PROT-AGE study group recommends average daily intake at least in the range of
  1.0 to 1.2 g protein per kilogram of body weight per day." "higher protein intake (ie, ≥ 1.2
  g/kg body weight/d) is advised for those who are exercising and otherwise active. Most older
  adults who have acute or chronic diseases need even more dietary protein (ie, 1.2-1.5 g/kg body
  weight/d). Older people with severe kidney disease (ie, estimated GFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m(2)), but
  who are not on dialysis, are an exception to this rule". Also: "Protein quality, timing of
  ingestion, and intake of other nutritional supplements may be relevant, but evidence is not yet
  sufficient to support specific recommendations."
- Design: expert position paper (EUGMS-led), not a systematic review with GRADE.

#### 5.4 ISSN position stand (Jäger 2017)
- **Citation:** Jäger R, Kerksick CM, Campbell BI, et al. International Society of Sports Nutrition
  Position Stand: protein and exercise. *J Int Soc Sports Nutr* 2017;14:20.
  doi:10.1186/s12970-017-0177-8.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Verbatim:** "an overall daily protein intake in the range of 1.4-2.0 g protein/kg body
  weight/day (g/kg/d) is sufficient for most exercising individuals". "Higher protein intakes
  (2.3-3.1 g/kg/d) may be needed to maximize the retention of lean body mass in resistance-trained
  subjects during hypocaloric periods." Per-serving: "0.25 g of a high-quality protein per kg of
  body weight, or an absolute dose of 20-40 g."
- Note: ISSN is a sports-nutrition society with supplement-industry ties among members; several
  authors disclose them in the full paper (not read; flag for Stage 1).

#### 5.5 Kidney safety
- **Healthy kidneys, Devries 2018:** Devries MC, Sithamparapillai A, Brimble KS, Banfield L, Morton
  RW, Phillips SM. Changes in Kidney Function Do Not Differ between Healthy Adults Consuming Higher-
  Compared with Lower- or Normal-Protein Diets: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. *J Nutr*
  2018;148(11):1760-1775. doi:10.1093/jn/nxy197. Abstract read. 28 trials, 1358 participants.
  "The change in GFR did not differ between interventions (SMD: 0.11; 95% CI: -0.05, 0.27; P =
  0.16)." Limitation stated: "the unclear risk of selection bias of the included trials."
  Conclusion: "HP intakes do not adversely influence kidney function on GFR in healthy adults."
  (Trials were short; >4 days qualified.)
- **Chronic kidney disease, KDIGO 2024:** KDIGO CKD Work Group. KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice
  Guideline for the Evaluation and Management of Chronic Kidney Disease. *Kidney Int*
  2024;105(4S):S117-S314. doi:10.1016/j.kint.2023.10.018. Read: the summary of recommendations PDF
  (kdigo.org, 2026 upload). Verbatim: "Recommendation 3.3.1.1: We suggest maintaining a protein
  intake of 0.8 g/kg body weight/d in adults with CKD G3–G5 (2C)." "Practice Point 3.3.1.1: Avoid
  high protein intake (>1.3 g/kg body weight/d) in adults with CKD at risk of progression."
  "Practice Point 3.3.1: Advise people with CKD to adopt healthy and diverse diets with a higher
  consumption of plant-based foods compared to animal-based foods and a lower consumption of
  ultraprocessed foods."
  *Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 05's Stage 4,* from the same summary PDF (local copy, text extracted
  by script), on what "G3–G5" means: Recommendation 3.15.1.1 applies to adults "with eGFR <60 ml/min
  per 1.73 m2 but not treated with chronic dialysis or kidney transplantation (GFR categories
  G3a–G5)", and Table 11 (standards for clinical laboratories) lists: "Reported eGFR levels <60
  ml/min per 1.73 m2 should be flagged as being low." (The "2" is a superscript in the PDF.) The summary does not explain the grading codes
  such as "(2C)"; the grading scheme was not read.
- **KDOQI 2020 (US, with the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics):** Ikizler TA et al. *Am J Kidney
  Dis* 2020;76(3 Suppl 1):S1-S107. doi:10.1053/j.ajkd.2020.05.006. Abstract read; it does not give
  the protein numbers. The 0.55-0.6 g/kg low-protein figure often cited for non-diabetic CKD 3-5 is
  **not verified** (full text not read).
- **The tension to teach:** the DGA's 1.2-1.6 g/kg range sits above KDIGO's ">1.3" avoid line for
  people with CKD at risk of progression. The DGA does say people with chronic disease should
  talk to their clinician. WHO's fact sheet also says excessive protein "can place a metabolic
  burden on the body, particularly the kidneys". Undiagnosed CKD is common, so a population
  target matters here (prevalence figure not fetched; check CDC before quoting a number).

---

### 6. Vegetarian and vegan diets

#### 6.1 Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics position (Melina 2016)
- **Citation:** Melina V, Craig W, Levin S. Position of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics:
  Vegetarian Diets. *J Acad Nutr Diet* 2016;116(12):1970-1980. doi:10.1016/j.jand.2016.09.025.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A; it is the full position statement).
- **Verbatim:** "It is the position of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics that appropriately
  planned vegetarian, including vegan, diets are healthful, nutritionally adequate, and may
  provide health benefits for the prevention and treatment of certain diseases. These diets are
  appropriate for all stages of the life cycle, including pregnancy, lactation, infancy,
  childhood, adolescence, older adulthood, and for athletes." "Vegans need reliable sources of
  vitamin B-12, such as fortified foods or supplements."
- **Caveats:** "appropriately planned" carries the weight. The position also makes an
  environmental claim ("more environmentally sustainable"). Two of three authors are known
  plant-based advocates (Levin works for the Physicians Committee for Responsible Medicine; this
  is from general knowledge, **not verified here**). The position has not been updated since 2016
  as far as I found (not checked on eatrightpro.org).
- **Contrast for balance:** the 2025-2030 DGA says "Vegetarian diets often fall short in vitamins
  D and E, choline, and iron, whereas vegan diets show broader shortfalls in vitamins A, D, E, B6,
  and B12; riboflavin; niacin; choline; calcium; iron; magnesium; phosphorus; potassium; zinc; and
  protein." The two statements are compatible in principle (planned vs unplanned), but the tone
  differs sharply.

#### 6.2 EPIC-Oxford (Tong 2019)
- **Citation:** Tong TYN, Appleby PN, Bradbury KE, Perez-Cornago A, Travis RC, Clarke R, Key TJ.
  Risks of ischaemic heart disease and stroke in meat eaters, fish eaters, and vegetarians over 18
  years of follow-up: results from the prospective EPIC-Oxford study. *BMJ* 2019;366:l4897.
  doi:10.1136/bmj.l4897. PMC6724406.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Design:** prospective cohort, UK; 48,188 people (24,428 meat eaters, 7506 fish eaters, 16,254
  vegetarians including vegans). Diet assessed at baseline (1993-2001) and around 2010.
- **Numbers, verbatim:** "fish eaters and vegetarians had 13% (hazard ratio 0.87, 95% confidence
  interval 0.77 to 0.99) and 22% (0.78, 0.70 to 0.87) lower rates of ischaemic heart disease than
  meat eaters". "equivalent to 10 fewer cases of ischaemic heart disease (95% confidence interval
  6.7 to 13.1 fewer) in vegetarians than in meat eaters per 1000 population over 10 years."
  Partly explained by risk factors: "hazard ratio 0.90, 95% confidence interval 0.81 to 1.00 in
  vegetarians with all adjustments". "By contrast, vegetarians had 20% higher rates of total
  stroke (hazard ratio 1.20, 95% confidence interval 1.02 to 1.40) than meat eaters, equivalent to
  three more cases of total stroke (95% confidence interval 0.8 to 5.4 more) per 1000 population
  over 10 years, mostly due to a higher rate of haemorrhagic stroke."
- **Teaching point:** a real trade-off reported by researchers generally sympathetic to plant
  diets. Observational; vegetarians in this cohort were health-conscious. The authors (in the full
  paper, not read) discuss low LDL or low B12 as possible explanations; do not state a mechanism
  as fact.

#### 6.3 B12
- **NIH ODS, Vitamin B12 Health Professional Fact Sheet**, updated **2 July 2025**. Read via
  Internet Archive (ods.od.nih.gov refused curl). Verbatim: "Vegans who consume no animal products
  and vegetarians who consume some animal products (e.g., dairy products, eggs, or both) but not
  meat have a higher risk of developing vitamin B12 deficiency because natural food sources of
  vitamin B12 are limited to animal foods". "Consumption of foods fortified with vitamin B12 (such
  as fortified nutritional yeasts) as well as vitamin B12 supplements can substantially reduce the
  risk of deficiency". On infants of vegan mothers: "Undetected and untreated vitamin B12
  deficiency in infants can result in neurological damage, failure to thrive, developmental
  delays, and anemia". Adult RDA/DV: 2.4 mcg.
- All sides agree on this one: the Academy, the ODS and the new DGA.

---

### 7. Low-carbohydrate and ketogenic diets

#### 7.1 The proponents' best case: Virta Health trial
- **Citations:** Hallberg SJ, McKenzie AL, Williams PT, et al (including Phinney SD, Volek JS).
  Effectiveness and Safety of a Novel Care Model for the Management of Type 2 Diabetes at 1 Year:
  An Open-Label, Non-Randomized, Controlled Study. *Diabetes Ther* 2018;9(2):583-612.
  doi:10.1007/s13300-018-0373-9. PMC6104272. And the 2-year report: Athinarayanan SJ, Adams RN,
  Hallberg SJ, et al. *Front Endocrinol* 2019;10:348. doi:10.3389/fendo.2019.00348.
- **What I read:** both abstracts (Appendix A).
- **Design:** open-label, **non-randomised**, controlled; 262 in the continuous care intervention
  (CCI: remote coaching, physician medication management, nutritional ketosis) vs 87 usual care.
  "Funding Virta Health Corp."
- **1-year numbers, verbatim:** "HbA 1c declined from 59.6 ± 1.0 to 45.2 ± 0.8 mmol mol -1 (7.6 ±
  0.09% to 6.3 ± 0.07%, P < 1.0 × 10 -16 ), weight declined 13.8 ± 0.71 kg". "Insulin therapy was
  reduced or eliminated in 94% of users; sulfonylureas were entirely eliminated in the CCI."
  "LDL-cholesterol + 10% (P = 5.1 × 10 -5 )", "apolipoprotein B was unchanged". 83% retention at
  one year.
- **2-year:** "resolution of diabetes (reversal, 53.5%; remission, 17.6%) in the CCI group".
- **Limits (my summary):** not randomised; control group chosen from people who did not opt in;
  company-funded and company-run; the intervention bundles intensive support with the diet, so the
  diet's share of the effect cannot be separated.

#### 7.2 The ADA consensus report (Evert 2019), which accepts low-carb
- **Citation:** Evert AB, Dennison M, Gardner CD, et al. Nutrition Therapy for Adults With
  Diabetes or Prediabetes: A Consensus Report. *Diabetes Care* 2019;42(5):731-754.
  doi:10.2337/dci19-0014. PMC7011201.
- **What I read:** no abstract exists; full text read via Europe PMC XML, sections on eating
  patterns, macronutrients and carbohydrate.
- **Verbatim:** "Evidence suggests that there is not an ideal percentage of calories from
  carbohydrate, protein, and fat for all people with or at risk for diabetes". "Reducing overall
  carbohydrate intake for individuals with diabetes has demonstrated the most evidence for
  improving glycemia and may be applied in a variety of eating patterns that meet individual needs
  and preferences." "For select adults with type 2 diabetes not meeting glycemic targets or where
  reducing antiglycemic medications is a priority, reducing overall carbohydrate intake with low-
  or very low-carbohydrate eating plans is a viable approach."
- Definitions: low-carb "26–45% of total calories"; very low-carb "<26% of total calories", "Often
  has a goal of 20–50 g of nonfiber carbohydrate per day to induce nutritional ketosis."
- Durability, verbatim: benefits "were more pronounced in the VLC interventions ... at 3 and 6
  months but not at 12 and 24 months".
- Cautions, verbatim: "Because of theoretical concerns regarding use of VLC eating plans in people
  with chronic kidney disease, disordered eating patterns, and women who are pregnant, further
  research is needed". "consultation with a knowledgeable practitioner at the onset is necessary
  to prevent dehydration and reduce insulin and hypoglycemic medications to prevent
  hypoglycemia."
- Good line for a lesson: "fish and processed red meats are both considered part of a
  low-carbohydrate eating pattern; and removing the bun from a fast food burger might make it part
  of a paleo eating pattern but does not necessarily make it healthier."

#### 7.3 ADA Standards of Care in Diabetes 2026, Section 5 (current)
- **Citation:** American Diabetes Association Professional Practice Committee. 5. Facilitating
  Positive Health Behaviors and Well-being to Improve Health Outcomes: Standards of Care in
  Diabetes—2026. *Diabetes Care* 2026. doi:10.2337/dc26-S005. PMC12690188.
- **What I read:** full text via PMC, carbohydrate and eating-pattern sections.
- **Verbatim:** "5.15 Consider reducing carbohydrate intake for some adults with diabetes to
  improve glycemia. An effective way to achieve this is by limiting consumption of processed
  foods. B". "There is no ideal percentage of calories from carbohydrate, protein, or fat for
  people with diabetes." "Evidence-based eating patterns most frequently recommended include
  Mediterranean, DASH, low-fat, carbohydrate-restricted, vegetarian, and vegan eating patterns."
  "Systematic reviews and meta-analyses of RCTs report carbohydrate-restricted eating patterns,
  particularly those considered very low carbohydrate (<26% total energy), were effective in
  reducing A1C in the short term (<6 months), with less difference in eating patterns beyond 1
  year". It cites a 12-week RCT (Keto-Med, below) and a meta-analysis of six ≥12-month RCTs (524
  participants) where low-carb "was beneficial for lipids but not glycemic management
  (standardized mean difference −0.11, 95% CI −0.33 to 0.11, P = 0.32)". "5.24 Emphasize minimally
  processed, nutrient-dense, high-fiber sources of carbohydrate (at least 14 g fiber per 1,000
  kcal). B"

#### 7.4 Critical and neutral evidence
- **Goldenberg 2021 BMJ meta-analysis:** Goldenberg JZ, Day A, Brinkworth GD, et al. Efficacy and
  safety of low and very low carbohydrate diets for type 2 diabetes remission: systematic review
  and meta-analysis of published and unpublished randomized trial data. *BMJ* 2021;372:m4743.
  doi:10.1136/bmj.m4743. Abstract read. 23 RCTs, 1357 participants. At six months, remission
  (HbA1c <6.5%) "76/133 (57%) v 41/131 (31%); risk difference 0.32, 95% confidence interval 0.17
  to 0.47". "Conversely, smaller, non-significant effect sizes occurred when a remission definition
  of HbA 1c <6.5% without medication was used." "At 12 months, data on remission were sparse".
  Benefits "diminished at 12 months". Conclusion: "On the basis of moderate to low certainty
  evidence, patients adhering to an LCD for six months may experience remission of diabetes
  without adverse consequences."
- **Keto-Med (Gardner 2022):** Gardner CD, Landry MJ, Perelman D, et al. *Am J Clin Nutr*
  2022;116(3):640-652. doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqac154. Abstract read. Crossover RCT, 40 enrolled, 33
  analysed; 12 weeks each of a well-formulated ketogenic diet vs a Mediterranean-plus diet (both
  low in added sugar and refined grain). "The HbA1c values did not differ between diets at 12
  weeks." LDL "+10% (SEM, 4%) compared with -5% (SEM, 5%)"; triglycerides fell more on keto.
  "Twelve-week follow-up data suggest the Med-Plus is more sustainable." Gardner is a co-author of
  the ADA consensus report, so this is not an outsider's critique.
- **DIETFITS (Gardner 2018) for weight loss in non-diabetics:** *JAMA* 2018;319(7):667-679.
  doi:10.1001/jama.2018.0245. Abstract read. 609 adults. "Weight change at 12 months was -5.3 kg
  for the HLF diet vs -6.0 kg for the HLC diet (mean between-group difference, 0.7 kg [95% CI, -0.2
  to 1.6 kg])." Neither genotype nor insulin secretion predicted which diet worked.
- **What would settle it:** multi-year randomised trials with hard outcomes (cardiovascular
  events, mortality) and with the support package held equal across arms. The LDL rise on
  ketogenic diets is the main open safety question.

#### 7.5 Carnivore diet (Lennerz 2021)
- **Citation:** Lennerz BS, Mey JT, Henn OH, Ludwig DS. Behavioral Characteristics and
  Self-Reported Health Status among 2029 Adults Consuming a "Carnivore Diet". *Curr Dev Nutr*
  2021;5(12):nzab133. doi:10.1093/cdn/nzab133. PMC8684475.
- **What I read:** abstract (Appendix A).
- **Design:** "A social media survey" of self-identified carnivore dieters for ≥6 months, 30 March
  to 24 June 2020. All outcomes self-reported.
- **Numbers, verbatim:** 2029 respondents, median 14 months on the diet. "Prevalence of adverse
  symptoms was low (<1% to 5.5%)." BMI "from 27.2 [23.5-31.9] to 24.3 [22.1-27.0]". "Among a subset
  reporting current lipids, LDL-cholesterol was markedly elevated (172 mg/dL)". Conclusion:
  "Contrary to common expectations, adults consuming a carnivore diet experienced few adverse
  effects and instead reported health benefits and high satisfaction." "The generalizability of
  these findings and the long-term effects of this dietary pattern require further study."
- **Design limits (my summary):** self-selected enthusiasts recruited online; survivorship bias
  (people who quit or got ill do not answer); self-reported labs; no control group; median 14
  months. It shows the diet is tolerable for some motivated people, not that it is safe or better.
  The LDL figure is a warning sign the authors themselves report. Ludwig is a well-known
  low-carbohydrate researcher, so the paper is from the sympathetic side.

---

### 8. Blue Zones: a case study in how a claim travels

- **Origin:** Poulain M, Pes GM, Grasland C, Carru C, Ferrucci L, Baggio G, Franceschi C, Deiana L.
  Identification of a geographic area characterized by extreme longevity in the Sardinia island:
  the AKEA study. *Exp Gerontol* 2004;39(9):1423-1429. doi:10.1016/j.exger.2004.06.016. Abstract
  read. The "Blue Zone" was a mapping term for a mountain area of Sardinia with many *validated*
  male centenarians. The authors floated inbreeding and genetics as "An alternative and
  interesting hypothesis". Diet is not in the abstract.
- **Popularisation:** Buettner D, Skemp S. Blue Zones: Lessons From the World's Longest Lived. *Am
  J Lifestyle Med* 2016;10(5):318-321. doi:10.1177/1559827616637066. Abstract read. "What began as
  a National Geographic expedition, lead by Dan Buettner, ... evolved into the discovery of the 5
  places around the world where people consistently live over 100 years old". "the Power 9". It
  also says the Blue Zones Project communities "have been able to increase life expectancy" (a
  commercial programme's claim, no data in the abstract).
- **Critique:** Newman SJ. Supercentenarian and remarkable age records exhibit patterns indicative
  of clerical errors and pension fraud. *bioRxiv* 2019, doi:10.1101/704080 (revised versions since;
  a 2024 version exists per press coverage). **A preprint, not peer-reviewed.** Abstract read
  (Appendix A). Verbatim: "The state-specific introduction of birth certificates is associated
  with a 69-82% fall in the number of supercentenarian records." "Only 18% of 'exhaustively'
  validated supercentenarians have a birth certificate". "the designated 'blue zones' of Sardinia,
  Okinawa, and Ikaria corresponded to regions with low incomes, low literacy, high crime rate and
  short life expectancy relative to their national average."
- UCL press release (Sept 2024, read via wired-gov.net mirror): Newman won the first Ig Nobel prize
  in Demography on 12 September 2024. It says "despite vegetables and sweet potatoes being
  promoted as key components of the Okinawan 'Blue Zone' diets, according to the Japanese
  government, Okinawans eat the least vegetables and sweet potatoes in Japan and have the highest
  body mass index." (UCL's claim; I did not check the Japanese data.)
- **The defenders' reply, in their own words:** "Demographers' Statement", October 2024, hosted on
  bluezones.com, signed by Gianni Pes, Luis Rosero-Bixby, Christina Chrysohoou, Christodoulos
  Stefanadis, Bradley Willcox and Craig Willcox (read in full). "In Sardinia, for example, we
  double-checked the age of every centenarian in the Blue Zone villages using: Civil status
  databases dating back to 1866, Handwritten records from ecclesiastical archives ... A complete
  genealogical reconstruction of village inhabitants from 1866 onward." "Newman's use of
  Sardinia-wide crime and poverty statistics to generalize about the blue zones regions is also
  misleading. The Sardinian blue zones regions is a small rural area of about 50,000 people".
  They also concede: "we have publicly acknowledged that Okinawa's longevity rates have declined
  with the arrival of modern diets and transportation."
- **Status as of mid-2026:** ABC News (Australia), 11 July 2026, reports the preprint still
  unpublished after "nine peer reviews" and a book forthcoming (**WebFetch summary, not
  verbatim-checked**).
- **How to teach it (my summary):** two separate claims got fused. (1) Are the ages real? Newman's
  critique is strongest for supercentenarians and for Okinawa; the Sardinian age validation is
  the most careful, and his regional statistics may not describe six villages. (2) Even if the
  ages are real, did diet cause them? That was never tested; the "Power 9" are descriptions of
  what long-lived people did, gathered after the fact, with no comparison group. The diet message
  travelled from a demographic map to a National Geographic feature to a book series and a
  commercial programme, gaining certainty at each step. The same mechanism (a striking
  observation, a plausible story, a good name) runs through much popular nutrition.

---

### 9. "Whole food" and "highly processed" in the new DGA (overlap only)

- The 2025-2030 DGA uses "highly processed", not "ultra-processed", and gives no formal
  definition: realfood.gov says "This is not an attack on industry or a legal definition." Its
  working examples are "Packaged, prepared, ready-to-eat, or other foods that are salty or sweet,
  such as chips, cookies, and candy", foods with "artificial flavors, petroleum-based dyes,
  artificial preservatives, and low-calorie non-nutritive sweeteners", and sugar-sweetened drinks.
  realfood.gov defines real food as "whole or minimally processed and recognizable as food".
- The Scientific Foundation criticises the DGAC for not setting "clear limits" on highly processed
  foods. The AHA's statement offers to help build "an operational definition of ultraprocessed
  food". Harvard notes "this terminology is somewhat vague on the surface (food processing is a
  spectrum after all)".
- Canada's 2019 guide and the WHO fact sheet already used "highly processed foods". Hand the rest
  to the UPF agent.

---

### Things that surprised me or cut against common belief

1. The 2025-2030 DGA **kept** the 10% saturated-fat cap while naming butter and beef tallow as
   cooking options and full-fat dairy as a goal. Harvard's arithmetic shows the recommended
   full-fat dairy alone nearly uses the cap. Both the praise and the criticism miss this if they
   look only at the graphic.
2. The administration formally set aside the DGAC report and commissioned its own 90-page
   "Scientific Foundation", with review authors including known low-carbohydrate researchers
   (Volek, Bikman) and a disclosed beef-industry grant.
3. The 2025-2030 DGA removed the numeric alcohol limit.
4. The WHO fact sheet was revised on 26 January 2026 and now gives carbohydrate 45-75% of energy
   and protein 10-15%, and explicitly acknowledges low-carb diets for "certain conditions".
5. PREDIMED's headline result survived its retraction nearly unchanged, but Cochrane grades its
   mortality evidence low and finds "little or no effect" on CVD or total mortality; the benefit
   is mostly stroke.
6. EPIC-Oxford: vegetarians had fewer heart attacks but **more** haemorrhagic stroke.
7. Protein supplementation adds about 0.3 kg of lean mass in training studies, and the benefit
   plateaus at about 1.6 g/kg, which is the top of the new DGA range.
8. Canada told its public to limit highly processed foods in 2019.
9. The ADA has accepted low-carb and very-low-carb patterns since 2019, yet its 2026 Standards
   note that the glucose advantage fades after about a year.
10. Newman's Blue Zones critique, widely reported as a "debunking", is still an unreviewed preprint
    as of mid-2026, and the demographers' rebuttal is hosted by the Blue Zones company.

### Not read / to fetch in Stage 1 proper
- IOM 2005 DRI protein chapter (for the RDA sentence verbatim).
- Scientific Report of the 2025 DGAC (for its own protein and saturated fat conclusions).
- J Nutr 2026 "A Critical Perspective on the DGA 2025-2030".
- AHA 2026 dietary guidance statement (reported 31 March 2026).
- NEJM PREDIMED retraction notice text; Carlisle 2017 supplementary list naming PREDIMED.
- SACN 2019 saturated fats report (exact recommendation wording).
- KDOQI 2020 protein figures.
- Morton 2018 full text (the confidence interval around 1.62 g/kg).
- Newman's latest bioRxiv version and any published version.
