## Part F: Supplements, alcohol, special populations and safety

Research notes for "Nutrition: The Evidence", Stage 1. Compiled 2026-09-24.

### How these notes were gathered (read this first)

- **Abstracts** were fetched as raw JSON from the Europe PMC REST API with `curl` and printed by a script. Nothing in the abstract appendix passed through a summarising model. Every abstract cited below is reproduced whole in the Appendix.
- **Guideline and government pages** were fetched with `curl` and converted to plain text by a small HTML-to-text script (no model in the loop), then searched with `grep`. Quotations from these pages are copied from that plain text. Where a site blocked `curl` (NIH ODS, HHS, NIH), the page was fetched from the Internet Archive's raw snapshot (`web.archive.org/web/<timestamp>id_/...`); the snapshot date is given.
- **PDFs** (Surgeon General advisory, NASEM highlights, ICCPUD draft, Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030, UK CMO guidelines) were downloaded and converted with `pdftotext`; quotations come from that text. Line breaks in PDFs were joined; no words changed.
- **WebSearch** was used only to find URLs. Where a fact rests only on a search-result summary it is marked **NOT READ: search-summary level only** and nothing from it is placed in quotation marks.
- Every sentence outside quotation marks is my summary.
- After writing, every quoted string in this file was checked by script against the fetched source texts (see the note at the end of the file).

---

### PART 1. Supplements for the general population

#### 1.1 USPSTF 2022: vitamin, mineral and multivitamin supplements to prevent CVD and cancer

**Citation.** US Preventive Services Task Force; Mangione CM, Barry MJ, Nicholson WK, Cabana M, Chelmow D, Coker TR, Davis EM, Donahue KE, Doubeni CA, Jaén CR, Kubik M, Li L, Ogedegbe G, Pbert L, Ruiz JM, Stevermer J, Wong JB. Vitamin, Mineral, and Multivitamin Supplementation to Prevent Cardiovascular Disease and Cancer: US Preventive Services Task Force Recommendation Statement. *JAMA*. 2022;327(23):2326-2333. PMID 35727271. DOI 10.1001/jama.2022.8970.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC (PMID 35727271). Also the USPSTF recommendation web page (https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/uspstf/recommendation/vitamin-supplementation-to-prevent-cvd-and-cancer-preventive-medication), fetched by curl; page shows release date June 21, 2022. Note: the DOI 10.1001/jama.2022.8970 resolved in Europe PMC to an *American Family Physician* reprint record with no abstract, so I fetched by PMID instead.

**Design.** Recommendation statement based on a commissioned systematic review (O'Connor EA et al, JAMA 2022, not read).

**Grades (verbatim from the abstract):**
- "The USPSTF recommends against the use of beta carotene or vitamin E supplements for the prevention of cardiovascular disease or cancer. (D recommendation)"
- "The USPSTF concludes that the current evidence is insufficient to assess the balance of benefits and harms of the use of multivitamin supplements for the prevention of cardiovascular disease or cancer. (I statement)"
- "The USPSTF concludes that the current evidence is insufficient to assess the balance of benefits and harms of the use of single- or paired-nutrient supplements (other than beta carotene and vitamin E) for the prevention of cardiovascular disease or cancer. (I statement)."

**Evidence assessment (verbatim):** "The USPSTF concludes with moderate certainty that the harms of beta carotene supplementation outweigh the benefits for the prevention of cardiovascular disease or cancer. The USPSTF also concludes with moderate certainty that there is no net benefit of supplementation with vitamin E for the prevention of cardiovascular disease or cancer."

**Usage figure (verbatim, abstract):** "According to National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey data, 52% of surveyed US adults reported using at least 1 dietary supplement in the prior 30 days and 31% reported using a multivitamin-mineral supplement."

**Scope (verbatim, web page):** "This recommendation applies to community-dwelling, nonpregnant adults. It does not apply to children, persons who are pregnant or may become pregnant, or persons who are chronically ill, are hospitalized, or have a known nutritional deficiency."

**The main harm (verbatim, web page):** "For many vitamins and nutrients, there was little evidence of serious harms. However, an important harm of increased lung cancer incidence was reported with the use of beta carotene by persons who smoke tobacco or have occupational exposure to asbestos."

**Teaching note (summary).** An *I statement* is not a verdict that supplements do nothing; it is a statement that the evidence cannot settle the balance. The course should resist rounding *I* to *no*. The D grades are the firm part. The scope sentence matters: this recommendation is silent on pregnancy, deficiency and illness, which is exactly where Part 2 below applies.

#### 1.2 VITAL: vitamin D and cancer/CVD (Manson 2019)

**Citation.** Manson JE, Cook NR, Lee IM, Christen W, Bassuk SS, Mora S, Gibson H, Gordon D, Copeland T, D'Agostino D, Friedenberg G, Ridge C, Bubes V, Giovannucci EL, Willett WC, Buring JE; VITAL Research Group. Vitamin D Supplements and Prevention of Cancer and Cardiovascular Disease. *N Engl J Med*. 2019;380(1):33-44. DOI 10.1056/NEJMoa1809944. PMID 30415629.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Nationwide randomised placebo-controlled 2x2 factorial trial; vitamin D3 2000 IU/day and marine omega-3 1 g/day; men 50 and over, women 55 and over, US. n = 25,871 (5,106 Black participants). Median follow-up 5.3 years.

**Key numbers (from abstract).** Invasive cancer: 793 vs 824, HR 0.96 (95% CI 0.88 to 1.06), P = 0.47. Major cardiovascular events: 396 vs 409, HR 0.97 (0.85 to 1.12), P = 0.69. Secondary: death from cancer HR 0.83 (0.67 to 1.02); all-cause death HR 0.99 (0.87 to 1.12).

**Conclusion (verbatim):** "Supplementation with vitamin D did not result in a lower incidence of invasive cancer or cardiovascular events than placebo."

**Safety (verbatim):** "No excess risks of hypercalcemia or other adverse events were identified."

**Limitations (my summary; abstract does not list them).** Participants were not selected for low vitamin D; 5.3 years may be short for cancer; the cancer-death confidence interval brushes 1.0, which is why cancer mortality remains an open question (see Cochrane and Endocrine Society below). Do not describe the cancer-death result as a finding; the prespecified primary endpoints were null.

#### 1.3 VITAL: marine omega-3 (Manson 2019, same issue)

**Citation.** Manson JE, Cook NR, Lee IM, Christen W, Bassuk SS, Mora S, Gibson H, Albert CM, Gordon D, Copeland T, D'Agostino D, Friedenberg G, Ridge C, Bubes V, Giovannucci EL, Willett WC, Buring JE; VITAL Research Group. Marine n-3 Fatty Acids and Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease and Cancer. *N Engl J Med*. 2019;380(1):23-32. DOI 10.1056/NEJMoa1811403. PMID 30415637.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Key numbers.** Major cardiovascular events 386 vs 419, HR 0.92 (0.80 to 1.06), P = 0.24. Invasive cancer 820 vs 797, HR 1.03 (0.93 to 1.13). Secondary: total myocardial infarction HR 0.72 (0.59 to 0.90). All-cause death HR 1.02 (0.90 to 1.15).

**Conclusion (verbatim):** "Supplementation with n-3 fatty acids did not result in a lower incidence of major cardiovascular events or cancer than placebo."

**Teaching note.** The myocardial infarction result is a secondary endpoint in a trial whose primary endpoint was null. It is a classic example for teaching why secondary endpoints generate hypotheses rather than conclusions.

#### 1.4 Cochrane review: vitamin D for prevention of mortality (Bjelakovic 2014)

**Citation.** Bjelakovic G, Gluud LL, Nikolova D, Whitfield K, Wetterslev J, Simonetti RG, Bjelakovic M, Gluud C. Vitamin D supplementation for prevention of mortality in adults. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev*. 2014;(1):CD007470. DOI 10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3. PMID 24414552.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC. Search date in the review was February 2012, so this predates VITAL and should be presented as older evidence. I did not check whether Cochrane has issued a newer version; a later writer should check the Cochrane Library for an update before citing this as current.

**Design.** Systematic review and meta-analysis of RCTs. 159 trials identified; 56 trials with 95,286 participants gave usable mortality data. Most trials in women over 70; mean 77% women.

**Key numbers.** All vitamin D forms: 12.5% vs 12.7% mortality, RR 0.97 (0.94 to 0.99), P = 0.02. Vitamin D3 only: RR 0.94 (0.91 to 0.98), 38 trials, 75,927 participants. Vitamin D3 and cancer mortality: RR 0.88 (0.78 to 0.98), 4 trials. Vitamin D3 plus calcium increased kidney stones: RR 1.17 (1.02 to 1.34).

**Authors' conclusion and stated limitations (verbatim):** "Vitamin D3 seemed to decrease mortality in elderly people living independently or in institutional care." And: "Because of risks of attrition bias originating from substantial dropout of participants and of outcome reporting bias due to a number of trials not reporting on mortality, as well as a number of other weaknesses in our evidence, further placebo-controlled randomised trials seem warranted."

**Also stated in the abstract (verbatim):** "'Worst-best case' and 'best-worst case' scenario analyses demonstrated that vitamin D could be associated with a dramatic increase or decrease in mortality."

**Teaching note.** A small relative effect concentrated in older, mostly institutionalised women. This fits with the Endocrine Society 2024 suggestion for adults 75 and over (below). It does not support vitamin D for younger healthy adults.

#### 1.5 Endocrine Society 2024 guideline: vitamin D for prevention of disease (recent change)

**Citation.** Demay MB, Pittas AG, Bikle DD, Diab DL, Kiely ME, Lazaretti-Castro M, Lips P, Mitchell DM, Murad MH, Powers S, Rao SD, Scragg R, Tayek JA, Valent AM, Walsh JME, McCartney CR. Vitamin D for the Prevention of Disease: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline. *J Clin Endocrinol Metab*. 2024;109(8):1907-1947. DOI 10.1210/clinem/dgae290. PMID 38828931.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Clinical practice guideline, GRADE methods, prioritising placebo-controlled RCTs.

**What it says (verbatim, conclusion):** "The panel suggests empiric vitamin D for those aged 1 to 18 years and adults over 75 years of age, those who are pregnant, and those with high-risk prediabetes." And: "The panel suggests against empiric vitamin D supplementation above the current DRI to lower the risk of disease in healthy adults younger than 75 years." And on testing: "Based on the absence of supportive clinical trial evidence, the panel suggests against routine 25(OH)D testing in the absence of established indications."

**Stated limitation (verbatim):** "Because the vitamin D doses in the included clinical trials varied considerably and many trial participants were allowed to continue their own vitamin D-containing supplements, the optimal doses for empiric vitamin D supplementation remain unclear for the populations considered."

**Teaching note.** Useful as the bridge between *supplements do not prevent disease in healthy adults* (VITAL, USPSTF) and *some groups do benefit*. Note that *suggests* is GRADE language for a conditional (weak) recommendation.

**Access note.** ods.od.nih.gov blocked direct curl (Cloudflare). All three sheets were fetched from Internet Archive raw snapshots taken 18 to 20 September 2026, i.e. four to six days before these notes. The "Updated" date printed on each page is given.

Recommended amounts (table, verbatim cell text):

Useful quotations:
- "Breastfed infants should be given a supplement of 10 mcg (400 IU) of vitamin D each day."
- "High levels of vitamin D are almost always caused by consuming excessive amounts of vitamin D from dietary supplements. You cannot get too much vitamin D from sunshine because your skin limits the amount of vitamin D it makes."
- "Overall, clinical trials find that vitamin D supplements do not reduce the risk of developing heart disease or dying from it, even if you have low blood levels of the vitamin."
- "Vitamin D does not seem to reduce the risk of developing cancer of the breast, colon, rectum, or lung."
- Blood level thresholds: "Levels of 50 nmol/L (20 ng/mL) or above are adequate for most people for bone and overall health." and "Levels above 125 nmol/L (50 ng/mL) are too high and might cause health problems."

URL: https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-Consumer/

| Life Stage | Recommended Amount |

| Birth to 6 months | 0.4 mcg |
| Infants 7–12 months | 0.5 mcg |

There is no upper limit table on the page. Quotations:
- "Vitamin B12 has not been shown to cause any harm, even at high doses."
- "Plant foods have no vitamin B12 unless they are fortified."
- "People who eat little or no animal foods, such as vegetarians and vegans, might not get enough vitamin B12 from their diets. Only animal foods have vitamin B12 naturally. When pregnant women and women who breastfeed their babies are strict vegetarians or vegans, their babies might also not get enough vitamin B12."
- "Many older adults don’t have enough hydrochloric acid in their stomach to absorb the vitamin B12 that’s naturally present in food. People over 50 should get most of their vitamin B12 from fortified foods or dietary supplements because, in most cases, their bodies can absorb vitamin B12 from these sources."
- "Vitamin B12 deficiency affects between 3% and 43% of older adults."
- Drug interaction: "Metformin might reduce vitamin B12 absorption and lower blood levels of vitamin B12."
- "Vitamin B12 deficiency can damage the nervous system even in people who don’t have megaloblastic anemia, so it’s important to treat a deficiency as soon as possible."
- On marketing claims: "Manufacturers often promote vitamin B12 supplements for energy, athletic performance, and endurance. However, vitamin B12 doesn’t provide these benefits in people who get enough B12 from their diet."

Quotations:
- "Vegetarians who do not eat meat, poultry, or seafood need almost twice as much iron as listed in the table because the body doesn’t absorb nonheme iron in plant foods as well as heme iron in animal foods."
- "Accidental overdose of iron-containing products is a leading cause of fatal poisoning in children under 6."
- "Some people have an inherited condition called hemochromatosis that causes toxic levels of iron to build up in their bodies." and "People with this disorder should avoid using iron supplements and vitamin C supplements."
- "Women who are pregnant should talk with their health care provider and take an iron supplement if recommended."
- On anaemia of chronic disease: "Taking more iron from foods or supplements usually does not reduce the resulting anemia of chronic disease because iron is diverted from the blood circulation to storage sites."

**Teaching note on iron.** Iron is the clearest example in the course of a supplement that is right for a diagnosed deficiency and wrong for a person who has not been tested: the UL, child poisoning and haemochromatosis sentences together make the case for "test, then treat", not "take one in case".

#### 1.7 US regulation: DSHEA 1994 and what FDA does and does not do

**Source.** FDA, "Questions and Answers on Dietary Supplements", https://www.fda.gov/food/information-consumers-using-dietary-supplements/questions-and-answers-dietary-supplements. Fetched by curl. Page shows "Content current as of: 08/27/2026" (updated last month).

Verbatim:
- "Under DSHEA, FDA does not have the authority to approve dietary supplements before they are marketed. Generally, a firm does not have to provide FDA with the evidence it relies on to substantiate safety before or after it markets its products; however, there is an exception for dietary supplements that contain a new dietary ingredient that is not present in the food supply as an article used for food in a form in which the food has not been chemically altered."
- "In general, FDA is limited to postmarket enforcement because, unlike drugs that must be proven safe and effective for their intended use before marketing, there are no provisions in the law for FDA to approve dietary supplements for safety before they reach the consumer."
- "Because FDA does not approve dietary supplements before they are marketed, the agency often does not know when new products come on the market."
- "However, by law, FDA does not approve dietary supplements or their labeling, although certain types of claims sometimes used in dietary supplement labeling require premarket review and authorization (e.g., health claims)."
- On dose: "there are no laws or regulations that limit the serving size of a dietary supplement or the amount of a dietary ingredient that can be in a serving of a dietary supplement. This decision is made by the manufacturer and does not require FDA approval."
- On the label disclaimer: "the claim must be accompanied by a disclaimer informing consumers that FDA has not evaluated the claim. The disclaimer must also state that the product is not intended to "diagnose, treat, cure, or prevent any disease" because only a drug can legally make such a claim."
- On advertising: "FDA and FTC share responsibility for the oversight of dietary supplements and related promotion, with FDA generally responsible for safety, quality, and labeling, and FTC generally responsible for advertising."

**Companion source.** NIH ODS, "Dietary Supplements: What You Need to Know" (Consumer), snapshot 2026-09-18, page "Updated: January 4, 2023". URL https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/WYNTK-Consumer/. Verbatim:
- "Medicines must be approved by FDA before they can be sold or marketed. Supplements do not require this approval. Supplement companies are responsible for having evidence that their products are safe, and the label claims are truthful and not misleading."
- "The U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) does not determine whether dietary supplements are effective before they are marketed."
- On third-party seals: "These seals do not guarantee that a product is safe or effective."
- "Keep in mind the term natural doesn’t always mean safe. Some all-natural botanical products, for example comfrey and kava, can harm the liver."
- Interactions: "Vitamin K can reduce the ability of the blood thinner warfarin to prevent blood from clotting." and "St. John’s wort can speed the breakdown of many medicines and reduce their effectiveness (including some antidepressants, birth control pills, heart medications, anti-HIV medications, and transplant drugs)." and "Antioxidant supplements, such as vitamins C and E, might reduce the effectiveness of some types of cancer chemotherapy."
- Pregnancy and children: "Be cautious about taking dietary supplements, beyond a standard prenatal supplement, if you are pregnant or nursing. Also, be careful about giving supplements to a child, unless recommended by their health care provider."
- Where supplements help (the page's own list): "Calcium and vitamin D help keep bones strong and reduce bone loss." "Folic acid decreases the risk of certain birth defects." "Omega-3 fatty acids from fish oils might help some people with heart disease."

**Neutrality note.** The course is not only for US readers. The UK and EU regulate food supplements under food law too (the EU Food Supplements Directive 2002/46/EC); I did not fetch any UK or EU regulator page for this, so a writer should either add one or keep the regulatory lesson explicitly American.

#### 1.8 Adulteration: unapproved drugs in supplements (Tucker 2018)

**Citation.** Tucker J, Fischer T, Upjohn L, Mazzera D, Kumar M. Unapproved Pharmaceutical Ingredients Included in Dietary Supplements Associated With US Food and Drug Administration Warnings. *JAMA Netw Open*. 2018;1(6):e183337. DOI 10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2018.3337. PMID 30646238.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Quality improvement study; analysis of FDA's "Tainted Products Marketed as Dietary Supplements" database, 2007 to 2016.

**Key numbers.** 776 adulterated supplements, 146 companies. Sexual enhancement 353 (45.5%), weight loss 317 (40.9%), muscle building 92 (11.9%). 157 (20.2%) had more than one unapproved ingredient. Commonest adulterants: sildenafil in 166 of 353 sexual enhancement products (47.0%); sibutramine in 269 of 317 weight loss products (84.9%); steroids or steroid-like ingredients in 82 of 92 muscle building products (89.1%). Of 28 products named in repeat warnings, 19 (67.9%) had new unapproved ingredients at the later warning.

**Conclusion (verbatim):** "Active pharmaceuticals continue to be identified in dietary supplements, especially those marketed for sexual enhancement or weight loss, even after FDA warnings."

**Limitation (my summary).** This counts products the FDA caught; it is not a prevalence estimate for the market as a whole. It says nothing about ordinary vitamin and mineral products. The risk is concentrated in three marketing categories.

#### 1.9 Liver injury from herbal and dietary supplements

**(a) Navarro VJ, Khan I, Björnsson E, Seeff LB, Serrano J, Hoofnagle JH.** Liver injury from herbal and dietary supplements. *Hepatology*. 2017;65(1):363-373. DOI 10.1002/hep.28813. PMID 27677775.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC. Design: summary of an AASLD/NIH research symposium (a narrative review, not a systematic review).

Verbatim: "HDS-induced liver injury now accounts for 20% of cases of hepatotoxicity in the United States based on research data. The major implicated agents include anabolic steroids, green tea extract, and multi-ingredient nutritional supplements." And: "Currently, however, the majority of cases of HDS-associated liver injury are due to multi-ingredient nutritional supplements, and the component responsible for the toxicity is usually unknown or can only be suspected."

Limitation: the 20% figure is "based on research data", which in context refers to the US Drug-Induced Liver Injury Network registry of referred cases; it is a share of recognised drug-induced liver injury cases, not a population risk. Say so if quoting it.

**(b) Likhitsup A, Chen VL, Fontana RJ.** Estimated Exposure to 6 Potentially Hepatotoxic Botanicals in US Adults. *JAMA Netw Open*. 2024;7(8):e2425822. DOI 10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2024.25822. PMID 39102266.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC. Design: cross-sectional survey analysis of NHANES January 2017 to March 2020, n = 9,685 adults.

Key numbers: any herbal or dietary supplement use in past 30 days 57.6% (95% CI 55.9% to 59.4%); use of at least one of six botanicals (turmeric/curcumin, green tea extract, Garcinia cambogia, black cohosh, red yeast rice, ashwagandha) 4.7% (3.9% to 5.7%), estimated 15.6 million US adults, comparable to the number prescribed simvastatin (about 14.0 million) or NSAIDs (about 14.8 million).

Verbatim conclusion: "Given a lack of regulatory oversight on the manufacturing and testing of botanical products, clinicians should be aware of possible adverse events from consumption of these largely unregulated products."

Limitation (my summary): this is an exposure estimate, not an injury rate. Most users of turmeric or green tea come to no harm. The point for the course is scale, not danger per dose.

**LiverTox.** Not fetched (NIH/NCBI Bookshelf blocked curl for another NIH page in this session). A writer who wants LiverTox entries on specific botanicals should fetch them directly.

### PART 2. Where supplements are clearly indicated

#### 2.1 Folic acid before conception: the MRC Vitamin Study (1991)

**Citation.** MRC Vitamin Study Research Group. Prevention of neural tube defects: results of the Medical Research Council Vitamin Study. *Lancet*. 1991;338(8760):131-137. PMID 1677062. (The DOI I expected, 10.1016/0140-6736(91)90133-A, did not resolve in Europe PMC; I retrieved the record by PMID. Do not print that DOI without checking it.)

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Randomised double-blind factorial prevention trial, 33 centres, seven countries. 1,817 women at high risk (previous affected pregnancy); 1,195 completed pregnancies with known outcome.

**Key numbers.** 27 neural tube defects: 6 in the folic acid groups, 21 in the other two groups. Verbatim: "a 72% protective effect (relative risk 0.28, 95% confidence interval 0.12-0.71)." Other vitamins: "relative risk 0.80, 95% Cl 0.32-1.72" (the abstract prints "Cl" for "CI").

**Stated limitation (verbatim):** "There was no demonstrable harm from the folic acid supplementation, though the ability of the study to detect rare or slight adverse effects was limited."

**Note.** The trial population was women with a previous affected pregnancy. Evidence for the general population came later (not fetched here; the Czeizel and Dudás 1992 Hungarian trial is the usual citation). The USPSTF relies on the combined body of evidence.

#### 2.2 USPSTF 2023 folic acid reaffirmation (Grade A)

**Citation.** US Preventive Services Task Force; Barry MJ, Nicholson WK, Silverstein M, et al. Folic Acid Supplementation to Prevent Neural Tube Defects: US Preventive Services Task Force Reaffirmation Recommendation Statement. *JAMA*. 2023;330(5):454-459. DOI 10.1001/jama.2023.12876. PMID 37526713.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC; USPSTF web page fetched by curl.

**Recommendation (verbatim):** "The USPSTF recommends that all persons planning to or who could become pregnant take a daily supplement containing 0.4 to 0.8 mg (400 to 800 μg) of folic acid. (A recommendation)."

**Evidence assessment (verbatim):** "there is high certainty that folic acid supplementation has a substantial net benefit to prevent neural tube defects in their offspring."

**Scope (verbatim, web page):** "It does not apply to persons who have had a previous pregnancy affected by neural tube defects or who are at very high risk because of other factors (eg, use of certain antiseizure medications or family history). It does not apply to persons taking certain medications known to block the function of folic acid (eg, methotrexate, carbamazepine, and valproic acid)."

**Why "could become pregnant" (summary).** The neural tube closes very early, often before a pregnancy is known, which is why the recommendation covers anyone who could become pregnant rather than only those planning it. The web page's scope sentence confirms the wording; the embryology reasoning is my summary, not a quote.

#### 2.3 NHS: folic acid and vitamin D in pregnancy (UK)

**Source.** NHS, "Pregnancy vitamins and supplements", https://www.nhs.uk/pregnancy/keeping-well/pregnancy-vitamins-and-supplements/. Fetched by curl. "Page last reviewed: 3 June 2026".

Verbatim:
- "When you're trying to get pregnant (ideally 3 months before) or as soon as you find out you're pregnant, it's recommended you take:" followed by the bullet "400 micrograms of folic acid every day".
- "Take folic acid for the first 12 weeks of pregnancy. If you take it for longer than this, it will not harm your baby."
- Higher dose criteria: "It's recommended you take a higher dose of folic acid every day if you're planning a pregnancy or are less than 12 weeks pregnant and:" then the list "you or the baby's father has a neural tube defect", "you or the baby's father has a relative with a neural tube defect", "you have had a previous pregnancy affected by a neural tube defect", "you have diabetes", "you have a blood condition such as sickle cell anaemia or thalassaemia", "you take medicine for epilepsy or HIV". Then: "A higher dose is 5 milligrams (5mg) and this can be prescribed by a GP."
- Vitamin D in pregnancy: "10 micrograms of vitamin D every day between early October and late March, as we cannot make enough vitamin D from sunlight during this time"
- "Do not take cod liver oil or supplements containing vitamin A (sometimes called retinol) when you're pregnant. Too much vitamin A can harm your baby's development. Always check the label."

**US / UK difference to teach.** USPSTF 0.4 to 0.8 mg for the general population; NHS 400 micrograms (0.4 mg) standard and 5 mg for defined higher-risk groups. The two are consistent: the USPSTF explicitly excludes the higher-risk groups from its recommendation.

#### 2.4 Vitamin D: UK winter advice, infants (NHS, SACN 2016) and US infants (DGA)

**SACN 2016.** "SACN vitamin D and health report", https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/sacn-vitamin-d-and-health-report. Fetched by curl. "Published 21 July 2016", "Last updated 11 December 2023". (Report PDF not read; only the gov.uk summary page.)

Verbatim: "a reference nutrient intake (RNI) of 10 micrograms of vitamin D per day, throughout the year, for everyone in the general population aged 4 years and older"; "a ‘safe intake’ of 8.5 to 10 micrograms per day for all infants from birth to 1 year of age"; "a ‘safe intake’ of 10 micrograms per day for children aged 1 to 4 years". And: "The RNI and safe intakes were developed to ensure that the majority of the UK population has enough vitamin D to protect musculoskeletal health, all year round." And: "SACN did not take account of sunlight exposure in making recommendations because of the number and complexity of factors that affect skin synthesis of vitamin D."

**NHS vitamin D page.** https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/vitamins-and-minerals/vitamin-d/. Fetched by curl. **"Page last reviewed: 03 August 2020", "Next review due: 03 August 2023"**: the page is overdue for review, so check it before a lesson relies on it.

Verbatim:
- "Government advice is that everyone should consider taking a daily vitamin D supplement during the autumn and winter."
- "People at high risk of not getting enough vitamin D, all children aged 1 to 4, and all babies (unless they're having more than 500ml of infant formula a day) should take a daily supplement throughout the year."
- "But between October and early March we do not make enough vitamin D from sunlight."
- "In the UK, cows' milk is generally not a good source of vitamin D because it is not fortified, as it is in some other countries."
- Upper limits: "Do not take more than 100 micrograms (4,000 IU) of vitamin D a day as it could be harmful. This applies to adults, including pregnant and breastfeeding women and the elderly, and children aged 11 to 17 years." and "Children aged 1 to 10 years should not have more than 50 micrograms (2,000 IU) a day. Infants under 12 months should not have more than 25 micrograms (1,000 IU) a day."
- Dark skin: "If you have dark skin – for example you have an African, African-Caribbean or south Asian background – you may also not make enough vitamin D from sunlight." and "You should consider taking a daily supplement containing 10 micrograms of vitamin D throughout the year."

**Note on a discrepancy.** NHS gives the UL for children 1 to 10 as 50 micrograms; NIH ODS gives 63 mcg (1 to 3 y) and 75 mcg (4 to 8 y). These are different agencies' values (UK versus US Institute of Medicine 2011), not an error. A lesson should say which country's figure it is using.

**US infants, Dietary Guidelines 2025-2030** (PDF, see 3.8 for source details). Verbatim: "All breastfed infants, as well as infants who consume less than 32 ounces of infant formula per day, should receive a daily oral vitamin D supplement of 400 IU starting shortly after birth." And: "Some infants require iron supplementation. Talk with your health care professional about iron supplementation."

#### 2.5 Vitamin B12 for vegans and older adults

Covered by ODS quotations in 1.6. The 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines add (verbatim): "Pay careful attention to potential nutrient gaps when consuming a vegetarian or vegan diet." and "To avoid nutrient gaps, prioritize targeted supplementation, diversify plant protein sources for amino acid balance, and enhance mineral bioavailability through food preparation techniques." and "Monitor nutrient status periodically, especially for iron, vitamin B12, vitamin D, calcium, and iodine."

#### 2.6 Iron in deficiency

Covered by ODS in 1.6. Summary for the lesson: iron supplementation is appropriate for diagnosed iron deficiency and as advised in pregnancy and some infants; it is inappropriate as a blind precaution because of the UL, child poisoning risk, and haemochromatosis. The ODS page explicitly says a doctor may prescribe above the UL for a while to treat deficiency (verbatim: "A doctor might prescribe more than the upper limit of iron to people who need higher doses for a while to treat iron deficiency.").

---

### PART 3. Alcohol: the contested J-curve

#### 3.0 The shape of the dispute (summary)

Observational cohorts repeatedly find that light drinkers have lower all-cause mortality and lower heart attack risk than non-drinkers (the *J-curve*). The dispute is whether that is causal. Three lines of argument say it is largely not: (1) *abstainer bias* or the *sick quitter* problem, where non-drinker groups include people who stopped because of illness; (2) healthy-lifestyle confounding, where light drinkers are richer, more active and more socially connected; (3) Mendelian randomisation studies, which use genes that affect drinking and tend not to find the protective dip. The other side argues that the association survives when former drinkers are separated out, that it is biologically plausible (HDL cholesterol, clotting), and that it appears for heart attack specifically across many cohorts. Both sides agree that alcohol causes several cancers and that heavy drinking is harmful. No randomised trial of long-term moderate drinking has reported outcomes; the one that was started (MACH15) was stopped (3.9).

#### 3.1 Zhao J et al 2023, JAMA Network Open (the "no benefit" meta-analysis)

**Citation.** Zhao J, Stockwell T, Naimi T, Churchill S, Clay J, Sherk A. Association Between Daily Alcohol Intake and Risk of All-Cause Mortality: A Systematic Review and Meta-analyses. *JAMA Netw Open*. 2023;6(3):e236185. DOI 10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2023.6185. PMID 37000449.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC. (A first fetch was corrupted by my own HTML-stripping script, which treated "<56" as a tag; the version in the appendix is from a corrected refetch and is complete.)

**Design.** Systematic review and meta-analysis; 107 cohort studies, 1980 to July 2021; 4,838,825 participants; 425,564 deaths; 724 risk estimates. Mixed linear regression models adjusting for study-level biases, including former drinker bias.

**Key numbers (adjusted, versus lifetime non-drinkers).** Occasional drinkers (more than 0 to under 1.3 g ethanol/day): RR 0.96 (95% CI 0.86 to 1.06). Low-volume (1.3 to 24.0 g/day): RR 0.93, P = .07 (no CI in the abstract). 25 to 44 g/day: RR 1.05, P = .28. 45 to 64 g/day: RR 1.19; 65 g/day or more: RR 1.35, both P < .001. Female drinkers versus female lifetime non-drinkers: RR 1.22, P = .03 (the abstract does not state the intake level for this comparison).

**Conclusion (verbatim):** "In this updated systematic review and meta-analysis, daily low or moderate alcohol intake was not significantly associated with all-cause mortality risk, while increased risk was evident at higher consumption levels, starting at lower levels for women than men."

**Reading carefully.** The low-volume point estimate is still below 1 (0.93) with P = .07. The honest statement is *no statistically significant reduction*, not *proven no benefit*. A lesson that says Zhao showed moderate drinking *has no benefit* overstates it slightly.

**Predecessor.** Stockwell T, Zhao J, Panwar S, Roemer A, Naimi T, Chikritzhs T. Do "Moderate" Drinkers Have Reduced Mortality Risk? A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Alcohol Consumption and All-Cause Mortality. *J Stud Alcohol Drugs*. 2016;77(2):185-198. DOI 10.15288/jsad.2016.77.185. PMID 26997174. Abstract read in full. This is the clearest demonstration of the mechanism of the dispute. Unadjusted, 87 studies reproduced the J-curve: low-volume drinkers RR 0.86 (0.83 to 0.90); former drinkers RR 1.22 (1.14 to 1.31). After adjustment: low-volume RR 0.97 (0.88 to 1.07). Verbatim: "However, low-volume drinkers may appear healthy only because the "abstainers" with whom they are compared are biased toward ill health."

#### 3.2 GBD 2016 Alcohol Collaborators, Lancet 2018 ("zero")

**Citation.** GBD 2016 Alcohol Collaborators. Alcohol use and burden for 195 countries and territories, 1990-2016: a systematic analysis for the Global Burden of Disease Study 2016. *Lancet*. 2018;392(10152):1015-1035. DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(18)31310-2. PMID 30146330.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Modelling study: 694 consumption data sources and 592 risk studies; new meta-analysis of relative risks for 23 outcomes; standard drink defined as 10 g ethanol.

**Key numbers.** Alcohol was the seventh leading risk factor for deaths and DALYs in 2016; 2.2% (1.5 to 3.0) of age-standardised female deaths and 6.8% (5.8 to 8.0) of male deaths. Leading risk factor among ages 15 to 49. Verbatim: "The level of alcohol consumption that minimised harm across health outcomes was zero (95% UI 0·0-0·8) standard drinks per week."

**Interpretation (verbatim):** "We found that the risk of all-cause mortality, and of cancers specifically, rises with increasing levels of consumption, and the level of consumption that minimises health loss is zero."

**Note.** The phrase *no safe level* is how this paper was widely reported. The abstract itself says "the level of consumption that minimises health loss is zero", which is a population-weighted minimum, not the words *no safe level*. Quote the abstract, not the headline. The upper uncertainty bound was 0.8 drinks per week.

#### 3.3 GBD 2020 Alcohol Collaborators, Lancet 2022 (varies by age)

**Citation.** GBD 2020 Alcohol Collaborators. Population-level risks of alcohol consumption by amount, geography, age, sex, and year: a systematic analysis for the Global Burden of Disease Study 2020. *Lancet*. 2022;400(10347):185-235. DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(22)00847-9. PMID 35843246.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Key findings.** Burden-weighted relative risk curves varied by region and age. Ages 15 to 39: the theoretical minimum risk exposure level (TMREL) ranged from 0 (0 to 0) to 0.603 (0.400 to 1.00) standard drinks per day. Ages 40 and over (verbatim): "the burden-weighted relative risk curve was J-shaped for all regions, with a 2020 TMREL that ranged from 0·114 (0-0·403) to 1·87 (0·500-3·30) standard drinks per day". Of people drinking harmful amounts in 2020, 59.1% were aged 15 to 39 and 76.9% were male.

**Interpretation (verbatim):** "There is strong evidence to support recommendations on alcohol consumption varying by age and location."

**Why this matters.** The same research group moved from *zero* (2018) to *J-shaped over 40* (2022). The change is methodological (weighting each outcome's risk curve by the local burden of that disease: where heart disease dominates deaths, as in older people, a small protective association for ischaemic heart disease pulls the curve down). This is a good worked example of how a modelling choice, not new biology, changes a headline. The J-shape in the 2022 paper still rests on the observational relative risk for ischaemic heart disease, which is the association Mendelian randomisation disputes.

#### 3.4 Mendelian randomisation

**(a) Millwood IY et al 2019, Lancet (China Kadoorie Biobank).**
Millwood IY, Walters RG, Mei XW, Guo Y, Yang L, Bian Z, Bennett DA, Chen Y, Dong C, Hu R, Zhou G, Yu B, Jia W, Parish S, Clarke R, Davey Smith G, Collins R, Holmes MV, Li L, Peto R, Chen Z; China Kadoorie Biobank Collaborative Group. Conventional and genetic evidence on alcohol and vascular disease aetiology: a prospective study of 500 000 men and women in China. *Lancet*. 2019;393(10183):1831-1842. DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(18)31772-0. PMID 30955975.

What I read: abstract in full via Europe PMC.

Design: prospective cohort of 512,715 adults, about 10 years' follow-up; 161,498 genotyped for ALDH2-rs671 and ADH1B-rs1229984, two variants common in East Asia that strongly affect drinking.

Findings: conventional analysis in men showed U-shaped associations for ischaemic stroke, intracerebral haemorrhage and MI, with the lowest risk at about 100 g/week. Genetic analysis did not. Stroke: RR per 280 g/week 1.58 (1.36 to 1.84) for intracerebral haemorrhage and 1.27 (1.13 to 1.43) for ischaemic stroke. MI: RR 0.96 (0.78 to 1.18), P = 0.69, i.e. no significant association either way.

Verbatim interpretation: "Genetic epidemiology shows that the apparently protective effects of moderate alcohol intake against stroke are largely non-causal. Alcohol consumption uniformly increases blood pressure and stroke risk, and appears in this one study to have little net effect on the risk of myocardial infarction."

Note the authors' own hedge: "in this one study" for MI. The strong conclusion is for stroke, not for heart attack.

**(b) Holmes MV et al 2014, BMJ.** Association between alcohol and cardiovascular disease: Mendelian randomisation analysis based on individual participant data. *BMJ*. 2014;349:g4164. DOI 10.1136/bmj.g4164. PMID 25011450. Abstract read in full. 56 studies, 261,991 individuals of European descent. Carriers of the ADH1B A-allele drank 17.2% fewer units per week and had lower odds of coronary heart disease, OR 0.90 (0.84 to 0.96). Verbatim conclusion: "This suggests that reduction of alcohol consumption, even for light to moderate drinkers, is beneficial for cardiovascular health."

**(c) Biddinger KJ et al 2022, JAMA Netw Open.** Association of Habitual Alcohol Intake With Risk of Cardiovascular Disease. *JAMA Netw Open*. 2022;5(3):e223849. DOI 10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2022.3849. PMID 35333364. Abstract read in full. UK Biobank, 371,463 participants; linear and non-linear MR. Verbatim: "Light to moderate alcohol consumption was associated with healthier lifestyle factors, adjustment for which attenuated the cardioprotective epidemiologic associations with modest intake." And: "light alcohol intake was associated with minimal increases in cardiovascular risk, whereas heavier consumption was associated with exponential increases in risk".

**Limitations of MR (my summary, not from these abstracts).** MR assumes the genetic variant affects the outcome only through alcohol (no pleiotropy). ADH1B and ALDH2 variants plausibly act through acetaldehyde as well as through amount drunk. Linear MR cannot easily detect a J-shape at low doses; non-linear MR methods (Biddinger) have their own contested assumptions. The ICCPUD draft (3.7) cites MR as decisive; NASEM (3.6) excluded it by design. That difference in inclusion criteria explains most of the disagreement between the two US reports.

#### 3.5 Wood AM et al 2018, Lancet (thresholds in current drinkers)

**Citation.** Wood AM, Kaptoge S, Butterworth AS, et al; Emerging Risk Factors Collaboration/EPIC-CVD/UK Biobank Alcohol Study Group. Risk thresholds for alcohol consumption: combined analysis of individual-participant data for 599 912 current drinkers in 83 prospective studies. *Lancet*. 2018;391(10129):1513-1523. DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(18)30134-X. PMID 29676281.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC.

**Design.** Individual participant data, 599,912 current drinkers, 83 studies, 19 high-income countries. Analysis limited to current drinkers (so it does not compare with non-drinkers and sidesteps abstainer bias, at the cost of not answering "drink or not").

**Key numbers.** Minimum all-cause mortality around or below 100 g/week. Per 100 g/week: stroke HR 1.14 (1.10 to 1.17); heart failure 1.09 (1.03 to 1.15); fatal hypertensive disease 1.24 (1.15 to 1.33); but MI HR 0.94 (0.91 to 0.97). Compared with more than 0 to 100 g/week, drinking more than 100 to 200, more than 200 to 350, and more than 350 g/week was associated with roughly 6 months, 1 to 2 years, and 4 to 5 years lower life expectancy at age 40.

**Interpretation (verbatim):** "These data support limits for alcohol consumption that are lower than those recommended in most current guidelines."

**Why include it.** It shows the two faces of the data in one paper: alcohol raises most cardiovascular outcomes roughly linearly but is associated with lower MI risk. The *protective* argument lives in that MI line; the *harm* argument lives in everything else.

#### 3.6 NASEM, "Review of Evidence on Alcohol and Health" (Dec 2024): the best statement of the side that finds moderate drinking associated with lower mortality

**Citation.** National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine. *Review of Evidence on Alcohol and Health*. Washington, DC: The National Academies Press; 2025. DOI 10.17226/28582. Prepublication released December 2024 (the Highlights PDF is dated "DECEMBER 2024"; the chapter citation says 2025).

**What I read.** The 5-page Consensus Study Report Highlights PDF (https://nap.nationalacademies.org/resource/28582/Highlights_Alcohol_and_Health.pdf) in full; Chapter 3 "All-Cause Mortality" read via https://www.nationalacademies.org/read/28582/chapter/5 (findings, conclusion and summary sections; not every table).

**Remit and definitions (verbatim, Highlights):** "the committee focused on data related to moderate alcohol consumption, which is defined as consuming alcoholic beverages up to the limit specified by the DGA (i.e., two drinks or 28 grams of alcohol in a day for men and one drink or 14 grams of alcohol in a day for women)." And: "An important requirement for this review, unlike other reviews, was to have a comparison group that separated never drinkers from former drinkers to avoid “abstainer bias.”" And: the report "presents the committee’s findings and conclusions and does not offer dietary recommendations or advice."

Study limits: studies before 2010 were excluded; the Highlights say "No conclusions in the report were issued with high certainty." Systematic reviews were carried out for the committee by the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics Evidence Practice Center.

**All-cause mortality (verbatim, Chapter 3):**
- "Finding 3-1: On the basis of a meta-analysis of eight eligible studies, there was a 16 percent lower risk of all-cause mortality among those who consumed moderate levels of alcohol compared with those who never consumed alcohol (RR = 0.84, 95%CI [0.81, 0.87])."
- "Conclusion 3-1: Based on data from the eight eligible studies from 2019 to 2023, the committee concludes that compared with never consuming alcohol, moderate alcohol consumption is associated with lower all-cause mortality (moderate certainty)."
- A caveat the committee itself states: "the comparison mainly reflected alcohol consumption toward the lower end of the range defined as moderate consumption versus never consuming alcohol."
- "Finding 3-4: On the basis of a meta-analysis of five studies published between 2019 and 2023, the committee found that, among moderate alcohol consumers, higher versus lower amounts of moderate alcohol consumption were associated with similar risks of all-cause mortality (RR = 0.96, 95%CI [0.87, 1.06])."
- Risk of bias: "The five studies with data for this analysis were determined to have “some concerns” about risk of bias, primarily due to confounding, and one study was at high risk of bias due concerns about both confounding and bias due to missing data (Table 3-3)."

**Cancer (verbatim, Highlights):** "Compared with never consuming alcohol, consuming a moderate amount of alcohol was associated with a higher risk of breast cancer (see Conclusion 5-1 in the report)." (moderate certainty). Also: "Alcohol has been identified as a carcinogen in humans, and associations of alcohol with cancer are likely linear". No conclusion could be drawn for colorectal cancer versus never drinkers, or for oral cavity, pharyngeal, oesophageal or laryngeal cancers.

**Cardiovascular (verbatim, Highlights):** "Compared with never consuming alcohol, consuming moderate amounts of alcohol is associated with a lower risk of CVD mortality in both females and males (see Conclusion 6-3 in the report)." (moderate certainty). Lower risk of non-fatal MI and non-fatal stroke: low certainty.

**Why this is the best statement of that side.** It is a congressionally mandated consensus report, it separated never drinkers from former drinkers (the main methodological objection to older J-curve studies), it used USPSTF-style certainty grading, and it did find higher breast cancer risk, so it is not a one-sided document. Its limits: observational studies only, only studies from 2010 (and 2019 to 2023 for the mortality meta-analysis), only eight studies in the key analysis, the effective comparison is light drinking versus never drinking, and residual confounding (healthy-drinker effects) is not removed by separating former drinkers. Mendelian randomisation was not part of its evidence base. The report was sponsored by USDA.

#### 3.7 ICCPUD, "Alcohol Intake and Health" study (draft, January 2025): the best statement of the side that finds risk beginning at low levels

**Citation.** Shield K, Keyes KM, Martinez P, Milam AJ, Naimi TS, Rehm J. *Draft Report: Scientific Findings of the Alcohol Intake & Health Study for Public Comment*. Interagency Coordinating Committee on the Prevention of Underage Drinking (ICCPUD), SAMHSA/HHS; January 2025. PDF: https://www.samhsa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-draft-public-comment-alcohol-intake-health-study.pdf.

**What I read.** The PDF was downloaded and converted; I read the Summary (pages 3 to 4) and the introduction on protective effects in full, and searched the rest. SAMHSA landing page (https://www.samhsa.gov/substance-use/prevention/iccpud/alcohol-intake-health-study) fetched; it says "Released on January 15, 2025" and "Last Updated: 05/16/2025".

**Key statements (verbatim, Summary):**
- "Among the U.S. population, the risk of dying from alcohol use begins at low levels of average use. Higher levels of alcohol consumption are linked with progressively higher mortality risk."
- "In the United States, males and females have a 1 in 1000 risk of dying from alcohol use if they consume more than 7 drinks per week. This risk increases to 1 in 100 if they consume more than 9 drinks per week."
- "Males and females who consumed 1 drink per day had an increased risk of liver cirrhosis, esophageal cancer, oral cancer, and injuries, but a lower risk for ischemic stroke. In addition, females had a higher risk for liver cancer and a lower risk for diabetes mellitus when they drank 1 drink per day."
- "Alcohol use is associated with increased mortality for seven types of cancer (colorectal, female breast, liver, oral cavity, pharynx, larynx, esophagus [squamous cell type]). Increased risk for these cancers begins with any alcohol use and increases with higher levels of use."

**Its own statement of method limits (verbatim):** "Most of the evidence evaluated in this report is from systematic reviews and meta-analyses of “observational” cohort studies. It did not include data from quasi-experimental (e.g., Mendelian randomization) studies or randomized controlled trials."

**But on MR (verbatim, introduction):** "However, Mendelian randomization studies, including those using advanced methodologies, find no protection at low levels of consumption for ischemic heart disease, ischemic stroke, or diabetes mellitus (26-28)." So MR was cited in framing even though it was not part of the modelled evidence.

**Fate of the report (recent change, summary).** Per VinePair (5 September 2025, read via WebFetch summary, not verbatim-checked), study authors said they were told in August 2025 that the administration did not intend to publish the final report, and the House Appropriations Committee announced it would defund ICCPUD. The House Oversight Committee majority published a report in January 2026 calling the study biased (**NOT READ: search-summary level only**; title as listed in search results: *A Study Fraught with Bias: How the Biden Administration's Alcohol Intake and Health Study Tried to Undermine the 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans*). Critics' main points, as far as I can tell from search results only: overlap between the report's authors and advocates of lower drinking guidelines (for example Naimi is also an author of Zhao 2023) and the use of lifetime-risk thresholds (1 in 1,000) that are stricter than those usually applied. I did not read these critiques and a writer should read them before summarising them. **The only published version remains the January 2025 draft.**

**What would settle it (summary).** Both reports agree that alcohol causes several cancers and that heavy drinking is harmful. They disagree on whether the lower mortality seen in light drinkers is causal. Evidence that would settle it: a long, large randomised trial of light drinking versus abstinence with hard outcomes (MACH15 was the attempt; see 3.9); non-linear MR with robust instruments in several ancestries that agree with each other; or natural experiments. Until then, a fair lesson says the heart attack question is genuinely open, the cancer question is not, and the net answer depends on a person's age and baseline risks, which is exactly what GBD 2022 said.

#### 3.8 What the 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans say about alcohol (recent change)

**Source.** *Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025–2030*. HHS and USDA. PDF https://cdn.realfood.gov/DGA.pdf (linked from https://realfood.gov/). 10 pages, PDF metadata created 6 January 2026; footer "January 2026". Downloaded and converted; alcohol, pregnancy, infant and vegetarian sections read in full.

**Verbatim (the whole alcohol section):** "Limit Alcoholic Beverages" / "Consume less alcohol for better overall health." / "People who should completely avoid alcohol include pregnant women, people who are recovering from alcohol use disorder or are unable to control the amount they drink, and people taking medications or with medical conditions that can interact with alcohol. For those with a family history of alcoholism, be mindful of alcohol consumption and associated addictive behaviors."

**What changed (summary).** The 2020-2025 DGA advised adults who drink to limit intake to 2 drinks or fewer a day for men and 1 or fewer for women (this earlier wording is confirmed by the Surgeon General's advisory quoted in 3.10: "the 2020-2025 U.S. Dietary Guidelines for Americans recommended limits of two drinks daily for men and one drink daily for women"). The 2025-2030 document gives no number and does not mention cancer. Press and professional bodies (AASLD, among others) criticised the removal of numeric limits (**NOT READ: search-summary level only**). Brewers and distillers' associations welcomed the guidelines (search-summary level only). A neutral lesson should report both reactions without adjectives and point out that "Consume less alcohol for better overall health." is itself a statement that less is better, which is more cautious than the old wording's implicit acceptance of up to 1 or 2 drinks.

#### 3.9 The trial that would have helped: MACH15, stopped 2018

**Source.** NIH news release, "NIH to end funding for Moderate Alcohol and Cardiovascular Health trial", Friday, June 15, 2018. nih.gov blocked curl; fetched from an Internet Archive raw snapshot (2025).

Verbatim: "The National Institutes of Health plans to end funding to the Moderate Alcohol and Cardiovascular Health (MACH) trial. The decision is based on concerns about the study design that cast doubt on its ultimate credibility. This includes whether the study would effectively address other significant consequences of moderate alcohol intake, such as cancer."

Design (verbatim): "The MACH study was designed as a multicenter, randomized clinical trial to determine the effects of one serving of alcohol (approximately 15 grams) daily, compared to no alcohol intake, on the rate of new cases of cardiovascular disease and the rate of new cases of diabetes among participants free of diabetes at baseline."

Funding (verbatim): NIAAA "expected to commit $20 million to the overall project over 10 years, of which $4 million has been spent, and in part by private donations of $67.7 million raised to date by the FNIH". The private donations came largely from alcohol companies (my summary from search results; the release excerpt I read does not name the donors).

Teaching note: the one attempt at the settling experiment was compromised by industry funding and the way it was solicited. This is a strong case study for the course's lesson on funding and conflicts of interest.

#### 3.10 US Surgeon General's Advisory, "Alcohol and Cancer Risk" (January 2025)

**Source.** Office of the Surgeon General (Vivek H. Murthy), *Alcohol and Cancer Risk: The U.S. Surgeon General's Advisory*, January 2025. Web page https://www.hhs.gov/surgeongeneral/reports-and-publications/alcohol-cancer/index.html ("Content last reviewed January 17, 2025"; fetched from Internet Archive snapshot of 14 September 2026 because hhs.gov blocked curl). PDF https://www.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/oash-alcohol-cancer-risk.pdf downloaded from the Archive and converted; Summary, section 1, absolute-risk section and recommendations read in full.

Verbatim:
- "This advisory highlights alcohol use as a leading preventable cause of cancer in the United States, contributing to nearly 100,000 cancer cases and about 20,000 cancer deaths each year."
- "This body of scientific evidence demonstrates a causal relationship between alcohol use and increased risk for at least seven different types of cancer, including breast (in women), colorectum, esophagus, liver, mouth (oral cavity), throat (pharynx), and voice box (larynx)."
- "The more alcohol consumed, the greater the risk of cancer. For certain cancers, like breast, mouth, and throat cancers, evidence shows that this risk may start to increase around one or fewer drinks per day." (superscript reference numbers removed)
- "Alcohol consumption is the third leading preventable cause of cancer in the United States, after tobacco and obesity."
- "About 83% of the estimated 20,000 U.S. alcohol-related cancer deaths per year occur at levels above the 2020-2025 U.S. Dietary Guidelines for Americans recommended limits of two drinks daily for men and one drink daily for women. Nonetheless, the remaining 17% of the estimated 20,000 U.S. alcohol-related cancer deaths per year occur at levels within those recommended limits." (superscripts removed)
- Awareness: "In a 2019 survey, 45% of Americans recognized alcohol use as a risk factor for cancer compared to 91% of Americans who recognized the risk of radiation exposure, 89% for tobacco use, 81% for asbestos exposure, and 53% for obesity"
- Absolute risk, women: "the absolute risk of developing any alcohol-related cancer over the lifespan of a woman increases from approximately 16.5% (about 17 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume less than one drink per week, to 19.0% (19 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume one drink daily on average to approximately 21.8% (about 22 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume two drinks daily on average"
- Absolute risk, men: "the absolute risk of developing any alcohol-related cancer increases from approximately 10.0% (about 10 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume less than one drink per week to about 11.4% (about 11 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume one drink daily on average to approximately 13.1% (about 13 out of every 100 individuals) for those who consume two drinks daily on average"
- Method note in the footnote to those figures (verbatim): "For these models, the reference group included very light drinkers, but not non-drinkers to avoid the sick quitter effect." The absolute risks derive from the Australian 45 and Up cohort (n = 226,162; Sarich 2021).
- Recommendations include: "Update the existing Surgeon General’s health warning label on alcohol-containing beverages to include a warning about the risk of cancer associated with alcohol consumption." and "Reassess recommended limits for alcohol consumption to account for the latest evidence on alcohol consumption and cancer risk."

**Teaching note.** The absolute-risk figures are the best teaching material in this topic: the same data read as *a 15% relative increase* or *about 2 to 3 extra women in 100 per daily drink* (my arithmetic from the quoted figures). As of the DGA 2025-2030 release, the warning label had not been changed by Congress (my understanding; I did not verify the label's current status against a primary source).

#### 3.11 WHO Europe statement (January 2023)

**Source.** WHO Regional Office for Europe, news item "No level of alcohol consumption is safe for our health", 4 January 2023, https://www.who.int/europe/news/item/04-01-2023-no-level-of-alcohol-consumption-is-safe-for-our-health. Fetched by curl. It summarises a WHO statement published in *The Lancet Public Health* (the Lancet piece itself not read).

Verbatim: "The World Health Organization has now published a statement in The Lancet Public Health: when it comes to alcohol consumption, there is no safe amount that does not affect health." And: "around half of all alcohol-attributable cancers in the WHO European Region occur among people whose alcohol consumption does not meet the definition of heavy drinking." And a quotation attributed to Dr Jürgen Rehm (who is also an ICCPUD author): "Potential protective effects of alcohol consumption, suggested by some studies, are tightly connected with the comparison groups chosen and the statistical methods used, and may not consider other relevant factors".

**Note.** This is where the phrase *no safe level* genuinely comes from in official language.

#### 3.12 Canada's Guidance on Alcohol and Health (2023)

**Source.** Canadian Centre on Substance Use and Addiction (CCSA), https://www.ccsa.ca/canadas-guidance-alcohol-and-health. Fetched by curl. The final report (Paradis C et al, 2023) was not read; only the landing page.

Verbatim key points:
- "There is a continuum of risk associated with weekly alcohol use where the risk of harm is:"
- "0 drinks per week — Not drinking has benefits, such as better health, and better sleep."
- "2 standard drinks or less per week — You are likely to avoid alcohol-related consequences for yourself or others at this level."
- "3–6 standard drinks per week — Your risk of developing several types of cancer, including breast and colon cancer, increases at this level."
- "7 standard drinks or more per week — Your risk of heart disease or stroke increases significantly at this level."
- "Consuming more than 2 standard drinks per occasion is associated with an increased risk of harms to self and others, including injuries and violence."
- "When pregnant or trying to get pregnant, there is no known safe amount of alcohol use."
- "When breastfeeding, not drinking alcohol is safest."
- "No matter where you are on the continuum, for your health, less alcohol is better."
- On the framing: "The guidance is based on the principle of autonomy in harm reduction and the fundamental idea behind it that people living in Canada have a right to know that all alcohol use comes with risk."
- Compared with 2011: "In 2011, people were provided with numerical limits for weekly and daily amounts of alcohol use. In comparison, the 2023 guidance recommends people consider reducing their alcohol use."

**Not verified.** The size of a Canadian standard drink (commonly given as 13.45 g of ethanol) is not on this page; check the final report before stating it. Note that the page lists both "2 standard drinks or less per week" and, further down, "1–2 drinks a week represents a low risk of harms"; these are the same threshold phrased two ways.

#### 3.13 UK Chief Medical Officers' low risk drinking guidelines (2016)

**Source.** Department of Health and Social Care, *UK Chief Medical Officers’ Low Risk Drinking Guidelines*, August 2016 (guidelines took effect 8 January 2016). gov.uk page https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/alcohol-consumption-advice-on-low-risk-drinking ("Published 25 August 2016"; no later update listed). PDF https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a80b7ed40f0b623026951db/UK_CMOs__report.pdf downloaded and converted; weekly, single-occasion and pregnancy sections read.

Verbatim:
- "To keep health risks from alcohol to a low level it is safest not to drink more than 14 units a week on a regular basis."
- "If you regularly drink as much as 14 units per week, it is best to spread your drinking evenly over 3 or more days."
- On benefits (the UK's statement of the J-curve, as of 2016): "That the net benefits from small amounts of alcohol are less than previously thought (with substantial uncertainties around the level of protection) and are significant in only a limited part of the population. That is women over the age of 55, for whom the maximum benefit is gained when drinking around 5 units a week, with some beneficial effect up to around 14 units a week."
- On cancer: "there is no level of regular drinking that can be considered as completely safe in relation to some cancers."
- The risk basis: "if people drink at or above the low risk level advised, overall any protective effect from alcohol on deaths is cancelled out and the risk of dying from an alcohol-related condition would then be expected to be at least 1% over a lifetime."
- Pregnancy: "If you are pregnant or think you could become pregnant, the safest approach is not to drink alcohol at all, to keep risks to your baby to a minimum."
- Heavy drinkers: "Very heavy drinkers, who are likely to experience alcohol withdrawal, are advised to seek medical advice before they stop drinking." (This belongs in the safety lesson: stopping suddenly can itself be dangerous for dependent drinkers.)

NHS units page (https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/alcohol-advice/calculating-alcohol-units/, fetched by curl) states: "14 units is equivalent to 6 pints of average-strength beer or 10 small glasses of lower-strength wine."

**Comparing thresholds (my arithmetic, check before publishing).** UK unit = 8 g ethanol, so 14 units = 112 g/week. US standard drink = 14 g, so the old US limit for men (2/day) = 196 g/week and for women (1/day) = 98 g/week. Wood 2018 found the mortality minimum around or below 100 g/week, close to the UK limit. The ICCPUD 1-in-1,000 threshold (7 drinks/week) = 98 g/week. Canada's "moderate risk" band starts at 3 drinks/week.

---

### PART 4. Pregnancy

#### 4.1 FDA/EPA Advice about Eating Fish

**Source.** FDA, "Advice about Eating Fish", https://www.fda.gov/food/consumers/advice-about-eating-fish. Fetched by curl. "Content current as of: 03/05/2024".

Verbatim:
- "This advice can help those who might become or are pregnant or breastfeeding as well as parents and caregivers who are feeding children make informed choices when it comes to the types of fish that are nutritious and safe to eat."
- "Fish intake during pregnancy is recommended because moderate scientific evidence shows it can help your baby’s cognitive development."
- "Those who are pregnant or breastfeeding consume between 8 and 12 ounces per week of a variety of seafood from choices that are lower in mercury."
- Serving guidance: "Pregnancy and breastfeeding:" / "1 serving is 4 ounces" / "Eat 2 to 3 servings a week from the "Best Choices" list" / "(OR 1 serving from the "Good Choices" list)."
- "Choice to Avoid: HIGHEST MERCURY LEVELS" is listed for: King mackerel, Marlin, Orange roughy, Shark, Swordfish, Tilefish (Gulf of Mexico), Tuna, bigeye.

**Teaching note.** The advice is to eat fish, not to avoid it: pregnancy advice that only lists what to avoid misses the benefit the FDA states first.

#### 4.2 NHS, Foods to avoid in pregnancy

**Source.** https://www.nhs.uk/pregnancy/keeping-well/foods-to-avoid/. Fetched by curl. "Page last reviewed: 15 June 2026".

Verbatim highlights:
- Opening: "Most foods and drinks are safe to have during pregnancy. But there are some you should be careful with or avoid."
- Dairy to avoid: "unpasteurised milk, cream, yoghurt, butter and ice cream (including cows', sheep's and goats' milk)"; reason: "Listeriosis can cause miscarriage or stillbirth, or make your newborn baby very unwell."
- Meat to avoid: "raw or undercooked meat", "liver and liver products", "game meats such as goose, partridge, pheasant and venison". Reasons: "There's a small risk of getting toxoplasmosis if you eat raw, undercooked or cured meat, which can cause miscarriage." and "Liver and foods containing liver have high levels of vitamin A, which can be harmful to your baby." and "Game meats may contain lead, which can also be harmful for your baby."
- Eggs: "hen eggs that have the red British Lion stamp or are produced under the Laid in Britain scheme can be eaten raw, partially cooked (runny yolk), or well-cooked, as long as they're eaten before the use-by date"
- Fish to limit: "tuna – you should not eat more than 4 cans or 2 tuna steaks a week" and "oily fish including salmon, trout, mackerel, sardines and herring – you should not eat more than 2 portions a week". Fish to avoid: "raw fish and raw shellfish", "sushi made with raw fish (even if it has been previously frozen) or raw shellfish", "swordfish", "shark", "marlin".
- Caffeine: "you should not have more than 200mg of caffeine a day"; reason: "Having too much caffeine has been linked to a higher chance of miscarriage and stillbirth." Amounts listed: "100mg in a cup of instant coffee", "75mg in a cup of tea", "40mg in a can of cola", "80mg in a 250ml can of energy drink".
- Herbal teas: "avoid drinking more than 4 cups of herbal tea a day, and avoid teas containing liquorice root"
- Alcohol: "Drinking alcohol in pregnancy can cause long-term harm to your baby. It's safest to avoid alcohol."
- Reassurance: "If you've already had a small amount of food or drink that should be avoided during pregnancy, it's unlikely to affect your baby if you did not get ill."

**UK/US difference.** The NHS allows raw or runny British Lion eggs; US advice (not fetched here) generally does not. Raw-fish sushi: NHS says avoid; note the NHS wording changed in recent years, so check the page date when quoting.

#### 4.3 Caffeine in pregnancy: ACOG

**Source.** ACOG Committee Opinion No. 462, "Moderate Caffeine Consumption During Pregnancy", August 2010, https://www.acog.org/clinical/clinical-guidance/committee-opinion/articles/2010/08/moderate-caffeine-consumption-during-pregnancy. Fetched by curl. The page says "Reaffirmed 2026".

Verbatim abstract: "Moderate caffeine consumption (less than 200 mg per day) does not appear to be a major contributing factor in miscarriage or preterm birth. The relationship of caffeine to growth restriction remains undetermined. A final conclusion cannot be made at this time as to whether there is a correlation between high caffeine intake and miscarriage."

**Note the framing difference.** ACOG frames 200 mg as a level below which harm is not apparent; the NHS frames it as a ceiling linked to miscarriage risk; EFSA (7.1) says up to 200 mg/day in pregnancy does "not raise safety concerns for the foetus". All three land on the same number. The ACOG evidence base is from 2010 and was reaffirmed without rewriting; newer observational studies (not fetched) have argued for lower intakes, and a writer should check whether any 2024-2026 review changes the picture before presenting 200 mg as settled.

#### 4.4 Other pregnancy supplement points

See 2.2 and 2.3 (folic acid, vitamin D, no vitamin A/retinol or cod liver oil). DGA 2025-2030 (verbatim): "Pregnancy increases nutrient needs to support maternal health and fetal growth, with iron, folate, and iodine as top priorities." and "Women should talk to their health care professional about taking a daily prenatal vitamin during pregnancy."
