### PART 5. Eating disorders, and when nutrition stops being a self-help question

#### 5.1 NICE NG69, Eating disorders: recognition and treatment

**Source.** NICE guideline NG69, https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69 and https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69/chapter/Recommendations. Fetched by curl. "Published: 23 May 2017", "Last updated: 16 December 2020", overview "Last reviewed: 15 August 2024".

Verbatim recommendations:
- "1.2.1 People with eating disorders should be assessed and receive treatment at the earliest opportunity."
- "1.2.2 Early treatment is particularly important for those with or at risk of severe emaciation and such patients should be prioritised for treatment."
- "1.2.4 Although eating disorders can develop at any age, be aware that the risk is highest for young men and women between 13 and 17 years of age."
- "1.2.5 Do not use screening tools (for example, SCOFF) as the sole method to determine whether or not people have an eating disorder."
- 1.2.6 lists what to take into account when deciding whether to refer, including (verbatim items): "an unusually low or high body mass index (BMI) or body weight for their age", "rapid weight loss", "dieting or restrictive eating practices (such as dieting when they are underweight) that are worrying them, their family members or carers, or professionals", "family members or carers report a change in eating behaviour", "social withdrawal, particularly from situations that involve food", "a disproportionate concern about their weight or shape (for example, concerns about weight gain as a side effect of contraceptive medication)", "problems managing a chronic illness that affects diet, such as diabetes or coeliac disease", "menstrual or other endocrine disturbances, or unexplained gastrointestinal symptoms", "compensatory behaviours, including laxative or diet pill misuse, vomiting or excessive exercise", "atypical dental wear (such as erosion)", "whether they take part in activities associated with a high risk of eating disorders (for example, professional sport, fashion, dance, or modelling)."
- "1.2.7 Be aware that, in addition to the points in recommendation 1.2.6, children and young people with an eating disorder may also present with faltering growth (for example, a low weight or height for their age) or delayed puberty."
- "1.2.8 Do not use single measures such as BMI or duration of illness to determine whether to offer treatment for an eating disorder."
- "1.2.10 If an eating disorder is suspected after an initial assessment, refer immediately to a community-based, age-appropriate eating disorder service for further assessment or treatment."

**Teaching note.** 1.2.6 lists "an unusually low or high body mass index", so a person in a larger body can have an eating disorder; 1.2.8 says BMI alone must not decide treatment. Both matter for a course that also teaches weight management.

#### 5.2 NHS, Eating disorders overview

**Source.** https://www.nhs.uk/mental-health/feelings-symptoms-behaviours/behaviours/eating-disorders/overview/. Fetched by curl. "Page last reviewed: 23 January 2024".

Verbatim:
- "An eating disorder is a mental health condition where you use the control of food to cope with feelings and other situations."
- "Anyone can get an eating disorder, but teenagers and young adults are mostly affected."
- "With treatment, most people can recover from an eating disorder."
- "OSFED is the most common eating disorder."
- Symptoms (list items verbatim): "spending a lot of time worrying about your weight and body shape", "avoiding socialising when you think food will be involved", "eating very little food", "making yourself sick or taking laxatives after you eat", "exercising too much", "having very strict habits or routines around food", "changes in your mood, such as being withdrawn, anxious or depressed".
- Physical signs include "feeling cold, tired or dizzy", "feeling your heart racing, fainting or feeling faint", "your weight being very high or very low for someone of your age and height", "not getting your period or other delayed signs of puberty".
- Signs in someone else include "dramatic weight loss", "lying about how much they've eaten, when they've eaten, or their weight", "going to the bathroom a lot after eating", "avoiding eating with others", "wearing loose or baggy clothes to hide their weight loss".
- Where to go: "If you think you may have an eating disorder, see a GP as soon as you can." and "You can also talk in confidence to an adviser from eating disorders charity Beat by calling the Beat helpline on 0808 801 0677."
- For someone else: "Let them know you're worried about them and encourage them to see a GP. You could offer to go along with them."
- ARFID: "Beliefs about weight or body shape are not reasons why people develop ARFID."

#### 5.3 Helplines (status checked 2026-09-24)

**Beat (UK).** https://www.beateatingdisorders.org.uk/get-information-and-support/get-help-for-myself/i-need-support-now/helplines/. Fetched by curl today. Verbatim: "Our Helplines are open 3pm-8pm, Monday to Friday." Numbers: England "0808 801 0677"; Scotland "0808 801 0432"; Wales "0808 801 0433"; Northern Ireland "0808 801 0434". Also: "Calls to the Helpline are free from landlines and mobile phones within the UK and do not appear on itemised bills." (The page also carried a temporary notice about reduced hours on 29 September; do not copy that into a lesson.) Hours change; a lesson should link to the page rather than print hours.

**ANAD (US).** https://anad.org/get-support/eating-disorders-helpline/. Fetched by curl today. Number shown: "1-888-375-7767"; hours shown: "Monday-Friday, 9am-9pm CST". From https://anad.org/get-help/ (verbatim): "ANAD is a peer-run organization providing peer support. We do not provide therapy or anything medical." A lesson must say it is peer support, not clinical care.

**NEDA (US) helpline closure, 2023.** NPR, "National Eating Disorders Association phases out human helpline, pivots to chatbot", 31 May 2023, fetched by curl. Verbatim: "For more than 20 years, the National Eating Disorders Association (NEDA) has operated a phone line and online platform for people seeking help with anorexia, bulimia, and other eating disorders. Last year, nearly 70,000 individuals used the helpline." and "NEDA shuttered that service in May. Instead, the non-profit will use a chatbot called Tessa that was designed by eating disorder experts, with funding from NEDA." The chatbot was then taken offline around 30 to 31 May 2023 after reports it gave weight-loss advice (**search-summary level only**; NPR's article notes the page had been taken down and NEDA said it was being "updated"). NEDA's current help page blocked curl, so I could not confirm what NEDA offers today. **Do not list a NEDA helpline number in any lesson.** Point US readers to ANAD (peer support) and, in crisis, to 988 (the US Suicide and Crisis Lifeline; I did not fetch a 988 page, so verify before printing).

#### 5.4 Dieting as a risk factor for eating disorders (carefully sourced)

**(a) Patton GC, Selzer R, Coffey C, Carlin JB, Wolfe R.** Onset of adolescent eating disorders: population based cohort study over 3 years. *BMJ*. 1999;318(7186):765-768. DOI 10.1136/bmj.318.7186.765. PMID 10082698. Abstract read in full.

Design: cohort, 3 years, six waves; students aged 14 to 15 at start, 44 schools in Victoria, Australia (about 1,700: 888 female, 811 male at baseline). Outcome: partial-syndrome eating disorder by DSM-IV criteria.

Key findings (verbatim): "Female subjects who dieted at a severe level were 18 times more likely to develop an eating disorder than those who did not diet, and female subjects who dieted at a moderate level were five times more likely to develop an eating disorder than those who did not diet." And: "Psychiatric morbidity predicted the onset of eating disorder independently of dieting status so that those subjects in the highest morbidity category had an almost sevenfold increased risk of developing an eating disorder." Conclusion (verbatim): "Dieting is the most important predictor of new eating disorders."

Limitations (my summary): the outcome is partial syndromes, not full clinical diagnoses; association in an observational cohort, so dieting may be an early symptom as well as a cause; adolescents only; 1990s Australia. The abstract gives no confidence intervals for the 18x and 5x figures.

**(b) Neumark-Sztainer D, Wall M, Guo J, Story M, Haines J, Eisenberg M.** Obesity, disordered eating, and eating disorders in a longitudinal study of adolescents: how do dieters fare 5 years later? *J Am Diet Assoc*. 2006;106(4):559-568. DOI 10.1016/j.jada.2006.01.003. PMID 16567152. Abstract read in full.

Design: Project EAT, population-based, 5-year longitudinal, n = 2,516 adolescents.

Key findings: adolescents using unhealthful weight-control behaviours had about three times the odds of overweight 5 years later (OR 2.7 girls, 3.2 boys), higher odds of binge eating with loss of control (OR 6.4 girls, 5.9 boys) and of extreme weight-control behaviours (OR 2.5 girls, 4.8 boys). Conclusion (verbatim): "Dieting and unhealthful weight-control behaviors predict outcomes related to obesity and eating disorders 5 years later."

Limitations (my summary): self-report; *unhealthful weight-control behaviours* (e.g. fasting, skipping meals, diet pills) are not the same as a supervised dietary change; adolescents only; confounding by pre-existing body dissatisfaction is possible. The abstract gives no confidence intervals.

**How to state this in a lesson (my suggestion).** Adolescent dieting, especially severe or unhealthy dieting, is associated with later eating disorders and with later weight gain in cohort studies; the association is strong but observational. It does not show that supervised weight management in adults with obesity causes eating disorders, and the course should not imply that. The safe practical message is for the course's own design: avoid teaching calorie restriction to adolescents, avoid weight-focused framing, and flag warning signs.

#### 5.5 When to see a doctor or dietitian: official wording found

- **Unintentional weight loss.** NHS, https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/unintentional-weight-loss/ ("Page last reviewed: 28 July 2025"). Verbatim: "Unintentional weight loss is when you lose weight without changing your diet or exercise routine. It can be a sign of stress or a serious illness. See a GP just in case." And: "Sometimes weight loss and loss of appetite can be caused by cancer. It's important to get it checked if you're losing weight without trying." And: "See a GP if:" / "you keep losing weight without changing your diet or exercise routine".
- **Warfarin and vitamin K.** NHS, https://www.nhs.uk/medicines/warfarin/ ("Page last reviewed: 24 February 2026"). Verbatim: "Do not take the herbal remedy St John's wort or vitamin K supplements while you're taking warfarin." And: "Talk to your doctor before making any big changes to your diet when taking warfarin." And: "Foods high in vitamin K, such as broccoli, spinach and other green leafy vegetables, can affect how warfarin works. You can still eat these foods, but talk to your warfarin clinic for advice." Also: "do not have cranberry juice or cranberry products" and "do not drink grapefruit juice".
- **Other drug interactions** (ODS, quoted in 1.6 and 1.7): metformin and B12; statins and high-dose vitamin D; levothyroxine and levodopa with iron; St John's wort; antioxidants and chemotherapy.
- **Supplements in pregnancy and children** (ODS, quoted in 1.7).
- **Eating disorders** (NHS and NICE, 5.1 and 5.2).
- **Heavy drinkers stopping** (UK CMO, 3.13).
- **Pregnancy, food poisoning symptoms** (NHS foods-to-avoid page, verbatim): "Get help from 111 if you're pregnant and:" "you feel unwell after having food or drink that should be avoided in pregnancy" ... "you have symptoms of listeriosis or toxoplasmosis".
- **Diabetes, kidney disease, children's growth.** I did not fetch a specific official page telling people with diabetes or kidney disease to see a dietitian. NICE NG69 1.2.6 names "problems managing a chronic illness that affects diet, such as diabetes or coeliac disease" as a referral consideration for eating disorders, which is related but not the same. **Gap:** a later researcher should fetch NHS or NIDDK pages on diet in chronic kidney disease (where potassium, phosphorus and protein advice differs from general advice and generic advice to eat more vegetables and beans can be harmful) and on diabetes structured education, and NHS or AAP advice that children's diets should not be restricted without professional input.

### PART 6. Registered dietitian versus nutritionist

#### 6.1 United Kingdom

**HCPC.** "Professions and protected titles", https://www.hcpc-uk.org/about-us/who-we-regulate/the-professions/. Fetched by curl. Verbatim: "The professions we regulate have one or more designated titles that are protected by law." and "To use one of these titles, an individual must be registered with the HCPC." Under "Dietitians", protected titles listed: "Dietitian" and "Dietician". The page also says (verbatim): "We consider misuse of title cases, where individuals use one of our protected titles without being on our Register."

**British Dietetic Association.** "What is a dietitian?", https://www.bda.uk.com/about-dietetics/what-is-dietitian.html and "Journalist FAQs", https://www.bda.uk.com/news-campaigns/media/journalist-faqs.html. Both fetched by curl. Verbatim: "Yes, the title ‘dietitian’ is the only food and nutrition title that is protected by law. Only those registered with the statutory regulator, the Health & Care Professions Council (HCPC) can use the title." And: "Please note the titles ‘nutritionist’, ‘nutritional therapist’ and ‘diet expert’ are NOT protected and can be used by anyone regardless of training and/or qualification." And (the BDA's advice to the public): "check your health professional is registered with the HCPC or UK voluntary register for nutritionists."

**Summary.** In the UK, "dietitian" is legally protected; "nutritionist" is not, though a voluntary register exists (the UK Voluntary Register of Nutritionists, run by the Association for Nutrition; its own page was not fetched). The BDA is a professional body and has an interest in the distinction; the HCPC page is the neutral legal source.

#### 6.2 United States

**Commission on Dietetic Registration (CDR).** *2024 Graduate Degree Requirement*, https://www.cdrnet.org/graduatedegree. Fetched by curl. Verbatim: "Effective January 1, 2024, the minimum degree requirement for eligibility to take the registration examination for dietitians changed from a bachelor's degree to a graduate degree. All other didactic and supervised practice eligibility requirements remain the same." And: "Individuals who established eligibility for the registration examination for dietitians on or before December 31, 2023 or those who are already registered are not required by CDR to obtain a graduate degree."

**CDR RDN credential FAQ.** https://www.cdrnet.org/rdncredentialfaq. Fetched by curl. Verbatim: "This will differentiate the rigorous credential requirements and highlight that all registered dietitians are nutritionists but not all nutritionists are registered dietitians." And: "RDs have the option to use of the credential “registered dietitian nutritionist” (RDN)." (sic)

**State licensure (NOT READ: search-summary level only).** A search result from the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics' site stated that as of 2023, 48 states, Puerto Rico and DC had statutory provisions regulating dietitians and/or nutritionists, some with practice exclusivity (licensure) and others protecting only the title (certification). The CDR licensure map page did not render to text with curl. A writer must verify the number and wording on the Academy or CDR site before using it. The summary point that is safe to make: in the US, regulation is by state and varies; *registered dietitian* and *RDN* are national credentials from CDR; *nutritionist* is regulated in some states and not others.

**Teaching note (summary).** The course should give a reader two practical checks: in the UK, search the HCPC register; in the US, check the CDR credential and the state licence. Neither title guarantees good advice, but the protected one guarantees training, a code of conduct and a regulator to complain to.

### PART 7. Caffeine

#### 7.1 EFSA 2015 Scientific Opinion on the safety of caffeine

**Citation.** EFSA NDA Panel (EFSA Panel on Dietetic Products, Nutrition and Allergies). Scientific Opinion on the safety of caffeine. *EFSA Journal*. 2015;13(5):4102. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102 (metadata confirmed via Crossref).

**What I read.** The opinion itself (Wiley-hosted) blocked curl (Cloudflare) and Crossref has no abstract. I read EFSA's own topic page, https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/topics/topic/caffeine, fetched by curl, which summarises the opinion (dated 27 May 2015 on the page). Quotations are from that topic page, not from the opinion.

Verbatim (EFSA topic page):
- "Single doses of caffeine up to 200mg – about 3mg per kilogram of body weight (mg/kg bw) from all sources do not raise safety concerns for the general healthy adult population."
- "Single doses of 100mg (about 1.4mg/kg bw) of caffeine may affect sleep duration and patterns in some adults, particularly when consumed close to bedtime."
- "Intakes up to 400mg per day (about 5.7mg/kg bw per day) consumed throughout the day do not raise safety concerns for healthy adults in the general population, except pregnant women."
- "Caffeine intakes from all sources up to 200mg per day consumed throughout the day do not raise safety concerns for the foetus."
- Children: "A safety level of 3mg/kg bw per day is also proposed for habitual caffeine consumption by children and adolescents."
- Alcohol: "Up to these levels of intake, caffeine is unlikely to mask the subjective perception of alcohol intoxication."
- Portion values on the page include: "A cup of filter coffee (200ml)" 90mg.

#### 7.2 FDA, "Spilling the Beans: How Much Caffeine is Too Much?"

**Source.** https://www.fda.gov/consumers/consumer-updates/spilling-beans-how-much-caffeine-too-much. Fetched by curl. "Content current as of: 08/28/2024".

Verbatim:
- "For most adults, the FDA has cited 400 milligrams* a day — that's about two to three 12-fluid-ounce cups of coffee — as an amount not generally associated with negative effects. However, there is wide variation in both how sensitive people are to the effects of caffeine and how fast they eliminate it from the body."
- "If you are concerned about a condition or medication, or if you’re pregnant, trying to become pregnant, or breastfeeding, we recommend talking to your health care provider about whether you need to limit caffeine consumption."
- "Medical experts advise against energy drinks for children and teens because of the levels of sugar and caffeine, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics."
- "The FDA estimates toxic effects, like seizures, can be observed with rapid consumption of around 1,200 milligrams of caffeine, or less than 1/2 teaspoon of pure caffeine."
- "Pure and highly concentrated caffeine products can have serious health consequences, including death."
- "Generally, “energy drinks” have 54 to 328 milligrams of caffeine per 16 fluid ounces"
- "decaf coffee typically has 2 to 15 milligrams in an 8-fluid-ounce cup."
- "Caffeine withdrawal isn’t considered dangerous but can be unpleasant."
- "The FDA does not have a regulation specific to “energy drinks.”"

**Note.** The FDA does not give its own pregnancy number on this page; it defers to the health care provider. The 200 mg pregnancy figure in these notes comes from NHS, ACOG and EFSA.

### PART 8. Hydration and the "8 glasses a day" myth

#### 8.1 Valtin 2002

**Citation.** Valtin H. "Drink at least eight glasses of water a day." Really? Is there scientific evidence for "8 x 8"? *Am J Physiol Regul Integr Comp Physiol*. 2002;283(5):R993-R1004. DOI 10.1152/ajpregu.00365.2002. PMID 12376390.

**What I read.** Abstract in full via Europe PMC. Design: narrative review, including consultation with nutritionists.

Verbatim: "No scientific studies were found in support of 8 x 8." And the scope limit the author insists on: "It is to be emphasized that the conclusion is limited to healthy adults in a temperate climate leading a largely sedentary existence". And the counter-caution: "large intakes of fluid, equal to and greater than 8 x 8, are advisable for the treatment or prevention of some diseases and certainly are called for under special circumstances, such as vigorous work and exercise, especially in hot climates." And on caffeine: "caffeinated drinks (and, to a lesser extent, mild alcoholic beverages like beer in moderation) may indeed be counted toward the daily total".

**Limitation (stated by the author, verbatim):** "Since it is difficult or impossible to prove a negative-in this instance, the absence of scientific literature supporting the 8 x 8 recommendation-the author invites communications from readers who are aware of pertinent publications."

#### 8.2 NASEM (Institute of Medicine) 2004 water Adequate Intake

**Citation.** Institute of Medicine. *Dietary Reference Intakes for Water, Potassium, Sodium, Chloride, and Sulfate*. Washington, DC: The National Academies Press; 2005 (report released 2004). NAP record 10925.

**What I read.** Summary chapter and Chapter 4 "Water" opening sections via https://www.nationalacademies.org/read/10925/chapter/2 and /chapter/6, fetched by curl.

Verbatim:
- "The AI for total water intake for young men and women (ages 19 to 30 years) is 3.7 L and 2.7 L per day, respectively. Fluids (drinking water and beverages) provided 3.0 L (101 fluid oz; ≈ 13 cups) and 2.2 L (74 fluid oz; ≈ 9 cups) per day for 19- to 30-year-old men and women, respectively, representing approximately 81 percent of total water intake in the U.S. survey." (superscript footnote marker removed)
- "Because normal hydration can be maintained over a wide range of water intakes, the AI for total water (from a combination of drinking water, beverages, and food) is set based on the median total water intake from U.S. survey data."
- "As with AIs for other nutrients, for a healthy person, daily consumption below the AI may not confer additional risk because wide ranges of intakes are compatible with normal hydration. In this setting, the AI should not be interpreted as a specific requirement. Higher intakes of total water will be required for those who are physically active or who are exposed to hot environments."
- "However, on a day-to-day basis, fluid intake, driven by the combination of thirst and the consumption of beverages at meals, allows maintenance of hydration status and total body water at normal levels."
- "No specific Tolerable Upper Intake Levels (ULs) were set for water, potassium, or sulfate as healthy persons can adapt to higher intakes from foods and beverages."

**Teaching trap.** The 3.7 L and 2.7 L figures are *total water including food*, set at the population *median*, and explicitly "should not be interpreted as a specific requirement". Lessons and the media often quote them as *drink 3.7 litres*. Note also the NASEM text sets no specific UL, which is not a statement that very large intakes are safe (water intoxication and hyponatraemia exist; I did not fetch a source on them).

#### 8.3 EFSA 2010 water reference values

**Citation.** EFSA NDA Panel. Scientific Opinion on Dietary Reference Values for water. *EFSA Journal*. 2010;8(3):1459. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2010.1459 (metadata via Crossref).

**What I read.** The PDF URL returned an HTML page, not the opinion, so the opinion was not read. EFSA press release *EFSA sets European dietary reference values for nutrient intakes*, https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/nda100326 (26 March 2010), fetched by curl. Verbatim: "For water a daily intake of 2.0 litres is considered adequate for women and 2.5 litres for men."

**Not verified verbatim.** A search-result summary said EFSA's values cover water from drinking water, all beverages and food moisture and apply only to moderate temperature and moderate activity (**search-summary level only**). A writer must read the opinion before stating the scope.

**Teaching note.** EFSA (2.0 and 2.5 L total water) and NASEM (2.7 and 3.7 L total water) differ because they were derived from different populations' median intakes, not because physiology differs across the Atlantic. Neither is *8 glasses*, and both include water from food.

#### 8.4 DGA 2025-2030 on hydration

Verbatim: "Hydration is a key factor in overall health. Choose water (still or sparkling) and unsweetened beverages." No volume is given.

---

### Things that surprised me or contradict common belief

1. **The *no safe level* phrase is not in the GBD 2018 abstract.** The abstract says the level that "minimises health loss is zero". The literal phrase comes from WHO Europe (2023). And GBD's own 2022 update found a J-shaped curve for people 40 and over in every region.
2. **The two US federal alcohol reviews reached different conclusions largely because of what they excluded.** NASEM required never-drinker comparison groups and excluded Mendelian randomisation; ICCPUD modelled observational data but framed its introduction around MR finding no protection. Neither is a randomised trial, and the one trial attempted (MACH15) was stopped over industry funding.
3. **The ICCPUD report was never finalised.** Only the January 2025 draft exists. The DGA 2025-2030 (January 2026) dropped numeric drink limits and does not mention cancer, while the Surgeon General's January 2025 advisory called for cancer warning labels and lower limits.
4. **Zhao 2023's low-volume point estimate is 0.93 (P = .07).** *Not significantly associated* is accurate; *no benefit* goes further than the data.
5. **In VITAL and in Wood 2018, heart attack is the outlier.** Omega-3 reduced MI in VITAL's secondary analysis; in Wood, alcohol raised almost every cardiovascular outcome except MI. Much of the pro-J-curve case rests on the MI signal.
6. **The NASEM water AI explicitly "should not be interpreted as a specific requirement"** and includes water from food.
7. **Beat's helpline is open 3pm to 8pm weekdays, not 24 hours**, and NEDA no longer runs a helpline. ANAD is peer support, not clinical.
8. **The NHS vitamin D page is past its review date** (reviewed 2020, due 2023). SACN's page was updated in December 2023.
9. **"Nutritionist" is not a protected title in the UK**, and in the US regulation varies by state. Since January 2024 a new US registered dietitian needs a graduate degree.
10. **The FDA supplement Q&A page was updated 27 August 2026**; a writer should re-read it before publication in case the wording has shifted.

### Gaps a later researcher should fill

- Read the EFSA caffeine and water opinions themselves (Wiley blocks curl; try a browser).
- Read the NASEM chapters on cancer and cardiovascular disease for confidence intervals, and the ICCPUD critiques (House Oversight report, International Scientific Forum on Alcohol Research critique no. 288) at first hand, noting who funds the Forum.
- Fetch official pages for diet in chronic kidney disease and diabetes (NHS, NIDDK, Diabetes UK, Kidney Care UK) to support "see a dietitian" advice.
- Check for a newer Cochrane vitamin D mortality review, and for any 2024-2026 review on caffeine in pregnancy.
- Fetch the UK/EU food supplement regulatory position (FSA, Directive 2002/46/EC) if the course covers regulation outside the US.
- Confirm the Canadian standard drink size and the US state licensure count from primary pages.

### Verification note

After writing, a script extracted every double-quoted string of 25 characters or more from this file and checked that it appears (after whitespace normalisation) in the fetched source texts saved in the working folder `research/F/` (abstracts, page texts, PDF texts). The result of that check is recorded in the line below.

Result: 335 quoted strings checked. All were found in the fetched texts except: (a) four quotations from the NASEM Highlights PDF, which is laid out in two columns so the plain-text conversion interleaves lines; I checked each against the column-preserving (`pdftotext -layout`) version and they are verbatim; (b) two Surgeon General quotations and one NASEM water quotation where I removed superscript reference numbers or footnote markers, as stated beside each; the words match the source; (c) about eight false positives where the script paired the closing quote of one quotation with the opening quote of the next (citation and URL lines). No quotation was found to differ from its source.
